Category A (medium first-year ice), Category B (thin first-year ice), and Category C (open waters or ice conditions less severe than Categories A and B).

--- QUESTION 0571 ---
What mandatory specialized documentation and certification must be carried on board a vessel to comply with SOLAS Chapter XIV and the Polar Code?
--- ANSWER ---
A Polar Ship Certificate and a ship-specific Polar Water Operational Manual (PWOM).

--- QUESTION 0572 ---
According to the Polar Code training standards, what level of training is required for the Master and Chief Mate operating a passenger ship in "Open Waters" with less than 1/10 ice coverage?
--- ANSWER ---
Basic training

--- QUESTION 0573 ---
What specific training must the Master and Chief Mate hold if operating any vessel type in "Other Waters" (more than 1/10 ice coverage) within the Polar regions?
--- ANSWER ---
Advanced training

--- QUESTION 0574 ---
Name at least four physical or environmental hazards uniquely associated with navigation in high-latitude Polar regions.
--- ANSWER ---
Any four of the following: ice (hull/machinery damage), topside icing (accretion), low temperatures affecting human performance, extended periods of darkness or light, high latitude compass limitations, remoteness/lack of hydrographic data, and limited search and rescue (SAR) facilities.

--- QUESTION 0575 ---
At what approximate latitude threshold do gyro compasses typically become unreliable, and by what latitude do they lose virtually all directive force and become unusable?
--- ANSWER ---
They become generally unreliable above 75 degrees latitude and completely unusable from 85 degrees latitude.

--- QUESTION 0576 ---
Why are standard magnetic compasses of minimal value when navigating very close to the Earth's geographic poles?
--- ANSWER ---
Because they experience extremely large diurnal changes in variation and suffer a total loss of the horizontal (H) directional component of the Earth's magnetic field.

--- QUESTION 0577 ---
Why does GNSS (including GPS) positional accuracy decrease when navigating within high latitudes?
--- ANSWER ---
Due to the low altitude of satellite orbits relative to the horizon at extreme latitudes.

--- QUESTION 0578 ---
When a vessel is navigating an ice area in high latitudes and it is impossible to distinguish between the shoreline and the ice, what primary method should be used to plot the vessel's position?
--- ANSWER ---
Dead reckoning

--- QUESTION 0579 ---
Name at least five pre-entry tank, deck, or structural engineering precautions that should be executed prior to entering an area where freezing ice conditions are expected.
--- ANSWER ---
Any five of the following:

1. Empty or slack off wing/double bottom water ballast and fresh water tanks.
2. Ensure draft, trim, and stability account for icing allowances.
3. Test hull heating and air bubble systems.
4. Add cold-temperature additives to fuel bunkers.
5. Align rudder and indicators.
6. Verify radar scanner heating functions.
7. Confirm all searchlights and NUC lights are functional.
8. Verify bridge and superstructure heating works.
9. Protect mooring gear and deck machinery with low-temperature grease.
10. Drain external fire-mains and deck lines.
11. Line up lower sea suctions.

--- QUESTION 0580 ---
What specific bridge tools or features are required by the Polar Code to maintain forward visibility through windows during extreme cold weather spray?
--- ANSWER ---
Means to clear melted ice, freezing rain, snow, mist, spray, and condensation.

--- QUESTION 0581 ---
What type of survival equipment must be provided for every person on board a passenger ship navigating in polar waters to prevent hypothermia?
--- ANSWER ---
An immersion suit or a thermal protective aid.

--- QUESTION 0582 ---
What type of lifeboats are strictly mandated for use on all vessels operating under the jurisdiction of the Polar Code?
--- ANSWER ---
Partially or totally enclosed lifeboats.

--- QUESTION 0583 ---
What does a True Vector on a radar display represent?
--- ANSWER ---
The true motion of a target, showing its course and speed with reference to the ground.

--- QUESTION 0584 ---
How can an indication of a close quarters situation be determined using True Vectors?
--- ANSWER ---
By running out the true vectors to show the predicted time of the encounter.

--- QUESTION 0585 ---
What does a Relative Vector represent on a radar display?
--- ANSWER ---
The predicted movement of a target relative to own ship.

--- QUESTION 0586 ---
How can the Closest Point of Approach (CPA) and Time to Closest Point of Approach (TCPA) be determined using the relative vector mode?
--- ANSWER ---
By extending the vectors using the vector length control; the CPA is determined visually and the TCPA is read off from the vector length control.

--- QUESTION 0587 ---
In True Motion with Relative Vectors selected, what do the target's relative vector and true trail indicate respectively?
--- ANSWER ---
The relative vector indicates the CPA of the target, and the true trail indicates the target vessel's true course.

--- QUESTION 0588 ---
What action is necessary from time to time as own ship tracks across the screen in True Motion?
--- ANSWER ---
It will be necessary to reset own ship back to the origin.

--- QUESTION 0589 ---
In Relative Motion with True Vectors selected, what does the true vector of own ship represent?
--- ANSWER ---
The heading and speed of own ship.

--- QUESTION 0590 ---
If own ship is in Relative Motion with True Vectors and reduces its speed, what displays the resulting change to the target's CPA?
--- ANSWER ---
The relative trail indicates the target vessel's CPA as a result of own ship reducing speed.

--- QUESTION 0591 ---
According to radar plotting basics, if a tracked target is discovered to be a stationary rock, what does its apparent movement or vector represent?
--- ANSWER ---
The current (set and drift).

--- QUESTION 0592 ---
Define the term aspect in radar plotting.
--- ANSWER ---
Aspect is the angle presented to us by a target vessel, measured as the angle between the target's head and her reciprocal bearing at an observation.

--- QUESTION 0593 ---
How is a target's reciprocal bearing calculated when determining aspect?
--- ANSWER ---
By adding or subtracting 180 degrees from the target's bearing (Bearing +/- 180 degrees).

--- QUESTION 0594 ---
If a target's reciprocal bearing is 115 degrees and her course is 011 degrees, what is the numerical value of the aspect?
--- ANSWER ---
104 degrees.

--- QUESTION 0595 ---
How is the side designation (Green or Red) determined for a target vessel's aspect?
--- ANSWER ---
It is designated Green if the starboard side of the target is visible, and Red if the port side is visible.

--- QUESTION 0596 ---
What data inputs are used to provide heading and speed for Ground Stabilisation?
--- ANSWER ---
Speed and Heading from GNSS (Global Navigation Satellite System).

--- QUESTION 0597 ---
What data inputs are used to provide heading and speed for Sea Stabilisation?
--- ANSWER ---
Speed from a Log (Speed through the water) and Heading from a Gyro or another Transmitting Heading Device (THD).

--- QUESTION 0598 ---
According to MGN 379, which stabilization mode is best suited for coastal navigation?
--- ANSWER ---
Ground Stabilisation.

--- QUESTION 0599 ---
According to MGN 379, which stabilization mode is best suited for anti-collision?
--- ANSWER ---
Sea Stabilisation.

--- QUESTION 0600 ---
Why can Ground Stabilisation lead to a dangerous situation or change in apparent aspect during an anti-collision scenario?
--- ANSWER ---
If there is a strong current, Ground Stabilisation can visually distort the aspect, potentially making a crossing situation look like an overtaking situation.

--- QUESTION 0601 ---
How does a fixed, stationary target appear on a Ground Stabilised radar display versus a Sea Stabilised radar display?
--- ANSWER ---
On a Ground Stabilised display, the fixed target appears stationary. On a Sea Stabilised display, it will have a vector direction reciprocal to the set, and a speed indicating the rate of drift.

--- QUESTION 0602 ---
According to Rule 19(a), under what visual condition does Rule 19 apply to vessels navigating in or near an area of restricted visibility?
--- ANSWER ---
It applies to vessels not in sight of one another.

--- QUESTION 0603 ---
According to Rule 19(b), what specific readiness status must a power-driven vessel maintain regarding her propulsion while navigating in restricted visibility?
--- ANSWER ---
She shall have her engines ready for immediate manoeuvre.

--- QUESTION 0604 ---
Which specific COLREG Rule deals in detail with the factors used to determine a safe speed?
--- ANSWER ---
Rule 6.

--- QUESTION 0605 ---
Besides Rule 19, which specific range of rules from Section I of Part B also applies when navigating in or near areas of restricted visibility?
--- ANSWER ---
Rules 4 to 10 inclusive.

--- QUESTION 0606 ---
Under Rule 19(d), if a vessel detects the presence of another vessel by radar alone, what two things must she determine?
--- ANSWER ---
She must determine if a close-quarters situation is developing and/or if a risk of collision exists.

--- QUESTION 0607 ---
According to Rule 19(d)(i), what specific course alteration should be avoided, so far as possible, for a vessel forward of the beam unless that vessel is being overtaken?
--- ANSWER ---
An alteration of course to port.

--- QUESTION 0608 ---
According to Rule 19(d)(ii), what type of course alteration should be avoided for a vessel that is located abeam or abaft the beam?
--- ANSWER ---
An alteration of course towards that vessel.

--- QUESTION 0609 ---
Why must avoiding action be taken in ample time if a risk of collision or close-quarters situation develops under Rule 19?
--- ANSWER ---
Because there are no "stand-on" vessels under Rule 19.

--- QUESTION 0610 ---
According to Rule 19(e), what action must a vessel take if she hears a fog signal apparently forward of her beam, unless it has been determined that a risk of collision does not exist?
--- ANSWER ---
She shall reduce her speed to the minimum at which she can be kept on her course, take all her way off if necessary, and navigate with extreme caution until the danger of collision is over.

--- QUESTION 0611 ---
According to Rule 35(a), what sound signal must a power-driven vessel make when making way through the water in or near an area of restricted visibility, and at what maximum interval?
--- ANSWER ---
One prolonged blast at intervals of not more than 2 minutes.

--- QUESTION 0612 ---
According to Rule 35(b), what sound signal must a power-driven vessel underway but stopped and making no way sound in restricted visibility, and what is the interval between the blasts?
--- ANSWER ---
Two prolonged blasts in succession with an interval of about 2 seconds between them, sounded at intervals of not more than 2 minutes.

--- QUESTION 0613 ---
Which group of vessels must sound three blasts in succession—specifically one prolonged followed by two short blasts—at intervals of not more than 2 minutes in restricted visibility?
--- ANSWER ---
Vessels not under command, vessels restricted in their ability to manoeuvre, vessels constrained by their draught, sailing vessels, vessels engaged in fishing, and vessels engaged in towing or pushing another vessel.

--- QUESTION 0614 ---
What sound signal must a vessel engaged in fishing or a vessel restricted in her ability to manoeuvre when carrying out her work do while at anchor in restricted visibility?
--- ANSWER ---
One prolonged blast followed by two short blasts (the signal prescribed in Rule 35(c)), instead of the standard anchor bell/gong signals.

--- QUESTION 0615 ---
If manned, what sound signal must a vessel being towed (or the last vessel of the tow if more than one is towed) make in restricted visibility?
--- ANSWER ---
Four blasts in succession, namely one prolonged followed by three short blasts, made immediately after the signal made by the towing vessel when practicable.

--- QUESTION 0616 ---
How are a pushing vessel and a vessel being pushed ahead regulated under Rule 35(f) if they are rigidly connected in a composite unit?
--- ANSWER ---
They shall be regarded as a power-driven vessel and give the standard power-driven vessel signals (one prolonged blast if making way, or two prolonged blasts if underway but stopped).

--- QUESTION 0617 ---
According to Rule 35(g), what is the primary sound signal required for a vessel at anchor to make at intervals of not more than one minute?
--- ANSWER ---
She shall ring the bell rapidly for about 5 seconds.

--- QUESTION 0618 ---
For a vessel at anchor that is 100 meters or more in length, where must the bell be sounded, and what additional instrument must be sounded in the after part?
--- ANSWER ---
The bell must be sounded in the forepart of the vessel, and a gong must be sounded rapidly for about 5 seconds in the after part immediately after the bell.

--- QUESTION 0619 ---
What optional whistle signal may an anchored vessel sound in restricted visibility to give warning of her position and the possibility of collision to an approaching vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
Three blasts in succession: one short, one prolonged, and one short blast.

--- QUESTION 0620 ---
What additional bell signal must a vessel aground give immediately before and after the rapid ringing of the bell?
--- ANSWER ---
Three separate and distinct strokes on the bell.

--- QUESTION 0621 ---
Vessels of what minimum length are permitted to forgo the mandatory bell or gong signals at anchor or aground, provided they make an alternative efficient sound signal at intervals of not more than 2 minutes?
--- ANSWER ---
Vessels of less than 20 meters in length (the rule explicitly exempts vessels of 12 meters or more but less than 20 meters from the strict bell signals, and also applies alternative intervals to vessels less than 12 meters).

--- QUESTION 0622 ---
What optional identity signal may a pilot vessel sound in restricted visibility when engaged on pilotage duty?
--- ANSWER ---
An identity signal consisting of four short blasts.

--- QUESTION 0623 ---
What is the definition of a solid bulk cargo as per IMSBC Code Regulation 1-1?
--- ANSWER ---
Any cargo other than liquid or gas, consisting of a combination of particles, granules or any larger pieces of material generally uniform in composition which is loaded directly into cargo spaces of a ship without any intermediate form of containment.

--- QUESTION 0624 ---
Which code regulates the safe carriage of solid bulk cargoes?
--- ANSWER ---
The IMSBC Code (International Maritime Solid Bulk Cargoes Code).

--- QUESTION 0625 ---
What does the acronym TML stand for in solid bulk cargo operations?
--- ANSWER ---
Transportable Moisture Limit.

--- QUESTION 0626 ---
What does the acronym FMP stand for when assessing bulk cargo properties?
--- ANSWER ---
Flow Moisture Point.

--- QUESTION 0627 ---
What is the mathematical formula used to calculate the Transportable Moisture Limit (TML) from the Flow Moisture Point (FMP)?
--- ANSWER ---
TML = 90% x FMP

--- QUESTION 0628 ---
Under what condition regarding moisture content can a Group A solid bulk cargo be safely accepted for loading?
--- ANSWER ---
Only if the actual moisture content of the cargo is less than its Transportable Moisture Limit (TML).

--- QUESTION 0629 ---
What primary hazard is associated with Group A solid bulk cargoes in the IMSBC Code?
--- ANSWER ---
They are cargoes that may liquefy if shipped at a moisture content exceeding their TML.

--- QUESTION 0630 ---
What action must a master take if visual monitoring reveals indications of high moisture content during the loading of a bulk cargo?
--- ANSWER ---
Stop loading immediately and seek further advice.

--- QUESTION 0631 ---
Why might a solid bulk cargo develop a dangerous wet base even if its average moisture content is less than the TML?
--- ANSWER ---
Because some cargoes are highly susceptible to moisture migration.

--- QUESTION 0632 ---
Which simple complementary test procedure can be conducted onboard a vessel to check for the possibility of liquefaction in a bulk cargo?
--- ANSWER ---
The "Can test" (as per IMSBC Section 8.4.2).

--- QUESTION 0633 ---
Does a dry result from an onboard "can test" definitively prove a cargo's moisture content is below the Transportable Moisture Limit (TML)?
--- ANSWER ---
No, if samples remain dry following a can test, the moisture content of the material may still exceed the TML.

--- QUESTION 0634 ---
What tragic consequence occurred in November 2010 after the master of the M/V Nasco Diamond was persuaded to load a cargo of nickel ore despite initially refusing?
--- ANSWER ---
The cargo liquefied and the vessel sank, resulting in the deaths of 22 crew members.

--- QUESTION 0635 ---
What dangerous phenomenon occurs when ship vibrations and motion cause moisture to migrate upwards, creating a liquid slurry on top of a compacted solid base?
--- ANSWER ---
Dynamic separation.

--- QUESTION 0636 ---
How does dynamic separation differ from total liquefaction in a solid bulk cargo hold?
--- ANSWER ---
Liquefaction turns the entire cargo mass into a fluid, whereas dynamic separation involves the separation of a fluid layer on top of a compacted solid layer.

--- QUESTION 0637 ---
What specific stability risk does the surface liquid slurry created by dynamic separation impose on a vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
It creates a "free surface effect" in the hold, reducing the vessel's metacentric height (GM) and causing a dangerous list.

--- QUESTION 0638 ---
What does the acronym BCSN stand for on a cargo information form?
--- ANSWER ---
Bulk Cargo Shipping Name.

--- QUESTION 0639 ---
According to the IMSBC Code, within what maximum timeframe prior to loading must the shipper ensure a TML test is conducted?
--- ANSWER ---
Within 6 months of the date of loading.

--- QUESTION 0640 ---
Within how many days prior to the commencement of loading must a bulk cargo's moisture content test take place?
--- ANSWER ---
Never more than 7 days before loading.

--- QUESTION 0641 ---
If significant or heavy rain occurs within 7 days of a moisture content test, what is the shipper's responsibility?
--- ANSWER ---
The shipper is responsible for ensuring the moisture content is still less than its TML and providing evidence of this to the master as soon as practicable.

--- QUESTION 0642 ---
Name three key operational elements that must be agreed upon with a terminal in a Loading Plan prior to loading.
--- ANSWER ---
Any three of the following: the sequence of loading, the quantity and rate of loading/unloading, the number of pours, deballasting/ballasting capability of the ship, and the permissible forces and moments on the ship.

--- QUESTION 0643 ---
Under what condition can a ship without a Document of Authorization load bulk grain under the optional stability requirements?
--- ANSWER ---
The total weight of the bulk grain must not exceed one third of the deadweight of the ship (and specific structural/stability criteria must be met).

--- QUESTION 0644 ---
What is the minimum required metacentric height (GM) throughout a voyage for a ship carrying bulk grain without a Document of Authorization?
--- ANSWER ---
0.3 meters (after correction for the free surface effects of liquids in tanks) or the value given by the specific grain stability formula, whichever is greater.

--- QUESTION 0645 ---
What immediate action must a Master take if a category B dangerous solid bulk cargo is accidentally dropped overboard or swept into the sea?
--- ANSWER ---
Report the particulars of the incident without delay and to the fullest extent possible to the nearest coastal State.

--- QUESTION 0646 ---
Why does a "gas free certificate" not guarantee total safety before entering a cargo hold that contains or contained fumigated cargo?
--- ANSWER ---
Lethal doses of fumigant may remain trapped in pockets or within the cargo itself.

--- QUESTION 0647 ---
According to SOLAS Chapter XII, what is a primary structural hazard associated with loading a high-density cargo if a hold becomes breached?
--- ANSWER ---
Because high-density cargo leaves large empty volumes in the hold, a greater weight of water could enter the space, potentially resulting in loss of hull/bulkhead failure or loss of stability.

--- QUESTION 0648 ---
How does MGN 668 define a high-density cargo?
--- ANSWER ---
Cargoes with a density of over 1,000 kg/m³.

--- QUESTION 0649 ---
How is the deadweight of a ship defined under the SOLAS Convention?
--- ANSWER ---
The difference in tonnes between the displacement of a ship in water of a specific gravity of 1.025 at the load waterline corresponding to the assigned summer freeboard and the lightweight of the ship.

--- QUESTION 0650 ---
What does the "Lightship Condition" or lightweight of a vessel exclude?
--- ANSWER ---
It excludes consumables, cargo, crew, and effects, and contains no liquids except operating fluids (lubricants/hydraulics).

--- QUESTION 0651 ---
What formula is used to determine cargo quantity in tonnes using hold capacity and stowage factor?
--- ANSWER ---
Cargo quantity in tonnes = Hold capacity / Stowage Factor.

--- QUESTION 0652 ---
How is the maximum permissible weight for a tanktop calculated using its dimensions and permissible weight rating?
--- ANSWER ---
Length x Breadth x Permissible Weight (PW per square meter).

--- QUESTION 0653 ---
What type of condensation forms on the surface of cold cargo when warm, moist outside air comes into contact with it?
--- ANSWER ---
Cargo sweat.

--- QUESTION 0654 ---
Under what voyage condition does cargo sweat typically occur?
--- ANSWER ---
When a vessel loads cargo in a cold area and moves toward a warmer region.

--- QUESTION 0655 ---
What type of condensation forms when warm, moist air inside a cargo hold comes into contact with the cold steel structure of the ship?
--- ANSWER ---
Ship sweat.

--- QUESTION 0656 ---
Under what voyage condition does ship sweat typically occur?
--- ANSWER ---
When a vessel loads cargo in a warm area and moves toward a colder region.

--- QUESTION 0657 ---
What are the two broad rules of thumb for cargo hold ventilation?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Cold to Hot -> Ventilate Not.
2. Hot to Cold -> Ventilate Bold.

--- QUESTION 0658 ---
According to the Dew Point Rule, when should a cargo hold be ventilated?
--- ANSWER ---
When the dew point of the outside air is lower than the dew point of the air inside the cargo space.

--- QUESTION 0659 ---
According to the Three Degree Rule, when should a cargo hold be ventilated?
--- ANSWER ---
When the dry bulb temperature of the outside air is at least 3°C lower than the temperature of the cargo.

--- QUESTION 0660 ---
Why is a vessel's hull girder permitted to carry higher levels of static stress (shear forces and bending moments) while in harbour compared to at sea?
--- ANSWER ---
Because in harbour the ship is in sheltered water and is subjected to reduced dynamic loads.

--- QUESTION 0661 ---
Which shipboard manual outlines the maximum allowable load per unit surface area of the tank top plating?
--- ANSWER ---
The Loading Booklet (or Loading Manual).

--- QUESTION 0662 ---
Which specific statutory document verifies that a ship is structurally sound and equipped for cargo operations by encompassing construction, equipment, and radio safety?
--- ANSWER ---
Cargo Ship Safety Certificates (or a combined Safety Certificate).

--- QUESTION 0663 ---
What specific authorization certificate must be carried by a bulk carrier to load grain in bulk?
--- ANSWER ---
A Document of Authorization (Grain Certificate).

--- QUESTION 0664 ---
What document is required to verify a vessel's suitability for carrying dangerous goods in solid bulk form?
--- ANSWER ---
A Document of Compliance for Dangerous Goods.

--- QUESTION 0665 ---
According to the IMSBC Code, what is the definition of a solid bulk cargo?
--- ANSWER ---
Any cargo other than liquid or gas, consisting of a combination of particles, granules, or any larger pieces of material generally uniform in composition, which is loaded directly into the cargo spaces of a ship without any intermediate form of containment.

--- QUESTION 0666 ---
What are the three core cargo Groups designated under the IMSBC Code classification system?
--- ANSWER ---
Group A (cargoes which may liquefy), Group B (cargoes which possess a chemical hazard), and Group C (cargoes which are neither liable to liquefy nor possess chemical hazards).

--- QUESTION 0667 ---
What specific danger is associated with Group A bulk cargoes under the IMSBC Code?
--- ANSWER ---
They are susceptible to liquefaction or moisture migration, which can lead to cargo shift and a dangerous loss of vessel stability if their moisture content exceeds the Transportable Moisture Limit (TML).

--- QUESTION 0668 ---
How is the Transportable Moisture Limit (TML) of a bulk cargo calculated relative to its Flow Moisture Point (FMP)?
--- ANSWER ---
The TML is established as 90% of the cargo's Flow Moisture Point (FMP).

--- QUESTION 0669 ---
What is the maximum allowed actual moisture content for a Group A solid bulk cargo to be safely accepted for loading?
--- ANSWER ---
The actual moisture content must be strictly less than the cargo's Transportable Moisture Limit (TML).

--- QUESTION 0670 ---
Under IMSBC Section 8.4.2, what complementary shipboard test procedure can the master use to visually check for the possibility of liquefaction?
--- ANSWER ---
The "Can test," which involves packing a sample into a can and checking if moisture or fluid behaviors emerge after impacts.

--- QUESTION 0671 ---
Why is a dry appearance on the surface of a Group A bulk cargo insufficient proof of safety during a voyage?
--- ANSWER ---
Cargoes can undergo moisture migration, developing a dangerous wet base at the bottom of the hold while the surface remains deceptively dry.

--- QUESTION 0672 ---
According to the IMSBC Code regulations, within what timeframe must the shipper ensure the Transportable Moisture Limit (TML) test is conducted prior to loading?
--- ANSWER ---
Within 6 months of the date of loading.

--- QUESTION 0673 ---
How close to the commencement of loading must a bulk cargo's actual moisture content test be carried out?
--- ANSWER ---
As close as possible to the commencement of loading, and never more than 7 days before.

--- QUESTION 0674 ---
If significant or heavy rain occurs within the 7-day testing window before loading a Group A cargo, what is the shipper's responsibility?
--- ANSWER ---
The shipper must ensure that the moisture content is still less than the TML and provide verified evidence to the master as soon as practicable.

--- QUESTION 0675 ---
What two primary signed certificates must the master obtain from the shipper before loading a bulk cargo prone to liquefaction?
--- ANSWER ---
A signed certificate of the Transportable Moisture Limit (TML) and a signed certificate or declaration of moisture content, both issued by a competent authority.

--- QUESTION 0676 ---
According to SOLAS, how is the deadweight of a ship precisely defined?
--- ANSWER ---
The difference in tonnes between the displacement of a ship in water of a specific gravity of 1.025 at the load waterline corresponding to the assigned summer freeboard and the lightweight of the ship.

--- QUESTION 0677 ---
What components are explicitly excluded from a ship's lightweight (lightship) condition?
--- ANSWER ---
Cargo, consumables (fuel, diesel, lubricating oil, fresh water), and crew and their effects.

--- QUESTION 0678 ---
What threshold of bulk density is used in MGN 668 to identify a "high density cargo"?
--- ANSWER ---
Cargoes with a bulk density of over 1,780 kg/m³.

--- QUESTION 0679 ---
What inherent structural risk is associated with loading a high-density cargo like iron ore under SOLAS XII regulations?
--- ANSWER ---
Because the cargo occupies small volumes, large empty spaces are left in the holds; if a hold is breached, an excessive weight of water can enter, potentially causing hull or bulkhead failure or catastrophic loss of stability.

--- QUESTION 0680 ---
How is the total allowable cargo quantity in tonnes determined for a single hold using its volume capacity and the cargo's Stowage Factor (SF)?
--- ANSWER ---
Cargo Quantity = Hold Capacity (in cubic meters or feet) divided by the Stowage Factor (m³/t or ft³/t).

--- QUESTION 0681 ---
Where can a deck officer find the authoritative Tanktop Maximum Permissible Weight parameters for a vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
In the vessel's Loading Manual, Ship Capacity Plans, and occasionally within the general Ship Particulars.

--- QUESTION 0682 ---
Define the phenomenon of "cargo sweat" inside a ship's hold.
--- ANSWER ---
Condensation that forms directly on the surface of a cold cargo when warm, moist outside air is admitted and comes into contact with it.

--- QUESTION 0683 ---
Under what specific voyage profile is cargo sweat most likely to manifest?
--- ANSWER ---
When a vessel loads cargo in a cold region and subsequently sails toward a warmer geographic region.

--- QUESTION 0684 ---
Define the phenomenon of "ship sweat" inside a cargo compartment.
--- ANSWER ---
Condensation that occurs when the warm, moist air trapped inside a cargo hold comes into contact with the cold steel structure of the ship.

--- QUESTION 0685 ---
Under what specific voyage profile is ship sweat most likely to occur?
--- ANSWER ---
When a vessel loads cargo in a warm, humid region and subsequently voyages toward a colder geographic region.

--- QUESTION 0686 ---
State the two broad traditional rules of thumb regarding ventilation based on transit temperature changes.
--- ANSWER ---
"Cold to Hot, Ventilate Not" and "Hot to Cold, Ventilate Bold."

--- QUESTION 0687 ---
According to the Dew Point Rule, when should a cargo hold be ventilated?
--- ANSWER ---
When the dew point of the outside ambient air is lower than the dew point of the air trapped inside the cargo space.

--- QUESTION 0688 ---
State the operational criteria dictated by the "Three Degree Rule" for bulk cargo ventilation.
--- ANSWER ---
A cargo hold should be ventilated when the dry bulb temperature of the outside ambient air is at least 3°C lower than the actual temperature of the loaded cargo.

--- QUESTION 0689 ---
Name at least three typical parameters that must be mutually agreed upon and included in a Terminal Loading Plan prior to loading solid bulk cargo.
--- ANSWER ---
The sequence of loading, the quantity and rate of loading/unloading, the number of pours, deballasting/ballasting capabilities, and permissible forces and moments.

--- QUESTION 0690 ---
Under IMSBC Regulation 7-4, what mandatory action must a Master take if dangerous goods in solid bulk form are accidentally dropped or lost overboard into the sea?
--- ANSWER ---
The master must report the particulars of the incident without delay and to the fullest extent possible to the nearest coastal State.

--- QUESTION 0691 ---
Why does a "gas-free certificate" fail to guarantee absolute safety before entering a previously fumigated cargo hold?
--- ANSWER ---
Lethal pockets of fumigant gas can remain trapped inside pockets or deep within the physical bulk structure of the cargo matrix, which may desorb later.

--- QUESTION 0692 ---
What safety precautions must be executed concerning access points to a cargo hold undergoing active fumigation?
--- ANSWER ---
All access spaces must be strictly padlocked, sealed, and labeled to prevent unauthorized or accidental entry, and entry must be forbidden until the space is thoroughly ventilated and cleared by experts.

--- QUESTION 0693 ---
List three vital operational datasets that must be included in a vessel's standard "Loading Booklet" according to IMSBC regulations.
--- ANSWER ---
Stability data/hydrostatic data, ballasting/deballasting rates and capacities, allowable shear forces and bending moments, tank capacities, and maximum allowable unit load on the tanktop plating.

--- QUESTION 0694 ---
Why are hull girders permitted to carry a higher level of static stress from shear forces and bending moments while in a harbor compared to at sea?
--- ANSWER ---
In harbor, the ship is in sheltered water and is subjected to significantly reduced dynamic wave-induced loads compared to the open sea.

--- QUESTION 0695 ---
Which three notorious bulk cargo types are explicitly highlighted as high-risk materials prone to sudden liquefaction under bad moisture management?
--- ANSWER ---
Iron ore fines, Nickel ore, and Bauxite.

--- QUESTION 0696 ---
What are the three standard types of Charter Parties?
--- ANSWER ---
The text notes that there are three standard types of Charter Parties, though it does not explicitly list their names in the provided pages.

--- QUESTION 0697 ---
What is meant by the charter party term Laytime?
--- ANSWER ---
Laytime is the amount of time allowed in a voyage charter for the loading and unloading of cargo.

--- QUESTION 0698 ---
What does the term Laydays refer to in a charter party?
--- ANSWER ---
Laydays refers to a period of specified days during which the shipowner must present the vessel to the charterer at the agreed place for loading.

--- QUESTION 0699 ---
What is Laycan?
--- ANSWER ---
Laycan (Laydays Cancelling) is the period during which the shipowner must tender a Notice of Readiness (NOR) to the charterer stating that the ship has arrived at the port of loading and is ready to load.

--- QUESTION 0700 ---
What is Demurrage?
--- ANSWER ---
Demurrage is an amount of money paid to the shipowner by the charterer for failing to complete loading or discharging within the allowed time specified in the Charter Party.

--- QUESTION 0701 ---
What is Despatch and how is its value typically calculated relative to demurrage?
--- ANSWER ---
Despatch is an amount of money payable by the shipowner to the charterer for completing loading and/or discharging in less than the allowed time. It is usually half the rate of the demurrage amount.

--- QUESTION 0702 ---
What is a Bill of Lading?
--- ANSWER ---
A Bill of Lading is a legal document issued by a carrier to a shipper that details the type, quantity, and destination of the goods being carried.

--- QUESTION 0703 ---
What are the three primary functions of a Bill of Lading?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Evidence of the Contract of Carriage between the Shipper and the Carrier.
2. A document of title (entitling the holder to delivery of the goods at destination).
3. A receipt for the goods loaded on board the vessel.

--- QUESTION 0704 ---
What is a Sea Waybill and how does its functions differ from a Bill of Lading?
--- ANSWER ---
Like a Bill of Lading, a Sea Waybill functions as a receipt for goods and evidence of the contract of carriage. However, unlike a Bill of Lading, it is not a Document of Title, and the carrier delivers the cargo to the named person regardless of whether they can produce the physical document at the discharge port.

--- QUESTION 0705 ---
What is a Mate's Receipt?
--- ANSWER ---
A Mate's Receipt is a document issued to the Shipper that serves as evidence in its own right of when and how the goods were received onboard.

--- QUESTION 0706 ---
How is a Mate's Receipt used in relation to a Bill of Lading?
--- ANSWER ---
It is issued to the Shipper to prepare the Bill of Lading and is referred to when checking the description accuracy of the cargo before the Bill of Lading is signed. It is eventually returned to the ship in exchange for the signed Bill of Lading.

--- QUESTION 0707 ---
Why is accurately describing the condition of cargo on a Bill of Lading critically important for a Master?
--- ANSWER ---
An accurate description protects the vessel from risks. Inaccurate descriptions can result in exposure to claims, loss of the right to limit liability, loss of P&I cover, loss of the right of indemnity from the charterer, or criminal prosecution.

--- QUESTION 0708 ---
Is a Master obligated to sign a Bill of Lading that contains an inaccurate cargo description?
--- ANSWER ---
No, the Master is under no obligation to sign bills of lading that inaccurately describe the cargo.

--- QUESTION 0709 ---
Name at least five specific items or details a Master should check before signing a Bill of Lading.
--- ANSWER ---
The Master should check:

1. It is presented on the correct form required by the Charter Party.
2. The required contractual terms appear in it.
3. The vessel's name is correct.
4. The place of discharge is safely reachable.
5. The correct date and place of shipment.
6. An accurate description of the goods as shipped.
7. Correct names of the Shipper and Consignee.
8. Copies are marked/stamped as 'Non-Negotiable copy'.
9. Cargo instructions match the Mate's receipt and voyage orders.

--- QUESTION 0710 ---
What immediate action should be taken if cargo damage is found prior to loading?
--- ANSWER ---
Take steps to prevent the damaged cargo from being loaded, inform the Shipper, and clause the bill of lading unless replacement goods are provided or the damage is made good.

--- QUESTION 0711 ---
Can a Master formally reject damaged cargo without shore-side management intervention?
--- ANSWER ---
Rejection should not formally be given without the Company's approval. However, if the charter party explicitly requires clean bills of lading, any cargo not in apparent good order and condition must be rejected.

--- QUESTION 0712 ---
What action should be taken if a shipper delivers cargo with broken container seals?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master should seek a replacement from the Shipper. If the Shipper refuses, the Master must protest in writing.

--- QUESTION 0713 ---
What does "clausing" a Bill of Lading mean?
--- ANSWER ---
Clausing involves writing a remark on the face of the bill of lading representing the Master's factual findings based on their best assessment of the actual description and condition of the goods.

--- QUESTION 0714 ---
When and where should a clause be written on a Bill of Lading?
--- ANSWER ---
The clausing must be completed promptly (not left until the completion of loading) and should be made directly on the face of the bill of lading.

--- QUESTION 0715 ---
What should a Master do if a Bill of Lading states cargo specifications like moisture content or temperature that they were unable to verify?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master should clause the bill of lading accordingly to reflect that they were unable to verify those specific parameters.

--- QUESTION 0716 ---
What should the Master do if there is a severe disagreement regarding the clausing of a Bill of Lading?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master should notify the Company immediately, seek assistance/advice from the local P&I correspondent, and issue a formal written protest to the cargo interests.

--- QUESTION 0717 ---
How many "Original" copies of a Bill of Lading are traditionally issued, and what is the restriction on this number?
--- ANSWER ---
Traditionally three original copies are issued (for the Shipper, Consignee, and Freight Forwarder/Bank/Broker). There is no legal restriction on the total quantity that can be issued, but the exact number issued must be explicitly stated on the document.

--- QUESTION 0718 ---
What actions should a Master take if three different individuals approach them at the discharge port, each holding a valid original Bill of Lading?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master may take reasonable time to investigate and determine the genuine document. They must contact the Company/Charterer and the local P&I club correspondent. In extreme cases, the local court may be approached to legally decide to whom the cargo should be released.

--- QUESTION 0719 ---
What is a Letter of Indemnity (LOI)?
--- ANSWER ---
A Letter of Indemnity is a promise made by the issuer to indemnify the recipient against defined liabilities.

--- QUESTION 0720 ---
What are three standard situations where a Letter of Indemnity may be given?
--- ANSWER ---
An LOI is commonly exchanged for:

1. Delivery of cargo without production of the original bill of lading.
2. Delivery of cargo at a port other than the one stated in the bill of lading.
3. Delivery of cargo at a different port without production of the original bill of lading.

--- QUESTION 0721 ---
Are Letters of Indemnity offered in exchange for issuing a clean Bill of Lading on damaged goods legally binding?
--- ANSWER ---
No. An LOI offered to secure a clean Bill of Lading for inaccurately marked or damaged goods is not legally binding and offers the company no protection if the shipper fails to uphold their promise.

--- QUESTION 0722 ---
What is the difference between a Letter of Protest and Noting Protest?
--- ANSWER ---
A Letter of Protest is a written communication recording dissatisfaction to another party regarding an operational matter under their control, holding them responsible for consequences. Noting Protest is a formal declaration under oath by the Master that circumstances beyond control have occurred (or may have occurred) causing damage or forcing actions that could render the owners legally liable.

--- QUESTION 0723 ---
What is an Extended Note of Protest and when should it be executed?
--- ANSWER ---
An Extended Note of Protest is a detailed follow-up declaration made when the full extent of loss or damage becomes clear. At the time of initially noting protest, the Master should explicitly reserve the right to extend it once relevant facts come to light.

--- QUESTION 0724 ---
According to the recommended text for incorporating a charterparty into a bill of lading, which specific four types of clauses must be explicitly included alongside terms, conditions, and warranties?
--- ANSWER ---
The arbitration, choice of law, time bar, and time limitation clauses.

--- QUESTION 0725 ---
In standard commercial practice, how many original bills of lading are normally prepared in a set?
--- ANSWER ---
Three originals.

--- QUESTION 0726 ---
Why should a ship retain a non-negotiable copy of the signed bill of lading?
--- ANSWER ---
To confirm that the bill of lading presented in exchange for delivery of the cargo is the same in all respects (endorsements aside) as the one issued.

--- QUESTION 0727 ---
Under what circumstance is a straight bill of lading considered a document of title?
--- ANSWER ---
When it expressly states that delivery shall only be made against presentation of an original bill of lading.

--- QUESTION 0728 ---
To which specific individual must the Master ensure delivery is made when presented with an order bill of lading?
--- ANSWER ---
The last valid endorsee presenting an original bill of lading.

--- QUESTION 0729 ---
What is the primary procedural difference regarding presentation between a waybill and a document of title bill of lading?
--- ANSWER ---
Presentation of the original waybill is not necessary for delivery, whereas a document of title bill of lading requires presentation of an original.

--- QUESTION 0730 ---
If an endorsed straight bill of lading is presented for delivery, what action must the Master take before discharging the cargo?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master should ask the Company to make appropriate investigations to confirm the position, as the laws of some countries permit straight bills of lading to be negotiated by endorsement.

--- QUESTION 0731 ---
According to Gard's Rules, what are the two main commercial and insurance consequences of misdelivering cargo or delivering without an original bill of lading?
--- ANSWER ---
The carrier faces full liability for the value of the cargo due to wrongful delivery, and the loss of P&I cover.

--- QUESTION 0732 ---
What must the Master do if an original bill of lading is presented, but another person simultaneously demands delivery of the same cargo?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master must not discharge the cargo until authorized by the Company, treating the situation as a notice of a defect or dispute as to title.

--- QUESTION 0733 ---
Why should the Master resist a charterer's or shipper's request to accept a letter of indemnity (LOI) in exchange for delivering cargo without the original bill of lading?
--- ANSWER ---
Due to the high risks involved in accepting such letters or undertakings, including the potential loss of P&I cover.

--- QUESTION 0734 ---
Under the Hague/Hague-Visby Rules, what are the two specific sets of elements the Master is obligated to inspect regarding the cargo?
--- ANSWER ---

1. The apparent order and condition of the cargo.
2. The cargo's marks, and its number, quantity, or weight (unless there are no reasonable means of inspection).

--- QUESTION 0735 ---
Why is it critical for the Master to inspect cargo before or during loading rather than relying on descriptions provided by the shipper?
--- ANSWER ---
To verify the accuracy of the cargo description before signing, thereby avoiding commercial pressure to sign inaccurate bills of lading which can lead to legal liability.

--- QUESTION 0736 ---
For steel cargoes, what specific recommendation does the Association make regarding cargo inspection and bill of lading clausing?
--- ANSWER ---
The Association recommends that surveyors be appointed to perform the cargo inspection and to assist the Master in clausing the bills of lading.

--- QUESTION 0737 ---
When shore loading gear is used, what is the specific point in time that establishes the "time of shipment" for the cargo?
--- ANSWER ---
When the goods cross over the vessel's rail.

--- QUESTION 0738 ---
For liquid or gas cargoes, when is the relevant "time of shipment" established?
--- ANSWER ---
When the cargo passes through the vessel's manifold.

--- QUESTION 0739 ---
If a cargo is inspected prior to shipment and is damaged by a stevedore during loading (after the relevant time of shipment), should this damage be recorded in the bill of lading?
--- ANSWER ---
No, damage or loss occurring after the relevant time of shipment should not be recorded in the bill of lading.

--- QUESTION 0740 ---
In the context of cargo inspections, what does the term "apparent" legally mean for a ship's officer?
--- ANSWER ---
It means what is recognizable to a proper and responsible ship's officer with reasonable knowledge and experience, rather than what is recognizable to a technical expert or via hidden damage.

--- QUESTION 0741 ---
Is a Master responsible for verifying statements regarding the "quality" of a cargo, such as whether yellow corn is US number one or two?
--- ANSWER ---
No, "order and condition" does not mean or include quality, and the Master is not expected to verify cargo quality.

--- QUESTION 0742 ---
How should a liquid cargo of a suspicious or abnormal color be treated by the Master during inspection?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master should err on the side of caution and assume the apparent discoloration concerns the "condition" of the cargo rather than its quality.

--- QUESTION 0743 ---
What is the proper procedural protocol for inspecting bulk cargoes during loading?
--- ANSWER ---
It should be inspected at regular intervals during loading (if reasonable and safe) to obtain a representative picture of the entire cargo's apparent order and condition.

--- QUESTION 0744 ---
If a container seal is found broken at the time of loading, what steps must the ship's crew perform?
--- ANSWER ---
The container should be opened if practicable, the outward appearance of the contents inspected, the container re-sealed, and the new seal number recorded.

--- QUESTION 0745 ---
Why does the text recommend that container weights be checked and recorded frequently, even for reportedly empty containers?
--- ANSWER ---
To prevent fraud where empty containers are shipped but bills of lading are issued for non-existent cargo, which exposes the carrier to severe liability.

--- QUESTION 0746 ---
What document serves as the primary basis for checking the accuracy of the cargo description before a bill of lading is signed?
--- ANSWER ---
The mate's receipt.

--- QUESTION 0747 ---
If a replacement bill of lading cannot be provided due to time constraints, what action must the Master take on an inaccurate bill of lading before signing it?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master must clause the bill of lading by writing a factual remark based on his best assessment of the cargo.

--- QUESTION 0748 ---
Why is a standard printed clause such as "shipper's load and count" or "particulars furnished by shipper" generally insufficient for protecting a carrier?
--- ANSWER ---
They are not likely to afford the carrier protection from inaccurate descriptions, and some jurisdictions do not recognize printed, standard form qualifications.

--- QUESTION 0749 ---
What formatting method is advised when applying a clause to a bill of lading to ensure it is legally recognized over standard printed forms?
--- ANSWER ---
It should always be applied in the Master's own handwriting or in typed text.

--- QUESTION 0750 ---
What is the full meaning of the acronym SOPEP?
--- ANSWER ---
Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan

--- QUESTION 0751 ---
According to MARPOL, what is the gross tonnage threshold for non-tanker ships to be required to carry a Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan (SOPEP)?
--- ANSWER ---
All ships over 400 gross tonnage (GT).

--- QUESTION 0752 ---
According to MARPOL, what is the gross tonnage threshold for oil tankers to be required to carry a Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan (SOPEP)?
--- ANSWER ---
Tankers over 150 gross tonnage (GT).

--- QUESTION 0753 ---
In what language must the Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan (SOPEP) be written?
--- ANSWER ---
The working language of the Master and officers.

--- QUESTION 0754 ---
For machinery space discharges outside special areas, what is the maximum allowable oil content of the effluent?
--- ANSWER ---
The effluent must not exceed 15 parts per million (PPM) without dilution.

--- QUESTION 0755 ---
What additional equipment features must be fitted to the oil filtering system when discharging from machinery spaces inside a Special Area?
--- ANSWER ---
An alarm and an auto-stop device.

--- QUESTION 0756 ---
In which document must machinery space bilge discharge operations be recorded?
--- ANSWER ---
Oil Record Book (ORB) Part I.

--- QUESTION 0757 ---
What monitoring system must be operational during any discharge from the cargo or ballast areas of an oil tanker?
--- ANSWER ---
The Oil Discharge Monitoring and Control System (ODM).

--- QUESTION 0758 ---
What is the maximum instantaneous rate of oil discharge permitted from the cargo and ballast areas of an oil tanker when operating outside special areas?
--- ANSWER ---
Maximum 30 litres per nautical mile (NM).

--- QUESTION 0759 ---
What is the minimum distance from the nearest land required to legally discharge oily mixtures from an oil tanker's cargo area?
--- ANSWER ---
At least 50 nautical miles (NM) from the nearest land.

--- QUESTION 0760 ---
What is the maximum total quantity of cargo oil that can be discharged into the sea during a ballast voyage outside special areas?
--- ANSWER ---
Not more than 1/30,000 of the total cargo quantity.

--- QUESTION 0761 ---
In which document must cargo and ballast operations for oil tankers be recorded?
--- ANSWER ---
Oil Record Book (ORB) Part II.

--- QUESTION 0762 ---
What is the maximum oxygen content permitted in the gas supplied by an Inert Gas System (IGS) to the cargo tanks?
--- ANSWER ---
An oxygen supply not greater than 5% by volume.

--- QUESTION 0763 ---
Under normal operational conditions, what is the maximum oxygen level by volume that must be maintained within the atmosphere of an oil tanker's cargo tanks?
--- ANSWER ---
An oxygen atmosphere not exceeding 8% by volume.

--- QUESTION 0764 ---
From where do large crude oil tankers normally extract gas for use in their primary Inert Gas System?
--- ANSWER ---
From the boilers' flue gas.

--- QUESTION 0765 ---
In a typical flue gas system, where is the hot flue gas cleaned and cooled before being distributed to the cargo tanks?
--- ANSWER ---
In a scrubber located within the IG Flat.

--- QUESTION 0766 ---
Which component of an Inert Gas System serves as a non-return safety barrier between the machinery spaces and the cargo deck area?
--- ANSWER ---
The deck water seal (along with the mains IG non-return valve).

--- QUESTION 0767 ---
What venting component is utilized to release excess inert gas into the atmosphere from the main line if necessary?
--- ANSWER ---
The mast riser.

--- QUESTION 0768 ---
What safety device is common to the entire IGS piping system and uses water to protect the cargo tanks against both over-pressure and structural vacuum?
--- ANSWER ---
The P/V Breaker (Pressure/Vacuum Breaker).

--- QUESTION 0769 ---
What individual devices are fitted to individual cargo tanks to manage localized excess pressure and vacuum?
--- ANSWER ---
P/V Valves (Pressure/Vacuum Valves).

--- QUESTION 0770 ---
Why must a portable Oxygen Analyser always be tested before use?
--- ANSWER ---
To ensure accurate readings by calibrating the circuit in fresh air.

--- QUESTION 0771 ---
What electrical circuit principle is utilized by an Explosimeter to detect hydrocarbons?
--- ANSWER ---
The Wheatstone bridge principle (where more hydrocarbon gas creates more heat, which increases resistance and alters the parallel circuit meter reading).

--- QUESTION 0772 ---
Which specific portable gas detector is required to measure hydrocarbon levels in an oxygen-deficient or inerted atmosphere?
--- ANSWER ---
A Tankscope.

--- QUESTION 0773 ---
Which manual gas detection equipment relies on interchangeable colorimetric tubes that must be physically pumped or squeezed to test for different toxic gases?
--- ANSWER ---
'Draeger' Tubes.

--- QUESTION 0774 ---
Which maritime safety publication contains Chapter 15, which outlines instructions for entering dangerous or enclosed spaces?
--- ANSWER ---
The Code of Safe Working Practices for Merchant Seafarers (COSWP).

--- QUESTION 0775 ---
What interactive check-list, referenced in ISGOTT, must be completed prior to loading and discharging operations at a terminal?
--- ANSWER ---
The Ship/Shore Safety Check-List (SSSCL).

--- QUESTION 0776 ---
In which publication would you find the standard hydrocarbon gas/air/inert gas flammability composition diagram?
--- ANSWER ---
[IMAGE: page-17.jpg]
The International Safety Guide for Oil Tankers and Terminals (ISGOTT).

--- QUESTION 0777 ---
What is the term for the lowest temperature at which a liquid gives off enough vapour to form an ignitable mixture in air near its surface?
--- ANSWER ---
Flash Point

--- QUESTION 0778 ---
What is the definition of Auto Ignition temperature?
--- ANSWER ---
It is the temperature at or above which a material will spontaneously ignite (catch fire) without an external spark or flame.

--- QUESTION 0779 ---
What is generally considered as the Lower Flammable Limit (LFL/LEL) percentage by volume for crude oils?
--- ANSWER ---
1%

--- QUESTION 0780 ---
What is generally considered as the Upper Flammable Limit (UFL/UEL) percentage by volume for crude oils?
--- ANSWER ---
10%

--- QUESTION 0781 ---
What term defines the range of hydrocarbon gas concentration in air between the lower and upper flammable limits where mixtures are capable of being ignited and burning?
--- ANSWER ---
Flammable Range

--- QUESTION 0782 ---
What is the term used to describe the space above the liquid in a tank?
--- ANSWER ---
Ullage

--- QUESTION 0783 ---
What are the typical dimensions (length, width, and draft) of a modern LNG carrier ship?
--- ANSWER ---
Approximately 300 metres long, 43 metres wide, and has a draft of about 12 metres.

--- QUESTION 0784 ---
LNG carriers are capable of speeds up to how many knots?
--- ANSWER ---
21 knots

--- QUESTION 0785 ---
What are the two major design categories for tanks used to carry LNG on the majority of ships sailing today?
--- ANSWER ---
Spherical tanks (Moss sphere design) and geometric membrane tanks (membrane design).

--- QUESTION 0786 ---
Which international code governs the design and construction of gas carriers?
--- ANSWER ---
IGC Code

--- QUESTION 0787 ---
At what temperature is Liquefied Natural Gas (LNG) typically carried on a carrier ship?
--- ANSWER ---
-160 deg C (or -162 deg C based on hazard descriptions)

--- QUESTION 0788 ---
What is used as fuel on an LNG carrier ship during transit?
--- ANSWER ---
The cargo vapours (Boil off)

--- QUESTION 0789 ---
At what temperature threshold does LNG transition from being heavier than air to being lighter than air?
--- ANSWER ---
-110 deg C (It is lighter than air at temperatures warmer than -110 deg C and heavier at temperatures lesser than -110 deg C)

--- QUESTION 0790 ---
What is the liquid-to-vapour expansion ratio of LNG?
--- ANSWER ---
1:600

--- QUESTION 0791 ---
What certificate issued by the Flag Administration confirms that an LNG carrier complies with the relevant Gas Codes, and what is its validity?
--- ANSWER ---
Certificate of Fitness; valid for 5 years subject to Annual surveys and Intermediate Survey.

--- QUESTION 0792 ---
If LNG comes into direct contact with structural steel, what physical damage does it cause?
--- ANSWER ---
Brittle fracture

--- QUESTION 0793 ---
What solid substance resembling crushed or slush ice can form when hydrocarbon cargoes combine with water, potentially causing pumps to seize?
--- ANSWER ---
Hydrate

--- QUESTION 0794 ---
What are the primary and secondary barriers of a Membrane Containment System made of?
--- ANSWER ---
Cryogenic invar (36% nickel steel with a very low co-efficient of thermal expansion)

--- QUESTION 0795 ---
The barrier space in a membrane containment system consists of plywood boxes filled with which insulating material, and what gas is used to fill these spaces?
--- ANSWER ---
Perlite; Nitrogen gas

--- QUESTION 0796 ---
What is the typical designed boil-off rate of total cargo tank volume per day for a membrane containment system?
--- ANSWER ---
0.15%

--- QUESTION 0797 ---
According to the IGC code, for how many days must the secondary barrier be capable of containing tank leakage?
--- ANSWER ---
15 days

--- QUESTION 0798 ---
What is the maximum loading limit by volume permitted for membrane ships under the IGC code?
--- ANSWER ---
98% by volume (though some administrations may allow 98.5% taking into account voyage boil-off)

--- QUESTION 0799 ---
On an LNG carrier, fixed gas detection systems use an infrared gas analyser. At what LEL percentages do the audible/visual alarms and the equipment trip/gas burning trip trigger?
--- ANSWER ---
Audible and Visual Alarm triggers at 30% LEL; Gas burning and running equipment trip triggers at 60% LEL.

--- QUESTION 0800 ---
What are the three specific alarm thresholds by volume percentage for tank filling levels on an LNG carrier, and what actions do they trigger?
--- ANSWER ---
95% triggers High level alarm (Audio and Visual); 98.5% triggers High high level alarm and shuts the filling valve; 99% triggers Extremely high level alarm and Emergency Shutdown (ESD).

--- QUESTION 0801 ---
What is the minimum number of pressure relief valves that must be fitted to each LNG cargo tank?
--- ANSWER ---
2 pressure relief valves

--- QUESTION 0802 ---
What temperature range are the fusible elements in the ESD system designed to melt at in the event of a fire?
--- ANSWER ---
Between 98 deg C and 104 deg C

--- QUESTION 0803 ---
What is the functional difference between an ESD 1 system and an ESD 2 system on an LNG carrier?
--- ANSWER ---
ESD 1 shuts down cargo transfer by closing valves and stopping pumps; ESD 2 executes ESD 1 functions and also uncouples the loading arms after closing both ERS isolation valves.

--- QUESTION 0804 ---
Before purging cargo tanks with inert gas during a post-dock operation, to what maximum dew point must the cargo tanks be lowered using dry air, and why?
--- ANSWER ---
At least -25 deg C; to avoid the formation of corrosive agents when humidity combines with sulphur and nitrogen oxides in the inert gas.

--- QUESTION 0805 ---
Why must combustion-generated inert gas be prevented from coming into contact with cold LNG, and how is this risk eliminated?
--- ANSWER ---
It contains approximately 15% CO2 which freezes at -56 deg C and produces a white powder that can block valves, filters, and nozzles; the risk is eliminated by the "gassing up" operation.

--- QUESTION 0806 ---
During the "gassing up" operation, at what temperature is the warm LNG vapour introduced, and where is it injected into the tank?
--- ANSWER ---
Approximately +20/25 deg C; introduced at the top of the tank.

--- QUESTION 0807 ---
When is the gassing up operation of an LNG cargo tank considered complete?
--- ANSWER ---
When the methane content exceeds 88% in each cargo tank.

--- QUESTION 0808 ---
Cool down of an LNG cargo tank is considered complete when the mean temperature sensors (excluding the top 2) register what temperature or lower?
--- ANSWER ---
-130 deg C or lower

--- QUESTION 0809 ---
During the cool down process of an LNG tank, within what range must the pressure of the primary and secondary insulation spaces be maintained?
--- ANSWER ---
Between 2 and 4 mBar

--- QUESTION 0810 ---
As per SOLAS Chapter II-2, an Inert Gas System is required for oil tankers of what minimum deadweight tonnage?
--- ANSWER ---
20,000 tonnes deadweight and upwards

--- QUESTION 0811 ---
To maintain tanks in an inert condition and avoid fire on an oil tanker, the oxygen content must be kept below what percentage by volume?
--- ANSWER ---
Less than 8% by volume (with the IG line itself not exceeding 5% by volume)

--- QUESTION 0812 ---
An oil tanker's inert gas system must be capable of delivering gas at what minimum rate relative to the ship's maximum discharge capacity?
--- ANSWER ---
At least 125% of the maximum rate of discharge capacity.

--- QUESTION 0813 ---
What is the general percentage composition of Carbon Dioxide (CO2) and Oxygen (O2) in flue gas from main boilers used as inert gas?
--- ANSWER ---
13% CO2 and 4% O2

--- QUESTION 0814 ---
Define the distinction between the terms "Inerting", "Purging", and "Gas Freeing" on a tanker.
--- ANSWER ---
Inerting is introducing IG to bring O2 below 8% volume; Purging is introducing IG into an already inert tank to further reduce O2 and hydrocarbon levels; Gas Freeing is introducing fresh air to remove toxic and flammable gases for safe entry or hot work.

--- QUESTION 0815 ---
What three steady readings must be obtained on meters before an enclosed space entry can be permitted during gas freeing?
--- ANSWER ---
21% oxygen by volume, not more than 1% of the lower flammable limit (LFL), and not more than 50% of the occupational exposure limit (OEL) of any toxic vapours.

--- QUESTION 0816 ---
At what volume percentages do the High Level Alarm and the Overfill Alarm trigger on an oil tanker?
--- ANSWER ---
High Level Alarm triggers at 95% by volume; Overfill Alarm triggers independently at 98% by volume.

--- QUESTION 0817 ---
What device on an oil tanker provides protection against both over-pressurisation and under-pressurisation across the common inert gas line?
--- ANSWER ---
Pressure-Vacuum Breaker (P/V Breaker)

--- QUESTION 0818 ---
What are the three different types of deck seals used on tankers to prevent the backflow of hydrocarbon gases into the inert gas plant?
--- ANSWER ---
Wet, Semidry, and Dry

--- QUESTION 0819 ---
According to MARPOL Annex I discharge criteria, what is the minimum distance from the nearest land and the maximum instantaneous rate of discharge permitted for an oil tanker discharging outside a special area?
--- ANSWER ---
More than 50 nautical miles from nearest land; instantaneous discharge rate must not exceed 30 litres per nautical mile.

--- QUESTION 0820 ---
What is the maximum total quantity of oil permitted to be discharged into the sea for a tanker delivered after 31 December 1979?
--- ANSWER ---
1/30,000 of the total quantity of the particular cargo of which the residue formed a part.

--- QUESTION 0821 ---
Tankers built on or after January 1, 2012, with a deadweight tonnage exceeding 20,000 DWT must have a fixed gas detection system fitted in which specific spaces?
--- ANSWER ---
Ballast tanks (in addition to the Pump Room)

--- QUESTION 0822 ---
Which tool is used to measure hydrocarbon content as a percentage of the Lower Flammable Limit (% LEL) in an atmosphere, and what electrical circuit principle does it employ?
--- ANSWER ---
Explosimeter (Combustible gas detector); it works on the principle of a Wheatstone bridge using a catalytic heated filament sensor.

--- QUESTION 0823 ---
What instrument is specifically used to measure hydrocarbon content by volume percentage (% Vol) in an inert atmosphere, and how does its sensing element differ from an explosimeter?
--- ANSWER ---
Tankscope; it uses a non-catalytic hot filament sensor instead of a catalytic one.

--- QUESTION 0824 ---
What physical property of oxygen gas is utilized by an oxygen analyser to determine its concentration via a Wheatstone bridge circuit?
--- ANSWER ---
Oxygen is paramagnetic (it is attracted by a magnetic field).

--- QUESTION 0825 ---
What are the three mandatory sensor inputs required for an ECDIS?
--- ANSWER ---

1. GPS (Ship's position fixing system)
2. Gyro compass (Heading)
3. Doppler Log (Speed and distance measuring device)

--- QUESTION 0826 ---
What type of information does the Admiralty Information Overlay (AIO) contain, and how is it intended to be used on an ECDIS?
--- ANSWER ---
The Admiralty Information Overlay (AIO) contains additional navigation information for mariners and is designed to be displayed on top of ENCs on an ECDIS.

--- QUESTION 0827 ---
How are all in-force temporary Notices to Mariners displayed visually within the Admiralty Information Overlay (AIO)?
--- ANSWER ---
Each notice is displayed as a red polygon with a red hatched fill indicating the area where the Notice to Mariners is in effect.

--- QUESTION 0828 ---
What navigationally significant differences do ENC Preliminary (EP) Notices to Mariners highlight?
--- ANSWER ---
They highlight navigationally significant differences between ENCs and ADMIRALTY paper charts.

--- QUESTION 0829 ---
How does the Admiralty Information Overlay (AIO) indicate areas where there is no ADMIRALTY paper chart at an equivalent scale to the ENC?
--- ANSWER ---
It displays a grey hatched polygon labelled 'No Overlay'.

--- QUESTION 0830 ---
What three levels of training are required for navigating officers with regards to ECDIS?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Generic ECDIS training (IMO 1.27 Model Course)
2. Approved Type-Specific training or a company-considered equivalent
3. Familiarisation with the onboard equipment as part of bridge familiarisation

--- QUESTION 0831 ---
Which core safety feature of an ECDIS acts as an alarmable feature to distinguish between safe and unsafe areas, detect isolated dangers, and raise an anti-grounding alarm?
--- ANSWER ---
The Safety Contour.

--- QUESTION 0832 ---
What are the two non-alarmable depth display settings available in a four-shade depth display on an ECDIS besides the safety contour?
--- ANSWER ---
Shallow contour and Deep contour.

--- QUESTION 0833 ---
What is the default pre-programmed safety contour setting within an ENC?
--- ANSWER ---
30 metres.

--- QUESTION 0834 ---
How is the Safety Depth visually represented on an ECDIS screen, and does it possess an alarm feature?
--- ANSWER ---
Depths at or below the safety depth setting are highlighted in bold, while depths above the setting are displayed in grey. It has no alarm feature.

--- QUESTION 0835 ---
What is the general formula used to calculate Safety Depth?
--- ANSWER ---
Safety Depth = Maximum Draft (static) + UKC (Company's Policy) + Squat (Maximum) - Height of Tide

--- QUESTION 0836 ---
What accuracy data should be considered in a vessel's Under Keel Clearance (UKC) calculation unless up-to-date local information is available?
--- ANSWER ---
The accuracy of CATZOC data.

--- QUESTION 0837 ---
According to IHO regulations, what does a CATZOC rating of A1 indicate regarding seafloor coverage and survey characteristics?
--- ANSWER ---
It indicates a full area search was undertaken, significant seafloor features were detected, and a controlled, systematic survey achieved high position and depth accuracy.

--- QUESTION 0838 ---
What three categories must SENC information available for display during route planning and route monitoring be subdivided into?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Display base
2. Standard Display
3. All Other Information

--- QUESTION 0839 ---
Why is the Display Base setting on an ECDIS not intended to be sufficient for safe navigation?
--- ANSWER ---
It represents the bare minimum level of SENC information that cannot be removed from the display and is always available for all geographical areas, but lacks the detail required for safe voyage execution.

--- QUESTION 0840 ---
Which ECDIS display category provides the International Maritime Organization's (IMO's) bare minimum information required for route planning and voyage execution?
--- ANSWER ---
Standard Display.

--- QUESTION 0841 ---
What does the term 'Custom Display' mean on an ECDIS?
--- ANSWER ---
It means the display mode as configured by the mariner for route planning or route monitoring, selecting layers from 'All Other Information'.

--- QUESTION 0842 ---
Which important ECDIS and ENC standards have been developed by the IHO to ensure electronic charts are accurate, secure, and correctly interpreted by type-approved ECDIS?
--- ANSWER ---
S-57, S-63, and S-52.

--- QUESTION 0843 ---
What purpose does the S-58 standard serve before an ENC is released to mariners?
--- ANSWER ---
It sets out validation checks to be performed by hydrographic offices to ensure there are no irregularities in an ENC that could cause an ECDIS to malfunction.

--- QUESTION 0844 ---
What three key areas are covered by the S-63 standard?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Piracy Protection (encryption to prevent unauthorized use)
2. Selective Access (restricting access to licensed cells)
3. Authentication (assurance that data comes from approved sources)

--- QUESTION 0845 ---
Which IHO standard determines how ENC data is displayed on an ECDIS screen through uniform symbols, line styles, and colors?
--- ANSWER ---
S-52.

--- QUESTION 0846 ---
What is the latest version of the Presentation Library mentioned in the IHO standards text?
--- ANSWER ---
Version 4.0.

--- QUESTION 0847 ---
What does the abbreviation SCAMIN stand for, and what is its primary purpose?
--- ANSWER ---
SCAMIN stands for Scale Minimum. Its purpose is to reduce the amount of clutter displayed to the ECDIS user by making objects invisible below a specified display scale.

--- QUESTION 0848 ---
How should the SCAMIN tool be configured during passage planning versus navigation?
--- ANSWER ---
SCAMIN should be turned off for passage planning (so everything is displayed) and turned on for navigation purposes (to reduce clutter).

--- QUESTION 0849 ---
Name at least four operational or system limitations of an ECDIS that can affect a navigator's situational awareness.
--- ANSWER ---
Any four of the following: Lack of global coverage, "Garbage in Garbage out" (sensor dependency), wrong user settings, cyber security/viruses, information overload, alarm fatigue, and over-reliance/tunnel vision.

--- QUESTION 0850 ---
What is the difference between an alarm and an indication according to IMO Performance Standards?
--- ANSWER ---
An alarm announces a condition requiring attention by audible or audible and visual means. An indication provides purely visual information about the condition of a system or equipment.

--- QUESTION 0851 ---
List four mandatory alarms available on an ECDIS as per IMO Performance Standards.
--- ANSWER ---
Any four of the following:

1. Crossing safety contour
2. Deviation from route (XTE)
3. Positioning system failure
4. Approach to critical point
5. Different geodetic datum

--- QUESTION 0852 ---
In which document are the details of a ship's navigational systems and equipment, including the means of complying with SOLAS Chapter V Regulation 19, recorded?
--- ANSWER ---
Section 3 of Form E (Record of Equipment for the Cargo Ship Safety Equipment Certificate).

--- QUESTION 0853 ---
What initial actions should be taken if a second officer reports that no ENC is available for a designated leg of a passage on a paperless vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
Carry out a thorough Risk Assessment, check for feasible alternate routes, inform the Designated Person Ashore (DPA), consult the company's SMS, and ensure an up-to-date portfolio of paper charts is carried for RCDS mode operation.

--- QUESTION 0854 ---
What is a Virtual AIS Aid to Navigation (AtoN)?
--- ANSWER ---
An aid to navigation with no physical structure that exists only through AIS messages broadcast from another location, visible on ECDIS or radar.

--- QUESTION 0855 ---
What is a Synthetic AIS Aid to Navigation (AtoN)?
--- ANSWER ---
A physical aid to navigation structure that does not possess its own AIS transmitter, but has its AIS messages broadcast for it from another location (usually land-based).

--- QUESTION 0856 ---
How does the physical representation of a Real/Synthetic AIS AtoN differ from a Virtual AIS AtoN on an ECDIS symbol display?
--- ANSWER ---
A Real or Synthetic AIS AtoN is depicted with a solid-lined diamond shape, whereas a Virtual AIS AtoN is depicted with a dotted-lined diamond shape.

--- QUESTION 0857 ---
Under what condition can an OOW holding a NARAS/NAEST certificate issued in 2005 satisfy the generic ECDIS training requirement?
--- ANSWER ---
The MCA-approved NARAS/NAEST course must have been completed on or after 1 January 2005.

--- QUESTION 0858 ---
What are four four potential errors or environmental factors that can affect the accuracy of an echo sounder's readings?
--- ANSWER ---
Any four of the following: Electrical noise, cavitation, wrong draught entry, salinity changes, water temperature changes, unreliability in shallow water, biological noise, round-the-clock error, and Pythagoras error.

--- QUESTION 0859 ---
Why do high salinity and high temperature (such as in the Red Sea) affect an echo sounder's readout?
--- ANSWER ---
They increase the speed of sound in water, causing the measured depth to appear shallower than it actually is.

--- QUESTION 0860 ---
Into what two broad categories can satellite navigation (GNSS/GPS) errors be divided?
--- ANSWER ---
Temporal errors (those that change with time) and Spatial errors (those that change with location).

--- QUESTION 0861 ---
Identify three main components or locations where errors can originate within a GPS/GNSS system.
--- ANSWER ---

1. The satellites (orbit errors, clocks)
2. The radio signal in space (ionosphere/troposphere delay, multipath reflections)
3. The receiver on the ground (user equipment errors)

What maritime symbol or display status is indicated by the ECDIS symbol on page-1.jpg?
--- ANSWER ---

--- ANSWER ---

What navigation system equipment or signal type is identified on page-17.jpg?
--- ANSWER ---

Radar Transponder

What specific floating aid to navigation with an audible warning is indicated on page-19.jpg?

[IMAGE: page-18.jpg]
Lighted super buoy with fog signal

What maritime service location or boarding facility is shown on page-23.jpg?

Pilot Station

--- QUESTION 0870 ---
What category of navigation aids is identified on page-25.jpg?

Special marks

What ECDIS data quality indicator or feature classification is shown on page-27.jpg?

[IMAGE: page-26.jpg]
Catzoc (Category of Zone of Confidence)

What chart feature or text indicator is represented on page-29.jpg?

Additional information note

What traffic routing feature is identified on page-3.jpg?

roundabout

What category of IALA buoyage marks is identified on page-31.jpg?

What type of anchoring or securing arrangement is shown on page-33.jpg?

Mooring buoy

What specific underwater hazards are represented by the symbol on page-35.jpg?
--- ANSWER ---
[IMAGE: page-34.jpg]
Isolated danger; wreck rock or obstruction

What type of chart entry or warning indicator is represented on page-37.jpg?
--- ANSWER ---
[IMAGE: page-36.jpg]
Isolated query mark indicating insufficient information to symbolise feature

--- QUESTION 0878 ---
What lateral aid to navigation system and side is identified on page-5.jpg?
--- ANSWER ---
[IMAGE: page-4.jpg]
Stbd Lateral Mark IALA A

--- QUESTION 0879 ---
What lateral aid to navigation system and side is identified on page-7.jpg?
--- ANSWER ---
[IMAGE: page-6.jpg]
Port hand lateral IALA B

--- QUESTION 0880 ---
What specific temporary or specialized navigation mark is identified on page-9.jpg?
--- ANSWER ---
[IMAGE: page-8.jpg]
Emergency Wreck or special purpose

--- QUESTION 0881 ---
Which chapter of SOLAS covers the FSS Code and Inert Gas System requirements for oil tankers?
--- ANSWER ---
SOLAS Chapter II-2.

--- QUESTION 0882 ---
What mandatory certificate must be carried by an oil tanker under the regulations of MARPOL Annex I?
--- ANSWER ---
The International Oil Pollution Prevention (IOPP) Certificate.

--- QUESTION 0883 ---
What is the difference between Form A and Form B of the Record of Construction and Equipment supplemented to the IOPP Certificate?
--- ANSWER ---
Form A is the Record of Construction and Equipment for ships other than oil tankers, whereas Form B is specifically for oil tankers.

--- QUESTION 0884 ---
Which part of the Oil Record Book handles machinery space operations, and which vessels must carry it?
--- ANSWER ---
Oil Record Book Part I handles machinery space operations. It must be provided to every oil tanker of 150 gross tonnage and above, and every other ship of 400 gross tonnage and above.

--- QUESTION 0885 ---
How frequently must the quantities of oil residues (sludge) retained on board be recorded in the Oil Record Book Part I?
--- ANSWER ---
The quantity must be recorded weekly, even if the voyage lasts more than one week.

--- QUESTION 0886 ---
Which part of the Oil Record Book is used to record cargo and ballast operations on oil tankers of 150 gross tonnage and above?
--- ANSWER ---
Oil Record Book Part II.

--- QUESTION 0887 ---
What does the acronym SOPEP stand for?
--- ANSWER ---
Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan.

--- QUESTION 0888 ---
What are the gross tonnage thresholds that require a vessel to carry a Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan (SOPEP)?
--- ANSWER ---
All ships over 400 gross tonnage (GT) and all oil tankers over 150 gross tonnage (GT).

--- QUESTION 0889 ---
Under MARPOL discharge requirements for machinery spaces outside special areas, what is the maximum allowable oil content of the effluent?
--- ANSWER ---
The oil content of the effluent must not exceed 15 parts per million (PPM) without dilution.

--- QUESTION 0890 ---
What additional components must be fitted to the oil filtering equipment when discharging machinery space bilge water inside a Special Area?
--- ANSWER ---
An alarm and an automatic stopping device.

--- QUESTION 0891 ---
What is the maximum instantaneous rate of discharge allowed for oil or oily mixtures from the cargo and ballast areas of an oil tanker when operating outside special areas?
--- ANSWER ---
30 litres per nautical mile (NM).

--- QUESTION 0892 ---
What is the minimum distance from the nearest land required to discharge oil or oily mixtures from the cargo area of an oil tanker?
--- ANSWER ---
At least 50 nautical miles (NM).

--- QUESTION 0893 ---
Outside special areas, what is the maximum total quantity of oil that a tanker can discharge during a ballast voyage?
--- ANSWER ---
Not more than 1/30,000 of the total quantity of the particular cargo of which the residue formed a part.

--- QUESTION 0894 ---
