According to the FSS Code, what is the maximum allowable oxygen content for inert gas supplied from the Inert Gas System to the cargo tanks?
--- ANSWER ---
An oxygen supply not greater than 5% by volume.

--- QUESTION 0895 ---
To keep the tank atmosphere outside the flammable range, what maximum oxygen level by volume must not be exceeded within the cargo tanks?
--- ANSWER ---
8% by volume.

--- QUESTION 0896 ---
How is Zone 0 defined in the grading of hazardous areas at a terminal?
--- ANSWER ---
An area in which a flammable gas mixture is continuously present or is present for long periods.

--- QUESTION 0897 ---
How is Zone 1 defined in the grading of hazardous areas at a terminal?
--- ANSWER ---
An area in which a flammable gas mixture is likely to be present under normal operating conditions.

--- QUESTION 0898 ---
How is Zone 2 defined in the grading of hazardous areas at a terminal?
--- ANSWER ---
An area in which the presence of a flammable gas mixture is unlikely, but if it is present, it will only be for a short period.

--- QUESTION 0899 ---
What component serves as the principal barrier preventing the backflow of flammable cargo gases from the cargo tanks to the inert gas plant?
--- ANSWER ---
The deck water seal.

--- QUESTION 0900 ---
In an Inert Gas System, what are the primary instruments used to control excess pressure and prevent a vacuum, and how do they differ in application?
--- ANSWER ---
The P/V Breaker and P/V Valves. The P/V Breaker utilizes water and is common to the entire system, while P/V Valves are fitted to individual tanks.

--- QUESTION 0901 ---
What is Crude Oil Washing (COW)?
--- ANSWER ---
A process carried out during discharge where part of the crude oil cargo is used to wash the cargo tanks to re-suspend settled sediments.

--- QUESTION 0902 ---
What are the primary structural and system safety prerequisites for a vessel to execute Crude Oil Washing (COW)?
--- ANSWER ---
The vessel must be fitted with a fixed washing system and an operational Inert Gas System.

--- QUESTION 0903 ---
Which cargo tanks must be selected for crude oil washing during a discharge operation?
--- ANSWER ---
All tanks intended to be used for clean ballast, plus an additional 25% of the remaining cargo tanks on a rotational basis.

--- QUESTION 0904 ---
What is the "Marpol line" on an oil tanker, and what is its purpose?
--- ANSWER ---
It is a small bore pipe connected to the outboard side of the manifold valve. It is used in conjunction with a stripping pump to discharge residual cargo line drainings.

--- QUESTION 0905 ---
Which portable gas detection instrument is designed to detect hydrocarbons specifically in an oxygen-deficient or inerted atmosphere?
--- ANSWER ---
The Tankscope.

--- QUESTION 0906 ---
Which portable gas detection instrument uses the Wheatstone bridge principle to detect hydrocarbons, requiring a standard oxygenated environment to function properly?
--- ANSWER ---
The Explosimeter.

--- QUESTION 0907 ---
According to the safety regulations, what three distinct characteristics define an enclosed space?
--- ANSWER ---
It has limited openings for entry and exit, has inadequate ventilation, and is not designed for continuous worker occupation.

--- QUESTION 0908 ---
Which types of NAVTEX messages are mandatory and cannot be rejected by the receiver?
--- ANSWER ---
Navigational Warnings, Meteorological Warnings, and Search and Rescue Information.

--- QUESTION 0909 ---
According to SOLAS V/31, what four specific dangerous meteorological conditions require the Master to transmit a danger message?
--- ANSWER ---
Dangerous ice, tropical storms, sub-freezing air temperatures associated with gale-force winds causing severe ice accretion on superstructures, and winds of Force 10 or above on the Beaufort scale (if no storm warning has been received).

--- QUESTION 0910 ---
To whom must a Master communicate a danger message regarding hazardous weather or ice conditions under SOLAS regulations?
--- ANSWER ---
To ships in the vicinity and to the competent shore authorities, using all means at the Master's disposal.

--- QUESTION 0911 ---
According to SOLAS V/32, what are the minimum details that must be included when transmitting a danger message for wind, sea state, or ice accretion?
--- ANSWER ---
The position (where), the time (when, stating if UTC or another specified time), and the specific meteorological observations (what) such as wind, sea state, pressure/tendency, temperature, ice type, age, extent, or accretion details.

--- QUESTION 0912 ---
Under SOLAS V Regulation 5, what specific weather hazards must contracting governments warn ships about?
--- ANSWER ---
Gales, storms, and cyclones.

--- QUESTION 0913 ---
How often are Contracting Governments required by SOLAS V Regulation 5 to broadcast marine weather information, and what must it contain?
--- ANSWER ---
At least twice a day via terrestrial and space radiocommunication services. The broadcasts must contain data, analyses, warnings, and forecasts of weather and ice.

--- QUESTION 0914 ---
At what minimum wind speed or Beaufort scale force does SOLAS V encourage Masters to inform nearby ships and shore stations of their experienced conditions?
--- ANSWER ---
A wind speed of 50 knots or more, which corresponds to Force 10 on the Beaufort Scale.

--- QUESTION 0915 ---
What are the primary differences in internal components and construction between a standard marine barometer and a Precision Aneroid Barometer (PAB)?
--- ANSWER ---
A normal barometer has a single aneroid capsule, whereas a Precision Aneroid Barometer (PAB) contains at least three aneroid capsules that contract and expand with atmospheric pressure.

--- QUESTION 0916 ---
What are the operational steps required to properly read a Precision Aneroid Barometer (PAB)?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Fit the dampener to reduce breezes.
2. Press and hold the button while turning the dial.
3. Watch for a break in the magic eye (line).
4. Note the reading at the break in the line.
5. Add the height of eye error correction.

--- QUESTION 0917 ---
What is the meteorological purpose of a barograph on a ship's bridge, and what is its standard measurement range?
--- ANSWER ---
A barograph provides a continuous recording of pressure tendency (change) over time. Its standard range is 950 mb to 1050 mb.

--- QUESTION 0918 ---
What are the four main components of a standard marine barograph?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Aneroid capsules
2. Stylus (pen arm)
3. Paper graph (drum)
4. Winding mechanism

--- QUESTION 0919 ---
What routine maintenance steps must be performed to ensure a marine barograph operates correctly?
--- ANSWER ---
The winding mechanism must be wound regularly (taking care not to overwind) and the graph paper chart must be changed weekly.

--- QUESTION 0920 ---
How is true wind speed and direction mathematically or graphically derived from an anemometer reading on a moving vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
An anemometer provides the apparent (relative) wind speed and direction (vector OA). By applying the ship's course and speed vector (vector WO), the true wind speed and direction vector (vector WA) can be found.

--- QUESTION 0921 ---
What physical phenomenon affects the accuracy of marine anemometer readings due to its location on a ship?
--- ANSWER ---
Turbulence created by the structure of the ship surrounding the instrument.

--- QUESTION 0922 ---
According to the WMO Beaufort Scale, what are the sea surface characteristics of a Force 5 (Fresh Breeze) compared to a Force 8 (Gale)?
--- ANSWER ---
Force 5 creates moderate waves (4–8 ft) taking longer form, with many whitecaps and some spray. Force 8 creates moderately high waves (18–25 ft) of greater length, where the edges of crests break into spindrift and foam is blown in well-defined streaks.

--- QUESTION 0923 ---
How is advection (sea) fog formed over marine environments?
--- ANSWER ---
Advection fog is formed when warm, moist air flows over a relatively cold sea surface, cooling the air down to its dew point temperature.

--- QUESTION 0924 ---
How does radiation fog form, and how far offshore can it typically drift?
--- ANSWER ---
It forms over low-lying land on clear nights due to radiation cooling of the earth. It can then drift offshore into coastal areas, but is usually found no further than 10 to 15 miles off the coast.

--- QUESTION 0925 ---
During which periods of the day is radiation fog typically at its thickest?
--- ANSWER ---
During the latter part of the night and the early part of the day.

--- QUESTION 0926 ---
What causes steam fog (also known as sea smoke)?
--- ANSWER ---
Steam fog forms when cold air passes over a relatively warm sea surface; evaporation from the water surface rises into the cold air and quickly cools to its dew point, forming steam.

--- QUESTION 0927 ---
Where does frontal fog typically form, and what causes it?
--- ANSWER ---
Frontal fog forms on a warm front or occlusion within a narrow belt near the frontal boundary. It is caused by the mixing of warm and cold air on the two sides of the frontal zone.

--- QUESTION 0928 ---
What is Buys Ballot's Law, and how is it used by a mariner to locate a low-pressure system?
--- ANSWER ---
Buys Ballot's Law states that if an observer stands with their back to the wind, the lower pressure is on their left in the Northern Hemisphere, and on their right in the Southern Hemisphere. Conversely, if facing the wind, low pressure is to the right in the Northern Hemisphere and to the left in the Southern Hemisphere.

--- QUESTION 0929 ---
What are the primary components contained inside a standard Stevenson Screen used on a ship?
--- ANSWER ---
A Mason's Hygrometer, which consists of a dry-bulb thermometer, a wet-bulb thermometer, a muslin wick, and a distilled water reservoir.

--- QUESTION 0930 ---
Where should a Stevenson Screen be mounted on a vessel to ensure an accurate atmospheric reading?
--- ANSWER ---
It must be hung to the windward side of the vessel to prevent heat from the ship affecting the thermometer readings.

--- QUESTION 0931 ---
According to NP 100 (The Mariner's Handbook), how can a watch officer graphically estimate if advection sea fog is likely to form?
--- ANSWER ---
By measuring the wet and dry bulb temperatures every 10 minutes, calculating the dew point, and plotting both the sea surface temperature and dew point temperature over time. If the two curves converge, fog can be expected where they coincide.

--- QUESTION 0932 ---
What atmospheric conditions and cloud types are associated with air blowing from a colder region over warmer water?
--- ANSWER ---
An unstable atmosphere, cumulus (Cu) clouds, showers (or snow showers in winter), and generally good visibility outside of the showers.

--- QUESTION 0933 ---
What atmospheric conditions and cloud types are associated with air blowing from a warmer region over colder water?
--- ANSWER ---
A stable atmosphere, stratus (St) clouds, continuous rain or drizzle, and poor visibility.

--- QUESTION 0934 ---
What are the defining characteristics of an anticyclone (high-pressure system) on a synoptic chart?
--- ANSWER ---
It features a slight pressure gradient with light winds near the center, is usually slow-moving or stationary for long periods, intensifies as internal pressure rises, and declines when internal pressure falls.

--- QUESTION 0935 ---
What is a Col in meteorology, and what type of weather is typically associated with it?
--- ANSWER ---
A Col is an atmospheric pressure distribution located between two high and two low pressure systems arranged alternately. It is associated with light variable winds, thunderstorms in the summer, and dull or foggy conditions in the winter.

--- QUESTION 0936 ---
What specific marine weather publications and charts should be referenced during voyage planning under SOLAS V/34?
--- ANSWER ---
The Mariner's Handbook (NP 100), Admiralty Sailing Directions (ASD), Admiralty Ocean Passages for the World, and Admiralty Routeing Charts.

--- QUESTION 0937 ---
What meteorological data can be extracted from an Admiralty Routeing Chart to assist in voyage planning?
--- ANSWER ---
Wind roses (showing wind speed and direction), seasonal weather averages (pressure, waves, temperatures), frequency and intensity of storms, low-pressure paths, tropical storm tracks, ocean currents, sea/air temperatures, ice limits, and recommended routes.

--- QUESTION 0938 ---
What specific Admiralty List of Radio Signals (ALRS) volumes cover maritime safety broadcasts and meteorological observation stations?
--- ANSWER ---
Volume 3 covers the Maritime Safety Information Service, NAVTEX, SafetyNET, and Radio-Facsimile frequencies. Volume 4 lists global Meteorological Observation Stations.

--- QUESTION 0939 ---
What primary weather hazards must a vessel plan for when transiting from Southampton to Boston in February?
--- ANSWER ---
Frequent and deep North Atlantic depressions, severe sea states with long fetches and large swells, freezing air temperatures causing ice accretion, and potential sea ice or icebergs west of 40°W and north of 40°N.

--- QUESTION 0940 ---
What meteorological risks dominate a transatlantic passage from the UK to Houston, USA during the month of September?
--- ANSWER ---
Peak hurricane season in the western Atlantic and Gulf of Mexico, presenting a high risk of encountering tropical cyclones, severe squalls, rough seas, and heavily restricted visibility.

--- QUESTION 0941 ---
What is the minimum sea water temperature required for a Tropical Revolving Storm (TRS) to form?
--- ANSWER ---
26.5°C

--- QUESTION 0942 ---
What is the minimum depth of water typically required for the formation of a Tropical Revolving Storm (TRS)?
--- ANSWER ---
50 meters

--- QUESTION 0943 ---
Between which latitudes do Tropical Revolving Storms (TRS) usually form north and south of the equator?
--- ANSWER ---
Between 5 and 20 degrees North and South.

--- QUESTION 0944 ---
Which force, responsible for the rotational movement of a TRS, is insufficient at the equator, causing these storms to form typically beyond 5 degrees latitude?
--- ANSWER ---
The Coriolis force.

--- QUESTION 0945 ---
Name three examples of pre-existing low-pressure areas mentioned in the text that can initiate a Tropical Revolving Storm.
--- ANSWER ---
An island, the ITCZ (Intertropical Convergence Zone), or an Easterly Wave.

--- QUESTION 0946 ---
Regarding vertical wind conditions, what must be absent in the upper atmosphere to allow a Tropical Revolving Storm to form?
--- ANSWER ---
Vertical wind shear.

--- QUESTION 0947 ---
How does a Tropical Revolving Storm (TRS) compare in size and intensity to a temperate latitude depression?
--- ANSWER ---
It is smaller in size but usually of far greater intensity.

--- QUESTION 0948 ---
How do the pressure gradients and isobar shapes of a Tropical Revolving Storm (TRS) differ from a typical temperate depression?
--- ANSWER ---
The pressure gradient is very steep, and the isobars are nearer to true circles with no fronts.

--- QUESTION 0949 ---
An Officer of the Watch (OOW) monitoring the barometer should suspect an approaching TRS if the pressure drops how far below the mean daily reading?
--- ANSWER ---
3 millibars (mb) below the mean daily or diurnal reading.

--- QUESTION 0950 ---
What type of cloud pattern is described as an early warning sign of an approaching TRS, and what cloud band follows it?
--- ANSWER ---
Spiralling cirrus clouds, followed by Cumulonimbus (Cb) bands.

--- QUESTION 0951 ---
List four atmospheric or sea state signs that indicate an approaching Tropical Revolving Storm, excluding barometric changes and cloud patterns.
--- ANSWER ---

1. Significant change in the direction and/or strength of the Trade Winds.
2. Long low swell.
3. Squally heavy rain.
4. Oppressive atmosphere / ugly lurid skies.

--- QUESTION 0952 ---
In the Northern Hemisphere, if an observer experiences a veering wind (changing clockwise), in which semi-circle of the TRS is the vessel located?
--- ANSWER ---
The dangerous semi-circle.

--- QUESTION 0953 ---
In the Northern Hemisphere, if the wind is backing (changing anti-clockwise), in which semi-circle of the TRS is the vessel located?
--- ANSWER ---
The navigable semi-circle.

--- QUESTION 0954 ---
In both the Northern and Southern Hemispheres, what does a steady wind direction with falling pressure indicate about the vessel's position relative to the TRS?
--- ANSWER ---
The vessel is located directly in the path of the TRS.

--- QUESTION 0955 ---
In the Southern Hemisphere, what semi-circle is a vessel in if the wind direction is veering?
--- ANSWER ---
The navigable semi-circle.

--- QUESTION 0956 ---
In the Southern Hemisphere, what semi-circle is a vessel in if the wind direction is backing?
--- ANSWER ---
The dangerous semi-circle.

--- QUESTION 0957 ---
What is the first step an operator should take to find the likely path of a Tropical Revolving Storm?
--- ANSWER ---
Obtain the bearing of the storm centre.

--- QUESTION 0958 ---
What action should a vessel take if it is caught in the dangerous semi-circle of a TRS in the Northern Hemisphere?
--- ANSWER ---
Put the wind on the starboard bow and alter course to starboard as the wind veers.

--- QUESTION 0959 ---
What action should a vessel take if it is caught in the navigable semi-circle of a TRS in the Northern Hemisphere?
--- ANSWER ---
Put the wind on the starboard quarter and alter course to port as the wind backs.

--- QUESTION 0960 ---
What action should a vessel take if it is caught in the dangerous semi-circle of a TRS in the Southern Hemisphere?
--- ANSWER ---
Put the wind on the port bow and alter course to port as the wind backs.

--- QUESTION 0961 ---
What action should a vessel take if it is caught in the navigable semi-circle of a TRS in the Southern Hemisphere?
--- ANSWER ---
Put the wind on the port quarter and alter course to starboard as the wind veers.

--- QUESTION 0962 ---
List three risks associated with other vessels or structural contact when a ship is remaining in port during a TRS.
--- ANSWER ---

1. Damage to the vessel due to contact with the quay.
2. Damage caused by flying debris.
3. Damage caused by other vessels breaking their moorings and drifting.

--- QUESTION 0963 ---
What precautionary steps should be taken regarding a vessel's mooring arrangement and propulsion status when remaining at a berth during a TRS?
--- ANSWER ---
Double up mooring lines, drop anchors on a short stay, and keep engines on standby.

--- QUESTION 0964 ---
Why might a port authority force a vessel to leave a berth unexpectedly when a TRS approaches?
--- ANSWER ---
The port may close and request vessels to sail at short notice.

--- QUESTION 0965 ---
What danger does a storm surge present to a vessel berthed in a shallow port during a TRS?
--- ANSWER ---
It can cause the vessel to make contact with the sea bed or face extreme draft changes.

--- QUESTION 0966 ---
What is the primary advantage of choosing to encounter a TRS at sea rather than in port?
--- ANSWER ---
Freedom of movement, meaning the vessel can take avoiding action.

--- QUESTION 0967 ---
List four distinct disadvantages or operational risks for a vessel handling a TRS at sea.
--- ANSWER ---

1. Very small chance of receiving assistance in an emergency.
2. Possibility of weather damage to the vessel.
3. Crew discomfort leading to fatigue.
4. Risk of losing or damaging deck cargo.

--- QUESTION 0968 ---
What specific asset must be closely monitored and managed to ensure a vessel at sea can successfully maneuver around a TRS over an extended period?
--- ANSWER ---
Fuel reserves.

--- QUESTION 0969 ---
What letters represent total concentration, partial concentration, stage of development, and form of ice within the standard international ice egg code?
--- ANSWER ---
C (Total concentration), Ca/Cb/Cc (Partial concentration), Sa/Sb/Sc (Stage of development), and Fa/Fb/Fc (Form of ice).

--- QUESTION 0970 ---
Which three letters are utilized in Canadian practice as minor additions to the egg code to report additional ice classes during freeze-up and break-up?
--- ANSWER ---
Cd, Se, and Fe.

--- QUESTION 0971 ---
According to the WMO colour code for ice concentration, what do the concentrations '1-3/10', '4-6/10', and '9-10/10' represent?
--- ANSWER ---
1-3/10 represents Very Open Drift / Open Ice, 4-6/10 represents Open Ice / Pack Ice, and 9-10/10 represents Very Close Pack Ice.

--- QUESTION 0972 ---
Which type of high-resolution satellite imagery uses pixels measuring 3 meters by 3 meters for inshore and nearshore tactical ice navigation?
--- ANSWER ---
CosmoSkyMed Himage.

--- QUESTION 0973 ---
What are the pixel dimensions of the Radarsat-2 WideFine and the Radarsat-2 ScanSAR Wide sensors used for ice monitoring?
--- ANSWER ---
Radarsat-2 WideFine uses 6.25m x 6.25m pixels (inshore/nearshore); Radarsat-2 ScanSAR Wide uses 50m x 50m pixels (offshore).

--- QUESTION 0974 ---
When an examiner asks about the weather in a specific location, what six key factors are expected in the interpretation?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Wind direction and strength.
2. Weather (particularly precipitation).
3. Cloud type.
4. Anticipated change in wind direction.
5. Anticipated change in temperature.
6. Anticipated change in pressure.

--- QUESTION 0975 ---
What are the three operational categories used by ice information services to classify user requirements for risk management?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Operations in ice.
2. Operations near ice edge.
3. Operations far from ice edge.

--- QUESTION 0976 ---
For each operational category of ice management, what three temporal types of information portfolios must national services provide?
--- ANSWER ---
Historical information (for journey planning), Current information (for tactical navigation), and Forecast information (for navigation).

--- QUESTION 0977 ---
According to the meteorological data, what wind speed threshold defines a "Gale Warning" on the Beaufort Scale?
--- ANSWER ---
Force 8.

--- QUESTION 0978 ---
In a standard Navtex marine weather warning, what transmission marker indicator text opens the message format, and what marker closes it?
--- ANSWER ---
It opens with ZCZC and closes with NNNN.

--- QUESTION 0979 ---
According to Britton v. U.S. Great Lakes Fleet, what must an employer show regarding non-disclosed pre-employment medical information to successfully defend against a maintenance and cure claim?
--- ANSWER ---
The employer must show that the non-disclosed medical information was material to its decision to hire the seaman.

--- QUESTION 0980 ---
In the case Britton v. U.S. Great Lakes Fleet, why did the plaintiff allege that the vessel was unseaworthy?
--- ANSWER ---
The plaintiff alleged there was a shortage of crew available to open the stairwell covers and vent hatches, forcing him to perform the tasks alone and resulting in a back injury.

--- QUESTION 0981 ---
Under the Jones Act section of Britton v. U.S. Great Lakes Fleet, what specific action by the owner's assigned health care provider was deemed a sufficient allegation of negligence to go to a jury?
--- ANSWER ---
Failing to exercise due care when reassigning the plaintiff back to work.

--- QUESTION 0982 ---
In Arkansas State Highways Commission v. Arkansas River Co., why was the barge deemed unseaworthy on delivery?
--- ANSWER ---
It was tendered to the tug's captain with a boom that was unobviously raised such that it could not pass safely under a Mississippi River bridge.

--- QUESTION 0983 ---
What percentage of liability for resulting damages was assigned to the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers in the Arkansas State Highways Commission v. Arkansas River Co. case?
--- ANSWER ---
100%

--- QUESTION 0984 ---
According to early maritime history, the warranty of seaworthiness was first applied to seamen by implication in what types of legal cases?
--- ANSWER ---
Wage forfeiture cases for abandonment of the ship before the completion of the voyage.

--- QUESTION 0985 ---
In the early case of Dixon v. The Cyrus, why did the crew abandon the vessel after reaching port?
--- ANSWER ---
The owner/master failed to provide meals after the ship had reached port.

--- QUESTION 0986 ---
According to the court in Dixon v. The Cyrus, what two elements are required to fulfill the implicit obligation of providing a seaworthy vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
The customary requisites for navigation and adequate provisions.

--- QUESTION 0987 ---
On which international rules is the UK Carriage of Goods by Sea Act (COGSA) 1971 based?
--- ANSWER ---
The Hague-Visby Rules.

--- QUESTION 0988 ---
Why do P&I Clubs typically prefer the Hague or Hague-Visby Rules over the Hamburg Rules?
--- ANSWER ---
Because the Hamburg Rules typically give too much favor towards the Shippers.

--- QUESTION 0989 ---
Under the Hague Rules, under what condition can live animals and deck cargo be loaded?
--- ANSWER ---
If it is a normal part of the trade.

--- QUESTION 0990 ---
How is deck cargo carried under the Hague-Visby Rules?
--- ANSWER ---
It is carried at the Shipper's risk, and this must be stated on the Bill of Lading (B/L).

--- QUESTION 0991 ---
What is the legal principle where all stakeholders in a sea venture proportionately share any losses resulting from a voluntary sacrifice of part of the ship or cargo to save the whole in an emergency?
--- ANSWER ---
General Average

--- QUESTION 0992 ---
Which set of rules defines a General Average act as an extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure intentionally and reasonably made for common safety?
--- ANSWER ---
The York-Antwerp Rules.

--- QUESTION 0993 ---
What are the five requirements for a General Average act under the York-Antwerp Rules?
--- ANSWER ---
A peril, extraordinary sacrifice, reasonable, voluntary (intentional), and common safety.

--- QUESTION 0994 ---
What is the function of the "New Jason Clause" in a charter party?
--- ANSWER ---
It ensures that General Average still applies in the event of negligence by the Master, Carrier, or Cargo Owner/Shipper.

--- QUESTION 0995 ---
Which three main parties normally contribute to General Average?
--- ANSWER ---
The Ship Owner, Cargo Owners/Voyage Charterer, and Time Charterer.

--- QUESTION 0996 ---
Under what circumstance would a Time Charterer contribute to General Average even if they do not own or hold liability for the bunkers?
--- ANSWER ---
When there are outstanding freight payments, as that could be considered a risk/liability.

--- QUESTION 0997 ---
What is the specific role of an Average Adjuster in maritime law?
--- ANSWER ---
It is the company that determines who will pay what in a General Average claim.

--- QUESTION 0998 ---
What is the difference between an Average Bond and an Average Guarantee?
--- ANSWER ---
An Average Bond is the agreement each party makes guaranteeing to meet any GA claims, whereas an Average Guarantee is an Underwriter's guarantee to pay the GA claims.

--- QUESTION 0999 ---
Why is a direct Act of Piracy not claimable under General Average?
--- ANSWER ---
Because it is deemed a robbery at sea under force or threat of force, meaning the act is not voluntary or reasonable.

--- QUESTION 1000 ---
Why is a ransom payment to pirates claimable under General Average?
--- ANSWER ---
It is voluntary, extraordinary, for the common safety of the maritime adventure, and reasonable (as far as possible) to minimize loss.

--- QUESTION 1001 ---
What does the Charter Party term "Safe Port" mean regarding navigational risks?
--- ANSWER ---
The port is navigable and does not present risks in terms of sandbanks, shallows, obstructions, weather conditions, political situations/war, or outbreaks of epidemics.

--- QUESTION 1002 ---
According to the case The Eastern City (1958), what criteria must a port or berth meet to be considered safe?
--- ANSWER ---
In the relevant period of time, the particular ship must be able to reach it, use it, and return from it without, in the absence of some abnormal occurrence, being exposed to danger which cannot be avoided by good navigation and seamanship.

--- QUESTION 1003 ---
According to the text, what is a "Place of Refuge"?
--- ANSWER ---
A place where a ship in need of assistance can take action to stabilize its condition, reduce hazards to navigation, and protect human life and the environment, where the safety of life is not involved.

--- QUESTION 1004 ---
If the safety of life is actively involved when a ship is in trouble, which convention's provisions should be followed instead of utilizing a Place of Refuge?
--- ANSWER ---
The SAR Convention (Search and Rescue Convention).

--- QUESTION 1005 ---
According to the SAR Convention 1979, how is a "Place of Safety" defined?
--- ANSWER ---
A location where rescue operations are considered to terminate, where survivors' safety of life is no longer threatened, and where their basic human needs (food, shelter, medical needs) can be met.

--- QUESTION 1006 ---
How does the Lloyds Open Form (LOF) define a "Place of Safety" for a salvaged vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
A place at which a vessel being salvaged can be returned to a reasonably safe condition such that her master and crew are capable of discharging responsibility for her safety and no further salvage assistance is necessary.

--- QUESTION 1007 ---
What does the maritime abbreviation NAABSA stand for?
--- ANSWER ---
Not always afloat but safely aground.

--- QUESTION 1008 ---
What type of seabed is required at a NAABSA port?
--- ANSWER ---
A seabed suitable for the vessel to rest at low tide without damage to its hull.

--- QUESTION 1009 ---
If a vessel's hull is damaged while resting at a NAABSA berth, what legal argument can the shipowner make against the charterers?
--- ANSWER ---
That the charterers breached their obligation to nominate a safe port/berth where the vessel can lie safely aground.

--- QUESTION 1010 ---
What does the abbreviation MAS stand for in coastal state maritime operations?
--- ANSWER ---
Maritime Assistance Service

--- QUESTION 1011 ---
According to the IMO, under what specific conditions are coastal States obliged to establish a Maritime Assistance Service (MAS)?
--- ANSWER ---
To handle events on ships that are in difficulty and where environmental harm is imminent or has already occurred, but where no danger to human life is present.

--- QUESTION 1012 ---
What are the four main tasks of a Maritime Assistance Service (MAS)?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Receive reports from ships in need of assistance; 2. Monitor the ship's situation; 3. Act as a point of contact between the ship and coastal authorities; 4. Act as a point of contact between interacting partners at sea and the coastal state.

--- QUESTION 1013 ---
If persons on board a vessel find themselves in immediate distress, which entity has operational priority over the MAS?
--- ANSWER ---
The MRCC (Maritime Rescue Coordination Centre).

--- QUESTION 1014 ---
How does Section 39 of the Marine Insurance Act 1906 define the warranty of seaworthiness?
--- ANSWER ---
A ship is deemed to be seaworthy when she is reasonably fit in all respects to encounter the ordinary perils of the seas of the adventure insured.

--- QUESTION 1015 ---
According to Section 42 of the Merchant Shipping Act 1995, what is the core obligation of the Master and Owner regarding seaworthiness?
--- ANSWER ---
To use all reasonable means to ensure the seaworthiness of the ship for the voyage at the time when the voyage commences and to keep the ship in a seaworthy condition during the voyage.

--- QUESTION 1016 ---
What document-specific errors led the UK Supreme Court to uphold an unseaworthy judgment in the CMA CGM Libra case study?
--- ANSWER ---
Errors in passage planning.

--- QUESTION 1017 ---
In Folger Coffee Co. v. Olivebank, why did the cargo interests fail to establish a defense against the vessel's general average claim?
--- ANSWER ---
The vessel was ruled seaworthy under COGSA at the commencement of the voyage.

--- QUESTION 1018 ---
In the Folger Coffee Co. v. Olivebank case, why was an open skylight and vent covers that allowed seawater to fail the emergency electrical system not classified as unseaworthiness?
--- ANSWER ---
It was deemed an error in the management of the ship, not unseaworthiness.

--- QUESTION 1019 ---
What are the three main types of Charter Parties listed in the Commercial Week 4 materials?
--- ANSWER ---
Voyage (Gencon), Time (Gentime), and Bareboat (Barecon).

--- QUESTION 1020 ---
Name at least five common clauses found within a Voyage Charter Party as listed in the text.
--- ANSWER ---
Any five of: Masters, Dispute, Arbitration, Ice, Liberty and Deviation, Laytime/Laycan/Laydays, Bills of Lading, Letters of credit, Lien, Taxes and dues, or General average.

--- QUESTION 1021 ---
According to the SOLAS Chapter II-2 regulation on fire prevention and suppression, what specific structural objective must be maintained to prevent the partial or whole collapse of the ship due to heat?
--- ANSWER ---
The structural integrity of the ship must be maintained, preventing collapse due to strength deterioration caused by heat.

--- QUESTION 1022 ---
How many minutes must an A Class Division division be able to prevent the passage of smoke and flame during a standard fire test?
--- ANSWER ---
60 minutes

--- QUESTION 1023 ---
During a fire test, what is the maximum temperature rise permitted on the unexposed side of an A Class or B Class boundary at any point?
--- ANSWER ---
180 degrees C

--- QUESTION 1024 ---
What is the primary material requirement for the construction of A Class and B Class Divisions?
--- ANSWER ---
Steel or equivalent material

--- QUESTION 1025 ---
Which class of division is constructed of approved non-combustible materials but is not required to meet any requirements regarding the rise in temperature or the passage of smoke and flame?
--- ANSWER ---
C Class Division

--- QUESTION 1026 ---
What is the maximum time allowed for an emergency generator to start automatically?
--- ANSWER ---
45 seconds

--- QUESTION 1027 ---
What are the minimum durations that an emergency generator must provide power on cargo ships and passenger ships respectively?
--- ANSWER ---
A minimum of 18 hours on cargo ships and a minimum of 36 hours on passenger ships.

--- QUESTION 1028 ---
Name four of the critical systems or areas that must be powered by the emergency generator.
--- ANSWER ---
Any four of the following: Emergency Lighting; Navigation lights, Navigation equipment, and Sound signaling appliances; GMDSS; General Alarm System and PA System; Emergency fire pump; Emergency Steering system; Watertight doors; Fire detection and fire alarm system.

--- QUESTION 1029 ---
According to SOLAS Chapter II-2 Regulation 13, how many means of escape must be provided from each machinery space of category A on ships over 1000 GT?
--- ANSWER ---
Two means of escape

--- QUESTION 1030 ---
What is the minimum air supply duration required for Emergency Escape Breathing Devices (EEBDs) located within machinery spaces?
--- ANSWER ---
10 minutes

--- QUESTION 1031 ---
Where can a fixed CO2 fire extinguishing system be operated from on board a vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
Remotely or from the CO2 bottle room.

--- QUESTION 1032 ---
What two physical indicators should be checked in the CO2 bottle room to verify that CO2 gas has been successfully released?
--- ANSWER ---
Check that the appropriate control valve has been activated and the manifold gas pressure gauge has dropped to zero, and check for frosting of the bottles and manifold pipes.

--- QUESTION 1033 ---
What are the Master's three overall structural priorities in an emergency situation?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Protection of life
2. Protection of the environment
3. Protection of the ship

--- QUESTION 1034 ---
Name three specific items of certification or documentation required onboard regarding Life-Saving Appliances (LSA) and Fire-Fighting Appliances (FFA).
--- ANSWER ---
Any three of the following: Passenger ship safety certificate / Cargo ship safety equipment certificate; Damage control plans and booklets; Fire safety training manual; Fire control plan / booklet; Onboard training and drills record; Fire safety operational booklet.

--- QUESTION 1035 ---
What are the Master's 5 safety objectives in the event of a fire?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Prevent the occurrence of fire and explosion.
2. Reduce the risk to life caused by fire.
3. Reduce the risk of damage caused by fire to the ship, its cargo, and the environment.
4. Contain, control and suppress fire and explosion in the compartment of origin.
5. Provide adequate and readily accessible means of escape for passengers and crew.

--- QUESTION 1036 ---
What is the standard configuration requirement for hydrants to ensure sufficient water spray coverage across the vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
The number and position of hydrants should allow two jets of water from different hydrants to reach any part of the ship normally accessible to passengers or crew.

--- QUESTION 1037 ---
What are the fire hose length limits for machinery spaces, other spaces/open decks, and ships with a breadth exceeding 30 meters?
--- ANSWER ---
Not more than 15 meters in machinery spaces, not more than 20 meters in other spaces and open decks, and not more than 25 meters on ships with a breadth in excess of 30 meters.

--- QUESTION 1038 ---
What specific safety feature must be fitted at intervals of not more than 40 meters on the fire main at the poop front and main deck of tankers?
--- ANSWER ---
Isolating valves and relief valves (to preserve the integrity of the fire main in the event of a fire or explosion).

--- QUESTION 1039 ---
What is the minimum number of portable fire extinguishers required in the accommodation, service spaces, and control stations of ships of 1000 GT and upwards?
--- ANSWER ---
At least 5 portable fire extinguishers

--- QUESTION 1040 ---
What is the required spare charge capacity for the first 10 portable fire extinguishers onboard, and what is the requirement for the remaining extinguishers?
--- ANSWER ---
100% spare charges for the first 10 extinguishers, and 50% for the remaining extinguishers.

--- QUESTION 1041 ---
What fixed system requirements apply to passenger ships over 500 GT and cargo ships over 2000 GT to protect machinery spaces without requiring engine shutdown or evacuation?
--- ANSWER ---
An approved fixed local application water-based fire-extinguishing system with automatic and manual release capabilities.

--- QUESTION 1042 ---
What emergency fire-fighting materials or equipment must be placed in each firing space of an oil-fired boiler room?
--- ANSWER ---
A receptacle containing sand and soda with a shovel, or an approved portable extinguisher.

--- QUESTION 1043 ---
What are the two immediate priorities for the Master upon confirming an oil spill in port?
--- ANSWER ---
Priority 1: Prevent and restrict the spill.
Priority 2: Prevent secondary incidents (e.g., injuries and explosions).

--- QUESTION 1044 ---
Which external organization has the authority to approve or refuse the use of dispersants during an oil spill response?
--- ANSWER ---
The Marine Management Organisation (MMO)

--- QUESTION 1045 ---
What acronym describes the emergency response plan that must be initiated immediately by the ship's crew in the event of an oil pollution incident?
--- ANSWER ---
SOPEP (Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan)

--- QUESTION 1046 ---
What actions should the engine room take immediately to halt and contain an active oil leak during bunkering?
--- ANSWER ---
Stop operations using emergency and remote stops, depressurize leaking tanks and pipelines, close up any leaks, and assess the transfer of oil from leaking tanks into available tanks or spaces.

--- QUESTION 1047 ---
What is the primary technical objective of the emergency teams during an initial grounding response?
--- ANSWER ---
To assess the damage to the vessel and the watertight integrity of the hull.

--- QUESTION 1048 ---
What navigation parameters must be assessed during a grounding incident to plan a safe recovery?
--- ANSWER ---
Vessel position, nature of the seabed, tides, weather, and proximity to navigational hazards.

--- QUESTION 1049 ---
What vessel maneuver and heading adjustment are recommended when conducting helicopter winching operations with the helicopter approaching from the stern?
--- ANSWER ---
Maintain a constant speed with the wind 30 degrees on the port bow.

--- QUESTION 1050 ---
According to MGN 325 and IAMSAR recommendations, what is the length threshold for sea-going ships to provide a dedicated winching area?
--- ANSWER ---
100 meters in length and over

--- QUESTION 1051 ---
List four fire safety principles utilized on ferries for the confinement and ventilation of vehicle deck fires.
--- ANSWER ---
Any four of the following: Division of the ship into vertical and horizontal zones by thermal and structural boundaries; Separation of the accommodation from the remainder of the ship; Restricted use of combustible material; Automatic fire detection; Fire containment and extinction in the space of origin; Protecting means of escape and firefighting access; Availability of fire extinguishing appliances; Minimizing ignition sources.

--- QUESTION 1052 ---
What potential hazard is indicated if a vessel's GPS position and AIS position display contradictory or incorrect data simultaneously while at sea?
--- ANSWER ---
Potential GPS Jamming

--- QUESTION 1053 ---
What details must be logged by the watch officer when an anomalous GPS event or malfunction occurs?
--- ANSWER ---
Note the date, time, and duration of the event.

--- QUESTION 1054 ---
What emergency action should be taken regarding the ship's watertight doors (WTDs) immediately following a collision or serious grounding?
--- ANSWER ---
Close all watertight doors (WTDs).

--- QUESTION 1055 ---
What structural check must be performed by emergency response teams to determine if a vessel can remain afloat following an impact?
--- ANSWER ---
An assessment of watertight integrity, hull breaching (above or below the waterline), and structural arrangements to determine if the vessel is taking in water.

--- QUESTION 1056 ---
In the event of a collision, what three items must be changed or displayed on the bridge regarding the vessel's navigation status and day shapes?
--- ANSWER ---
The Navigation lights must be changed to Not Under Command (NUC), the AIS status must be updated to NUC, and the appropriate day shapes must be hoisted.

--- QUESTION 1057 ---
What specific action should the Engine Room Party take regarding the suction intakes during a vessel grounding?
--- ANSWER ---
They should change from low to high suction intakes.

--- QUESTION 1058 ---
According to the text, what are the three overarching priority categories to establish during a maritime emergency such as a collision, fire, or grounding?
--- ANSWER ---
Safety of the crew, safety of the ship, and protection of the marine environment.

--- QUESTION 1059 ---
Which specific emergency piece of equipment is mentioned for preventing the leakage of oil from pipes during an oil spill response?
--- ANSWER ---
Smith Blair Clamps (or emergency pipe clamping arrangements).

--- QUESTION 1060 ---
What adjustments should be made to the vessel's positioning or stability to manage an on-deck oil spill effectively?
--- ANSWER ---
Adjust the trim to direct the oil into the dump valves leading to the slop tank, and drain lines to any empty tanks.

--- QUESTION 1061 ---
When a gyro failure occurs, what immediate manual navigation step should be taken by the helmsman or OOW?
--- ANSWER ---
Engage hand steering using the magnetic compass.

--- QUESTION 1062 ---
How is ARPA target data affected when a vessel experiences a total gyro failure?
--- ANSWER ---
The ARPA will switch to a head-up unstabilised mode, making target data unreliable, though CPA and TCPA remain accurate.

--- QUESTION 1063 ---
To what status should the navigation lights and AIS be changed if the vessel suffers a total steering gear failure?
--- ANSWER ---
Not Under Command (NUC).

--- QUESTION 1064 ---
What action is the Chief Officer or Deck Party responsible for immediately preparing if the vessel suffers a steering gear failure near shallow waters?
--- ANSWER ---
Assembling the anchor party and preparing for emergency anchoring.

--- QUESTION 1065 ---
What are the initial actions the Engine Room Party must take under the leadership of the Chief Engineer in the event of a fire?
--- ANSWER ---
Stop the ventilation and cut off fuel supplies.

--- QUESTION 1066 ---
What specific safety briefing and structural check requirements must be completed before the SCBA fire-fighting team enters a burning space?
--- ANSWER ---
Communications must be tested, a pre-entry briefing conducted, and the team must be informed of escape routes and the location of portable fire extinguishers (PFE) as per the fire plan.

--- QUESTION 1067 ---
What action should the Master instruct the Chief Engineer to take if a fire cannot be extinguished by the manual fire-fighting parties?
--- ANSWER ---
Exit the space and activate the fixed fire fighting system.

--- QUESTION 1068 ---
What specific task is assigned to the Back-Up Party during a fire emergency while the primary team is fighting the fire?
--- ANSWER ---
Prepare 2 persons in SCBA as a back-up, commence boundary cooling, check for hot spots, and remove combustible materials from adjacent spaces.

--- QUESTION 1069 ---
What primary documentation and tool should the Deck Emergency Party consult during a grounding to determine the viability of re-floating the vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
The damage stability booklet, plans, and the stability computer (to evaluate emergency ballasting or de-ballasting).

--- QUESTION 1070 ---
What navigational details must the bridge team determine from the chart and local data immediately following a vessel grounding?
--- ANSWER ---
Identify the nature of the seabed, determine tidal conditions, and find the time of the next high water (HW).

--- QUESTION 1071 ---
In the event of a collision, what are the immediate initial actions that must be taken on the bridge?
--- ANSWER ---
Immediately stop the engine, sound the general alarm, make a PA announcement, call the Master, and switch on deck lights if it is night time.

--- QUESTION 1072 ---
During a collision emergency, what specific responsibility does the crew have toward the other vessel once it is safe?
--- ANSWER ---
There is an obligation to render assistance, establish contact with the other ship, and offer assistance.

--- QUESTION 1073 ---
What must the Chief Officer do immediately before deploying fixed fire-fighting arrangements?
--- ANSWER ---
Take a head count to ensure no one is present in the space and then report to the bridge.

--- QUESTION 1074 ---
Once a fire has been extinguished, what actions must be assigned and continued by the Chief Officer?
--- ANSWER ---
Assign a crewmember for a fire watch and continue hot spot checks and boundary cooling.

--- QUESTION 1075 ---
In the event of a fire, what specific actions are taken by the Engine Room to stop and control the spread?
--- ANSWER ---
The Engine Room will stop ventilation to the space and cut off fuel to avoid the spread of fire.

--- QUESTION 1076 ---
If a fire is located in the engine room, why is the fire main isolation valve closed?
--- ANSWER ---
It is closed to maintain the pressure in the fire main.

--- QUESTION 1077 ---
What changes must be made to the vessel's navigation lights, day shapes, and AIS status immediately after a grounding?
--- ANSWER ---
Change navigation lights and AIS status to aground, and hoist day shapes.

--- QUESTION 1078 ---
During a grounding response, what environmental factors must be checked or sounded to assess re-floating the vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
Check when the next high tide is and sound around the vessel to determine the nature of the seabed.

--- QUESTION 1079 ---
What operations should be immediately stopped by the Chief Officer in the event of an oil spill?
--- ANSWER ---
Immediately stop any bunkering or cargo operations.

--- QUESTION 1080 ---
What immediate defensive action must the crew take on deck to prevent spilled oil from escaping overboard?
--- ANSWER ---
Instruct a crew member to cross-check that all scuppers are shut tight.

--- QUESTION 1081 ---
What specific items and tools are listed to assist with containment and clean-up operations during an oil spill?
--- ANSWER ---
Smith blair clamps, saw dust, absorbent pads, wilden pump, and other SOPEP equipment.

--- QUESTION 1082 ---
In the event of an oil spill, what fire preparedness steps does the Engine Room party take?
--- ANSWER ---
Start the fire pump and line up the foam fire-fighting system in case of a fire on deck.

--- QUESTION 1083 ---
Following a gyro failure, how is the ARPA system affected, and what data remains correct?
--- ANSWER ---
The ARPA will go into head up unstabilised mode making all target data unreliable, however CPA and TCPA will still be correct.

--- QUESTION 1084 ---
How are the autopilot and ECDIS systems affected by a gyro failure?
--- ANSWER ---
The autopilot will be non-functional, and the ECDIS will lose its heading input and go on alarm.

--- QUESTION 1085 ---
To what specific status should the AIS be changed during a steering gear failure?
--- ANSWER ---
Change the AIS status to NUC (Not Under Command).

--- QUESTION 1086 ---
What immediate manual actions and control mode changes must be executed during a steering gear failure?
--- ANSWER ---
Instruct the bosun and an officer to prepare anchor stations, switch to NFU (Non-Follow-Up) mode, and send a crew member to the emergency steering flat.

--- QUESTION 1087 ---
What is the primary difference in intent between grounding and beaching a vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
Grounding is an unintentional consequence with a risk of damage and pollution, whereas beaching is a planned action taken in an emergency to minimize loss of life, damage to the environment, or when watertight integrity is compromised to carry out emergency repairs.

--- QUESTION 1088 ---
What initial emergency response plan should be consulted and what physical equipment deployed if pollution is detected after grounding?
--- ANSWER ---
The Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan (SOPEP) should be consulted, and oil booms should be deployed as appropriate.

--- QUESTION 1089 ---
Which shore-based authority should be contacted immediately after a grounding to allow them to activate local pollution control?
--- ANSWER ---
The local Coastguard.

--- QUESTION 1090 ---
What three types of radio messages might be transmitted following a grounding, depending on the severity of the situation?
--- ANSWER ---
Mayday, Pan Pan, or Securite.

--- QUESTION 1091 ---
What internal company role must be contacted ashore following a maritime emergency such as a grounding?
--- ANSWER ---
The Designated Person Ashore (DPA).

--- QUESTION 1092 ---
Besides the Coastguard and the DPA, which three external organizations or societies should be notified following a grounding incident?
--- ANSWER ---
The Marine Accident Investigation Branch (MAIB), the Maritime and Coastguard Agency (MCA), and the Classification Society.

--- QUESTION 1093 ---
Where must records of the emergency actions and details be written down onboard?
--- ANSWER ---
In the Deck Log Book and the Official Log Book (OLB).

--- QUESTION 1094 ---
What are the immediate physical actions required for the vessel's propulsion system and hull monitoring following a grounding?
--- ANSWER ---
Stop the engine, sound around the vessel, sound the tanks, and check for water ingress into any compartments.

--- QUESTION 1095 ---
What specialized book and computerized tool should a ship's officer consult to assess stability after structural damage or grounding?
--- ANSWER ---
The Damage Stability Booklet and the damage stability computer program.

--- QUESTION 1096 ---
How does bilging cause a loss of metacentric height (GM) when a vessel runs aground?
--- ANSWER ---
Bilging causes a loss of buoyancy, which results in a loss of GM.

--- QUESTION 1097 ---
If a vessel is aground but its hull is not holed, what physical force causes the loss of stability?
--- ANSWER ---
The upward thrust (P force) exerted by the seabed due to the loss in buoyancy.

--- QUESTION 1098 ---
According to COLREGS, what action must be taken immediately regarding visual or audible indicators after running aground?
--- ANSWER ---
Show vessel aground signals.

--- QUESTION 1099 ---
Which five specific certificates are noted as being affected if a cargo vessel runs aground?
--- ANSWER ---
Cargo Ship Safety Construction, Cargo Ship Safety Equipment, Loadline, International Oil Pollution Prevention (IOPP), and Certificate of Class.

--- QUESTION 1100 ---
What are the three core priorities for a bridge team during a bridge equipment failure emergency?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Safe navigation of the vessel.
2. Maintain collision avoidance and navigation capabilities.
3. Maintain situational awareness.

--- QUESTION 1101 ---
What secondary system should be initiated immediately if bridge equipment suffers a power failure?
--- ANSWER ---
An Uninterruptible Power Supply (UPS) secondary power source.

--- QUESTION 1102 ---
What alternatives are used for position fixing if a vessel experiences a total GNSS failure?
--- ANSWER ---
Dead reckoning, visual fixing methods, and celestial fixing methods.

--- QUESTION 1103 ---
How should a bridge team utilize RADAR to maintain safe coastal navigation if GNSS fails?
--- ANSWER ---
Increase the use of RADAR and employ parallel index lines.

--- QUESTION 1104 ---
Why must the steering mode be changed to manual or hand steering if GNSS inputs fail while in 'Nav' mode?
--- ANSWER ---
The failure of speed and position inputs can result in a miscalculation of waypoints by the autopilot.

--- QUESTION 1105 ---
Why is automatic Automatic Identification System (AIS) data considered degraded and unreliable for collision avoidance during a GNSS failure?
--- ANSWER ---
AIS relies on dynamic data inputs, which are lost or corrupted without constant position updating.

--- QUESTION 1106 ---
How is the gyrocompass display affected during a total GNSS failure?
--- ANSWER ---
It requires manual speed and latitude inputs to prevent errors in calculating speed over ground (SOG) and position.

--- QUESTION 1107 ---
What quick visual indicator system can be used on secondary GNSS sources to check the accuracy of incoming data?
--- ANSWER ---
A 'traffic light system'.

--- QUESTION 1108 ---
Which critical item of navigational equipment determines and transmits a vessel's heading to the RADAR, AIS, ECDIS, and autopilot?
--- ANSWER ---
The gyrocompass.

--- QUESTION 1109 ---
If the gyrocompass fails, what display orientation and vector mode must be applied to the RADAR?
--- ANSWER ---
'Head-Up' display orientation and relative vectors.

--- QUESTION 1110 ---
What primary backup heading reference must be used to steer the ship and monitor heading if the gyrocompass fails?
--- ANSWER ---
The magnetic compass (or Transmitting Magnetic Compass).

--- QUESTION 1111 ---
How must an officer fix the vessel's position on an ECDIS screen if the gyro heading input is lost?
--- ANSWER ---
By manually plotting positions using radar ranges.

--- QUESTION 1112 ---
What two tool features within a RADAR/ARPA provide critical support to an officer for range and bearing estimation?
--- ANSWER ---
The Variable Range Marker (VRM) and Electronic Bearing Line (EBL).

--- QUESTION 1113 ---
What feature on an ARPA allows an OOW to simulate a turn or speed change to assess collision avoidance options before executing them?
--- ANSWER ---
Trial Manoeuvre.

--- QUESTION 1114 ---
If a RADAR/ARPA hardware failure occurs, what alternative methods can be used to cross-check GNSS positions and maintain collision avoidance?
--- ANSWER ---
Using cross bearings on ECDIS, visual bearings, and relying on AIS data.

--- QUESTION 1115 ---
According to Appendix 6 of the performance standards, what is the core purpose of an ECDIS back-up system?
--- ANSWER ---
To ensure that safe navigation is not compromised in the event of an ECDIS failure and to allow the vessel to be navigated safely until the termination of the voyage.

--- QUESTION 1116 ---
What are five potential software, hardware, or operational hazards that can cause an ECDIS failure or impairment?
--- ANSWER ---
Hardware failure, software failure, power failure, failure to update charts correctly, input failure, virus infection, or operator error.

--- QUESTION 1117 ---
How is the severity of an ECDIS failure categorized under a typical safety management framework?
--- ANSWER ---
Minor, Major, or Critical.

--- QUESTION 1118 ---
What defines a "Major" severity risk during an ECDIS breakdown?
--- ANSWER ---
Severe disruption to the availability of navigation information, loss of alarm functions, and an inability to continuously monitor the vessel's position.

--- QUESTION 1119 ---
What defines a "Critical" severity risk when operating ECDIS in the Raster Chart Display System (RCDS) mode?
--- ANSWER ---
The ECDIS in RCDS mode becomes totally unreliable, resulting in a loss of safety-critical navigational information and an inability to maintain or monitor the planned track.

--- QUESTION 1120 ---
What formula defines the loss of stability during the initial phase of the critical period when a ship's stern touches the blocks?
--- ANSWER ---
P= COT x MCTC / Dist LCF

--- QUESTION 1121 ---
What event marks the start of the critical period during dry-docking?
--- ANSWER ---
The critical period begins when the water is pumped out of the dock and the ship touches the blocks aft.

--- QUESTION 1122 ---
During dry-docking, what occurs to the true mean draught as water is pumped out further during the critical period?
--- ANSWER ---
The true mean draught starts to reduce.

--- QUESTION 1123 ---
How do the forward and aft draughts behave when a vessel begins to change trim by the head during the critical period?
--- ANSWER ---
The aft draught reduces more quickly than the forward draught increases.

--- QUESTION 1124 ---
What force gradually increases to replace the upthrust due to buoyancy force at the center of buoyancy as water is pumped out?
--- ANSWER ---
The upthrust at the keel (P force).

--- QUESTION 1125 ---
When is the critical period considered over during the dry-docking process?
--- ANSWER ---
When the vessel comes to rest on the blocks along its entire length (also known as the Critical Instant).

--- QUESTION 1126 ---
When a ship touches the blocks forward and aft, what maximum change has it experienced?
--- ANSWER ---
Its maximum change of trim.

--- QUESTION 1127 ---
Once a ship touches the blocks forward and aft and the draught is reduced uniformly, how is the upthrust (P) force distributed?
--- ANSWER ---
The upthrust (P) is distributed along the entire length of the ship.

--- QUESTION 1128 ---
When the dry dock is completely empty, what does the upthrust (P) equal?
--- ANSWER ---
The upthrust (P) equals the ship's displacement.

--- QUESTION 1129 ---
At what exact point does the maximum loss of GM of concern occur during dry-docking?
--- ANSWER ---
The instant the ship touches the blocks forward and aft.

--- QUESTION 1130 ---
With which specific value does the loss of stability vary directly during the critical period?
--- ANSWER ---
The value of the upthrust at the keel (P force).

--- QUESTION 1131 ---
What are some essential stability considerations when preparing a vessel for dry-docking?
--- ANSWER ---

1. GM of the ship and maximum loss of GM during the critical period.
2. Ensuring the vessel is stable with positive stability throughout.
3. Ensuring the vessel is trimmed as per dock requirements.
4. Keeping the vessel upright.
5. Knowing and accounting for all ballast, fresh water (FW), fuel oil (FO), and cargo onboard.

--- QUESTION 1132 ---
What type of dock can be intentionally operated with a list or trim to match a vessel landing on it?
--- ANSWER ---
A floating dry dock.

--- QUESTION 1133 ---
Which main supporting components of a floating dry dock displace the weight of the vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
The pontoons.

--- QUESTION 1134 ---
How must a floating dock's pontoon distribute the concentrated load of a ship along the dock's centerline?
--- ANSWER ---
By its transverse strength to the uniform buoyant support of the water pressure.

--- QUESTION 1135 ---
What are the three regulatory bodies that share jurisdiction over work carried out by ships' crews in UK shipyards and dry docks?
--- ANSWER ---
The Health and Safety Executive (HSE), the Maritime and Coastguard Agency (MCA), and the Marine Accident Investigation Branch (MAIB).

--- QUESTION 1136 ---
What agreement describes in detail how the HSE, MCA, and MAIB co-operate in enforcement responsibilities?
--- ANSWER ---
A Memorandum of Understanding (MoU).

--- QUESTION 1137 ---
Which specific UK legislation generally applies to the activities of ships' crews in Great Britain, including vessels being loaded or unloaded?
--- ANSWER ---
The Health and Safety at Work etc. Act 1974 (HSW Act).

--- QUESTION 1138 ---
When normal shipboard activities are carried out by the crew, which legislation takes precedence over the HSW Act?
--- engineering ---
Merchant shipping legislation enforced by the MCA.

--- QUESTION 1139 ---
Under what circumstances does Health and Safety Executive (HSE) legislation apply to crew work performed in a shipyard?
--- ANSWER ---
When the work does not meet the test of "normal shipboard activities."

--- QUESTION 1140 ---
Which specific Workplace Regulations apply on ships in respect of lighting and safe access while in a shipyard?
--- ANSWER ---
The Workplace (Health, Safety and Welfare) Regulations.

--- QUESTION 1141 ---
How are voyage repairs defined, and which agency deals with them when carried out by crew members afloat in docks or at sea?
--- ANSWER ---
Voyage repairs are typically minor work activities (such as replacement of seals on hatch covers or minor engine repairs) and are dealt with by the MCA.

--- QUESTION 1142 ---
According to COSWP Chapter 34, what document should an organization create to interlink the shipowner's health and safety expectations with the shipyard's safety management systems?
--- ANSWER ---
An interface document.

--- QUESTION 1143 ---
Name two methods used to comply with the International Ship and Port Facility Security (ISPS) Code at a dry-dock gangway.
--- ANSWER ---
A T-card system or a computer-linked swipe card system.

--- QUESTION 1144 ---
Why must shipboard security officers consider additional measures or amendments to security protocols while in a shipyard?
--- ANSWER ---
Because shipyard workers may need to work in areas of the vessel that are normally only accessible by crew members.

--- QUESTION 1145 ---
What information must the shipyard maintain regarding attending ship personnel to manage worksite access?
--- ANSWER ---
An up-to-date list of all the ship's personnel who will be attending the worksite.

--- QUESTION 1146 ---
What training or briefing must external specialist personnel or newly joining seafarers receive upon assignment to a vessel in a shipyard?
--- ANSWER ---
A detailed safety induction/re-familiarization covering emergency alarms, procedures, escape routes, key personnel, and the nature of the works being carried out.

--- QUESTION 1147 ---
Name at least five utility services or arrangements typically required by a vessel from a dry dock facility.
--- ANSWER ---
Shore power, sludge removal, garbage disposal, heating (in cold climates), a charged fire main, fire rounds (if not made by the crew), and a minimum of two means of access.

--- QUESTION 1148 ---
What essential components must be included within a shipyard-ship communication interface document?
--- ANSWER ---
Identification of personnel, roles and responsibilities; communications and safety procedures; cross-references to the works' project plan; and emergency plans.

--- QUESTION 1149 ---
In what language(s) must the shipyard interface and emergency documents be prepared?
--- ANSWER ---
In the working language(s) used within the shipyard and on board the vessel.

--- QUESTION 1150 ---
Following what specific event must a shipyard interface document be formally reviewed and updated?
--- ANSWER ---
Following any incident within the shipyard.

--- QUESTION 1151 ---
What safety document must the shipyard make available that depicts operational fire hydrants, extinguishers, safety equipment, muster points, and escape routes?
--- ANSWER ---
A safety arrangement plan (based on the general arrangement of the vessel).

--- QUESTION 1152 ---
Where should a copy of the shipyard safety arrangement plan be visibly placed for contractors and crew?
--- ANSWER ---
In prominent areas and at the access point (gangway) to the vessel.

--- QUESTION 1153 ---
What is the minimum number of means of access/egress required between a vessel and the quayside when in dry dock?
--- ANSWER ---
A minimum of two means of access/egress.

--- QUESTION 1154 ---
Why are slips, trips, and falls particularly likely during shipyard repair periods?
--- ANSWER ---
Due to reduced lighting, the removal or movement of handrails/guardrails, the use of slippery substances, and accumulated works debris like scrap metal.

--- QUESTION 1155 ---
What administrative safety measure should ship staff maintain to monitor enclosed space entry when multiple hatches are open in a shipyard?
--- ANSWER ---
A register of all access hatches that have been opened, combined with appropriate barriers to prevent unauthorized access.

--- QUESTION 1156 ---
What fire protection measure must always be posted during hot work in a shipyard due to the risk of heat transfer to adjacent spaces?
--- ANSWER ---
Fire watchers.

--- QUESTION 1157 ---
According to COSWP Chapter 34.6, what factors must shipowners evaluate regarding seafarer wellbeing if crew members continue to live on board during planned shipyard works?
--- ANSWER ---
Disruption to basic facilities and environmental factors such as noise, dust, and ambient temperature that could disrupt sleep and rest.

--- QUESTION 1158 ---
If a gangway or physical means of access is loaned by a shoreside employer to the shipmaster for use as ship's equipment, who is responsible for its rigging and maintenance?
--- ANSWER ---
The ship's master (it is deemed to have been provided by the ship).

--- QUESTION 1159 ---
Under what section of the HSW Act does a shoreside supplier retain a duty to supply access equipment in a safe condition, even if loaned to the ship?
--- ANSWER ---
Section 6 of the Health and Safety at Work etc. Act.

--- QUESTION 1160 ---
According to Approved Code of Practice (ACOP) guidelines, where must safety nets be securely rigged near a vessel's access point?
--- ANSWER ---
Where the means of access passes over water and there is a significant risk of a person falling into the water and drowning.

--- QUESTION 1161 ---
What formal document should the Master and Safety Officer receive from the dry dock facility prior to the commencement of work to define statutory obligations?
--- ANSWER ---
A statement or formal document detailing the transfer of responsibilities under the applicable shore regulations.

--- QUESTION 1162 ---
List at least six specific safety, technical, or administrative checks that must be completed and approved prior to flooding a dry dock.
--- ANSWER ---

1. Stability check approved (P force and loss of GM calculated).
2. Bottom plugs replaced with evidence of replacement.
3. No debris left on the dock bottom.
4. Load lines and draught marks painted.
5. Ship identity markings painted (Name, Port of Registry, MMSI).
6. Sacrificial anodes in place.
7. Protective coverings replaced for echo sounders.
8. Sea chest covers replaced.
9. Authority to flood agreed and signed.

--- QUESTION 1163 ---
How is the tightness and effectiveness of hatch cover sealing arrangements checked during a docking survey?
--- ANSWER ---
By hose testing or an equivalent method.

--- QUESTION 1164 ---
Which survey verification requires confirmation that overall and close-up surveys, thickness measurements, and repairs below the light ballast waterline have been completed?
--- ANSWER ---
A docking survey held as part of an Intermediate or Special Survey.

--- QUESTION 1165 ---
What operational test must be carried out following major repairs to the main propulsion, steering gear, or controls before a surveyor verifies proper operation?
--- ANSWER ---
A trial of the relevant machinery items, including a sea trial if considered necessary.

--- QUESTION 1166 ---
What certificate is officially endorsed upon the satisfactory completion of a bottom survey in dry dock?
--- ANSWER ---
The SAFCON (Cargo Ship Safety Construction) Certificate.

--- QUESTION 1167 ---
What specific measurement must be recorded and verified for the rudder assembly during a dry-docking inspection?
--- ANSWER ---
Clearances in the rudder bearings (bush clearances).

--- QUESTION 1168 ---
What are the minimum parameters required for analyzing stern tube lubricating oil at intervals not exceeding 6 months under Tailshaft Condition Monitoring (TCM)?
--- ANSWER ---
Water content, chloride content, content of metal particles, and oil aging (oxidation).

--- QUESTION 1169 ---
What non-destructive testing requirement is prescribed at 5-yearly intervals for shafts with keyed propellers?
--- ANSWER ---
Examination of the tailshaft cone, key, and keyway using an efficient crack detection method.

--- QUESTION 1170 ---
What structural areas must be examined on a dredger if the docking survey is part of a special survey?
--- ANSWER ---
Hopper bottom doors and accessories such as hinges, actuating rods, and hydraulic systems.

--- QUESTION 1171 ---
What visual indicator or tool is used to record and verify that stern bush clearances are within satisfactory parameters?
--- ANSWER ---
Poker gauge readings.

--- QUESTION 1172 ---
What formal statement must the Master provide at the end of a docking survey checklist regarding the vessel's recent operational history?
--- ANSWER ---
A declaration confirming that the vessel did not sustain damage due to grounding, contact, or weather.

--- QUESTION 1173 ---
According to IAMSAR Manual Volume III, what hard-copy publications are required to be carried onboard UK flagged vessels?
--- ANSWER ---
The IAMSAR Manual Volume III and the International Code of Signals.

--- QUESTION 1174 ---
Which maritime radio signal is used for messages concerning the safety of navigation or important meteorological warnings?
--- ANSWER ---
The safety signal SECURITE.

--- QUESTION 1175 ---
When is the urgency signal PAN-PAN used under maritime communication protocols?
--- ANSWER ---
It is used when the safety of a mobile craft or a person is in jeopardy, indicating an unsafe situation exists that may eventually require assistance.

--- QUESTION 1176 ---
What specific emergency scenario is explicitly listed as a valid reason to use the distress signal MAYDAY to request immediate assistance?
--- ANSWER ---
A man overboard situation where further help is necessary, or when a mobile craft or person is threatened by grave and imminent danger.

--- QUESTION 1177 ---
When a vessel is 100 miles from the UK coast and needs to request helicopter medical assistance (Medivac) for a suspected heart attack, what are the initial communication steps?
--- ANSWER ---
Contact the Rescue Coordination Centre (RCC) and provide vessel details, name, call sign, contact numbers, position, course, speed, weather conditions, medical information, and indicate a landing or winching area.

--- QUESTION 1178 ---
What specific VHF frequency radio channel is used to establish a direct radio link with a rescue helicopter during an assistance operation?
--- ANSWER ---
VHF Channel 16.

--- QUESTION 1179 ---
According to Marine Guidance Note MGN 325 (M), what are four key pieces of information to be exchanged once a direct radio link is established with a helicopter?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Position of the vessel.
2. Course and speed to the rendezvous position.
3. Local weather conditions.
4. How to identify the vessel from the air (e.g., flags, smoke, or spotlights).

--- QUESTION 1180 ---
Which Marine Guidance Note (MGN) issued by the Maritime and Coastguard Agency (MCA) specifically covers "Helicopter Assistance at Sea" and replaces MSN 1506?
--- ANSWER ---
MGN 325 (M).

--- QUESTION 1181 ---
Under what circumstances would a helicopter pilot typically implement the "Hi-line technique" during a maritime rescue?
--- ANSWER ---
During poor weather, obstructed vision, or when there is a confined winching area that prevents lowering a crewman or lifting harness directly onto the deck from above.

--- QUESTION 1182 ---
How is the weighted line delivered and managed on deck during a helicopter Hi-line transfer operation?
--- ANSWER ---
The weighted line is lowered to the vessel attached to the aircraft's hook by a weak link. It must be handled by one crew member, and slack should only be taken in when instructed by the helicopter crew. The line must never be attached to the vessel.

--- QUESTION 1183 ---
What critical precaution must be taken before handling a helicopter winch hook or lifting harness as it reaches the vessel's deck?
--- ANSWER ---
The earthing lead must make contact with the vessel before handling to discharge static electricity.

--- QUESTION 1184 ---
On a final lift during a multi-transfer helicopter Hi-line operation, how should the ship's crew handle the end of the line?
--- ANSWER ---
The end of the line should be released over the side of the vessel.

--- QUESTION 1185 ---
If a vessel receives a DSC distress alert on 2187.5 kHz in Sea Area A3, how long should it initially listen to the associated radiotelephony distress channel before taking further action if the alert is not acknowledged?
--- ANSWER ---
The vessel should listen on the distress frequency (2182 kHz) for 5 minutes.

--- QUESTION 1186 ---
Under GMDSS regulations, if your ship receives a DSC distress alert that is not acknowledged by a coast station or another ship within 5 minutes, what action must you take?
--- ANSWER ---
Acknowledge the alert by radiotelephony to the ship in distress on the appropriate frequency (e.g., VHF Ch 16 or 2182 kHz) if able to assist, or transmit a distress relay on behalf of the vessel in distress to inform a coast station or RCC.

--- QUESTION 1187 ---
What is the term given to the transmission of a distress message by a station not itself in distress under GMDSS protocol?
--- ANSWER ---
DROBOSE (Distress Relay On Behalf Of Someone Else).

--- QUESTION 1188 ---
According to SOLAS Regulation V/33, what is the legal obligation of a master of a ship at sea upon receiving a distress signal?
--- ANSWER ---
The master is bound to proceed with all speed to the assistance of the persons in distress, informing them or the search and rescue service if possible.

--- QUESTION 1189 ---
If a master is unable to respond to a distress signal or considers it unreasonable to proceed, what legal entries and notifications must be made?
--- ANSWER ---
The master must enter the reason for failing to proceed in the ship's logbook and inform the appropriate search and rescue (SAR) authorities.

--- QUESTION 1190 ---
When a DSC distress message is received via Satcom C while operating in the Pacific Ocean (Sea Area A3), how should the vessel reply to acknowledge receipt?
--- ANSWER ---
Reply to the Land Earth Station (LES) or Rescue Coordination Centre (RCC) using Satcom C, acknowledging receipt of the message, confirming that you are responding, and providing your ETA.

--- QUESTION 1191 ---
What four primary factors must be evaluated when determining "datum" for plotting a search and rescue course?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Reported position and time of the SAR incident.
2. Supplementary information like DF (Direction Finding) bearings or sightings.
3. Time interval between the incident and the arrival of SAR facilities.
4. Estimated surface movements of the distressed craft or survival craft based on drift.

--- QUESTION 1192 ---
When evaluating the effects of leeway on an abandoned liferaft, what two configurations alter its drift rate relative to wind speed?
--- ANSWER ---

1. A liferaft with a ballast system and a deployed drogue (slower drift).
2. A liferaft with a canopy but without a deployed drogue (faster drift).

--- QUESTION 1193 ---
What are the primary operational duties of a vessel that arrives first at a distress scene and assumes the role of On-Scene Coordinator (OSC)?
--- ANSWER ---
Coordinate the operations of all on-scene SAR facilities, execute and modify the search/rescue action plan, coordinate on-scene communications, safely maintain separation between facilities, provide situation reports (SITREPs) to the SMC, and maintain a detailed log.

--- QUESTION 1194 ---
Which search pattern is most effective when the position of the search object is accurately known and the search area is small and circular?
--- ANSWER ---
A Sector Search (Sector pattern: single-unit VS).

--- QUESTION 1195 ---
What are the standard operational radius and turn characteristics for a merchant vessel performing a Sector Search?
--- ANSWER ---
The radius is usually between 2 NM and 5 NM, and each turn is 120 degrees, normally executed to starboard.

--- QUESTION 1196 ---
Which search pattern is characterized by a commence search point at the datum, with the first leg oriented directly into the wind, and is highly appropriate for finding persons in the water with little to no leeway?
--- ANSWER ---
An Expanding Square Search (SS).

--- QUESTION 1197 ---
When is a Parallel Track Search pattern preferred over sector or expanding square patterns?
--- ANSWER ---
It is used to search a large area when the survivor's location is uncertain, or when a large search area must be divided into sub-areas for assignment to multiple individual search facilities on-scene simultaneously.

--- QUESTION 1198 ---
How is the track spacing formula written mathematically to determine the spacing 'S' needed for merchant vessels during a search?
--- ANSWER ---
S = Su x fw (where Su is the recommended track spacing based on visibility and search object type, and fw is the weather correction factor).

--- QUESTION 1199 ---
Based on the recommended track spacing tables, what is the base track spacing (Su) for a "Person in water" when the meteorological visibility is 5 nautical miles?
--- ANSWER ---
0.5 nautical miles.

--- QUESTION 1200 ---
According to maritime search guidance, if you discover wreckage consisting of debris and an oil slick from a distressed vessel, where will survival craft usually be located in relation to the debris field?
--- ANSWER ---
Survival craft will usually be found downwind from the debris (unless the vessel was abandoned some time before sinking, in which case they may be upwind).

--- QUESTION 1201 ---
What key environmental factors must an On-Scene Coordinator evaluate when deciding whether to terminate an active search after 8 hours without success?
--- ANSWER ---
The probability that survivors are in the area, the probability of detection, remaining search endurance of facilities, and the survival time of casualties based on water temperature.

--- QUESTION 1202 ---
What ten points are mandated to be taken into account within a ship-specific 'Rescue Recovery Plan'?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Maneuverability of the ship.
2. Freeboard of the ship.
3. Recovery points on the ship.
4. Characteristics and limitations of recovery equipment.
5. Available crew and personal protective equipment (PPE).
6. Wind force, direction, and spray.
7. Significant wave height.
