8. Period of waves.
9. Swell.
10. Safety of navigation.

--- QUESTION 1203 ---
What is the primary operational purpose of performing a Loren Turn maneuver during a rescue operation?
--- ANSWER ---
It facilitates the launch and recovery of a rescue boat by circling to windward, which creates turbulence and a calm "lee" that interferes with wave patterns and subdues rough seas.

--- QUESTION 1204 ---
What are the step-by-step instructions for executing a standard Loren Turn procedure?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Head into the wind at full speed.
2. Begin the circle and reduce speed to slow when the wind is abeam.
3. When the wind crosses the stern to the opposite quarter, increase speed to half.
4. Continue circling as long as calmer water is needed.
5. Slow down or stop to launch and recover the rescue boat on the leeward side inside the circle.

--- QUESTION 1205 ---
If severe weather conditions prevent launching a rescue boat to assist a sinking vessel, what alternative methods can be used to recover survivors alongside according to the IMO Guide to Recovery Techniques?
--- ANSWER ---
Steaming slowly ahead with the recovery object secured alongside (with the weather on the opposite quarter), transferring survivors to an intermediate platform like a veered-down liferaft acting as a fender, or having survivors enter the water with flotation aids and safety lines to be pulled across the gap.

--- QUESTION 1206 ---
What immediate action must a watch officer take upon sighting a red flare on the horizon?
--- ANSWER ---
Identify the bearing and estimated distance, determine the datum point, report the sighting, turn the vessel onto the heading of the flare to proceed, post extra lookouts, and plot an initial search box using bearing lines adjusted for potential errors.

--- QUESTION 1207 ---
After receiving a DSC Distress Alert Relay from an MRCC on MF, what should your immediate next action be?
--- ANSWER ---
Listen for a MAYDAY RELAY message on 2182 kHz.

--- QUESTION 1208 ---
Under maritime GMDSS radio regulations, what does the specific signal phrase "Seelonce Feenee" indicate?
--- ANSWER ---
It indicates that a radio distress period has ended and normal communication traffic can resume.

--- QUESTION 1209 ---
What are the first five core 'Safety Leadership Qualities' described in COSWP Chapter 1 that apply during critical shipboard operations?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Instill respect and command authority.
2. Lead the team by example.
3. Draw on knowledge and experience.
4. Remain calm in a crisis.
5. Practice 'tough empathy'.

--- QUESTION 1210 ---
Where is the pivot point located on a ship that is stationary or stopped?
--- ANSWER ---
Approximately at midships.

--- QUESTION 1211 ---
Where is the pivot point located on a ship that starts to move ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
Approximately 1/8th from the bow.

--- QUESTION 1212 ---
Where is the pivot point located on a ship that is moving steadily ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
Approximately 1/4 from the bow.

--- QUESTION 1213 ---
Where is the pivot point located on a ship that is moving astern?
--- ANSWER ---
We are told it is approximately 1/4 from the stern.

--- QUESTION 1214 ---
According to IACS UR A1, for what purpose is anchoring equipment intended?
--- ANSWER ---
It is intended for temporary mooring of a vessel within a harbour or a sheltered area when the vessel is awaiting berth, tide, etc.

--- QUESTION 1215 ---
What are the maximum wind speed and maximum current speed environmental parameters for which anchoring equipment is designed under IACS UR A1?
--- ANSWER ---
A maximum wind speed of 11 meters per second (11 m/s) and a maximum current speed of 3 knots.

--- QUESTION 1216 ---
What is the maximum significant wave height assumed under standard anchoring conditions defined by IACS UR A1?
--- ANSWER ---
2 meters.

--- QUESTION 1217 ---
What is the minimum chain scope ratio recommended under IACS UR A1 guidelines for a good holding ground?
--- ANSWER ---
A ratio between 6 to 10 (6:10).

--- QUESTION 1218 ---
What minimum lifting capacity performance is required of an anchor winch motor?
--- ANSWER ---
It must be designed to lift the anchor and fully stop 3 lengths of chain (which equals 82.5 meters).

--- QUESTION 1219 ---
What is the physical length of 3 lengths of anchor chain?
--- ANSWER ---
82.5 meters.

--- QUESTION 1220 ---
Why is a standard windlass or anchoring equipment not designed to hold a ship off an exposed coast in rough weather?
--- ANSWER ---
Because the loads on the anchoring equipment increase to such a degree that its components may be damaged or lost owing to the high energy forces generated, particularly in large ships.

--- QUESTION 1221 ---
Why is it necessary to walk out the anchor under power when a large vessel is anchoring in deep water?
--- ANSWER ---
If the cable is allowed to run unchecked, the windlass brake bands may not be able to exert enough force to stop the cable from running out, resulting in the loss of the anchor.

--- QUESTION 1222 ---
What is a controllable pitch propeller (CPP)?
--- ANSWER ---
A propeller system where the blades are movable and controlled hydraulically, allowing changes in power and direction without reversing the engines or changing engine speed.

--- QUESTION 1223 ---
How is a change from full ahead to full astern accomplished on a vessel equipped with a controllable pitch propeller (CPP)?
--- ANSWER ---
By changing the pitch of the blades without altering the engine speed.

--- QUESTION 1224 ---
What are the operational disadvantages of a Controllable Pitch Propeller (CPP) when the vessel is stopped?
--- ANSWER ---
The propeller continues to rotate, steering is affected at low pitch settings, and fuel is used unnecessarily.

--- QUESTION 1225 ---
How does a right-handed Controllable Pitch Propeller (CPP) behave regarding transverse thrust when moving ahead versus moving astern?
--- ANSWER ---
Going ahead, it produces a weak transverse thrust. Going astern, it produces a very strong transverse thrust (wheeling effect).

--- QUESTION 1226 ---
What is defined as transverse thrust?
--- ANSWER ---
It is the transverse force exerted by the rotation of the ship's propeller.

--- QUESTION 1227 ---
In which directions does a right-handed propeller push the stern and bow when the vessel is going ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
It pushes the stern to starboard and the bow to port.

--- QUESTION 1228 ---
In which directions does a right-handed propeller push the stern and bow when the vessel is going astern?
--- ANSWER ---
It pushes the stern to port and the bow to starboard.

--- QUESTION 1229 ---
In which directions does a left-handed propeller push the stern and bow when the vessel is going ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
It pushes the stern to port and the bow to starboard.

--- QUESTION 1230 ---
In which directions does a left-handed propeller push the stern and bow when the vessel is going astern?
--- ANSWER ---
It pushes the stern to starboard and the bow to port.

--- QUESTION 1231 ---
What is the definition of "scope" in relation to ship anchoring?
--- ANSWER ---
The ratio between the length of chain paid out and the water depth.

--- QUESTION 1232 ---
Where does the pivot point settle on a vessel moving ahead under power?
--- ANSWER ---
Approximately 25% to 30% of the waterline length from forward.

--- QUESTION 1233 ---
Where does the pivot point settle on a vessel moving astern under power?
--- ANSWER ---
Approximately 25% or less of the waterline length from aft.

--- QUESTION 1234 ---
Where is the pivot point located on a stationary vessel that is stopped in the water?
--- ANSWER ---
Close to the Centre of Gravity.

--- QUESTION 1235 ---
What is the definition of a ship's pivot point?
--- ANSWER ---
The point on a ship's centreline which appears to be the centre of rotation.

--- QUESTION 1236 ---
At what point on a ship's centreline is the drift angle equal to zero?
--- ANSWER ---
The pivot point.

--- QUESTION 1237 ---
Why is a stern thruster more effective than a bow thruster of equal force when a vessel is moving ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
Because the pivot point moves forward, giving the stern thruster a larger turning lever.

--- QUESTION 1238 ---
Why does a bow thruster have a larger turning lever than an equal-force stern thruster when making sternway?
--- ANSWER ---
Because the pivot point moves aft when moving astern, increasing the forward distance to the bow thruster.

--- QUESTION 1239 ---
How does being trimmed by the stern affect the pivot point when a vessel is moving ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
The pivot point is further aft than on an even keel and will not move as far forward, which reduces the turning lever and steering capability.

--- QUESTION 1240 ---
How does being trimmed by the head affect the position of the pivot point?
--- ANSWER ---
The pivot point moves further forward, increasing the turning lever.

--- QUESTION 1241 ---
What is the definition of the "centre of effort of the wind" (W) on a vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
The position that represents the mean of the area of the vessel exposed to the wind.

--- QUESTION 1242 ---
How do most vessels behave when completely stopped in the water with respect to a steady wind?
--- ANSWER ---
They will generally lie with the wind on or close to the beam, because the centre of effort of the wind and the pivot point are close and create minimal turning influence.

--- QUESTION 1243 ---
What turning effect occurs when a vessel makes headway with the wind directly ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
The pivot point moves forward, creating a minimum turning lever, allowing the vessel to maintain its course.

--- QUESTION 1244 ---
If a vessel making headway experiences a change in relative wind direction from ahead, how does the centre of effort of the wind (W) respond?
--- ANSWER ---
W moves aft, creating a turning lever that tends to turn the bow back into the wind.

--- QUESTION 1245 ---
What is the effect on course-keeping ability when a vessel making headway has a large trim by the stern and wind ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
Course-keeping ability is reduced because W moves further forward, leading to a reduction in the turning lever.

--- QUESTION 1246 ---
What turning tendency occurs when a vessel makes sternway with the wind ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
The pivot point shifts aft while W remains forward; a change in relative wind direction creates a lever that turns the bow away from the wind, causing a loss of course stability.

--- QUESTION 1247 ---
What happens to a vessel's bow and stern when making sternway with the wind originally from astern?
--- ANSWER ---
The pivot point moves aft and beyond W, causing the bow to fall off the wind and the stern to seek the wind.

--- QUESTION 1248 ---
Why is a vessel difficult to steer and unstable in course when making headway with the wind astern?
--- ANSWER ---
The centre of effort of the wind (W) is well aft, and any relative change moves W forward (but still aft of the pivot point), creating a turning lever that tends to turn the stern away from the wind.

--- QUESTION 1249 ---
What turning effect is created when a vessel makes sternway with the wind astern and the relative wind direction shifts?
--- ANSWER ---
W moves forward of the pivot point, creating a turning lever that tends to turn the stern back into the wind, providing course stability.

--- QUESTION 1250 ---
What is maritime "squat"?
--- ANSWER ---
The reduction in under-keel clearance (UKC) between a vessel at-rest and underway due to the increased flow of water past the moving body.

--- QUESTION 1251 ---
What physical interaction triggers the onset of squat?
--- ANSWER ---
It occurs when the ship's elliptical pressure domain comes into contact with that of the sea bed or a bank.

--- QUESTION 1252 ---
How does squat affect a ship's vertical position at the bow and stern?
--- ANSWER ---
It produces a downward vertical force (sinkage) that causes the ship to sink into a water level depression, which can result in different sinkage values at the bow and stern.

--- QUESTION 1253 ---
How does an increased pressure force ahead of the bow during a swing in shallow water affect steering?
--- ANSWER ---
It moves the pivot point aft, which reduces the steering lever.

--- QUESTION 1254 ---
What type of hull shape features a high block coefficient (Cb) that causes it to squat more and typically by the bow?
--- ANSWER ---
Vessels with a large block coefficient (Cb).

--- QUESTION 1255 ---
List three observable indicators or effects on a vessel experiencing severe squat.
--- ANSWER ---
Sluggish steering, increased stopping distance/turning circle, ship vibrations, decreased engine RPM, increased bow wave/wake width, and altered draught indicator readings.

--- QUESTION 1256 ---
What turning radius change should be expected when transitioning from deep water to shallow water?
--- ANSWER ---
The turning radius can expect to be as much as double that in deep water.

--- QUESTION 1257 ---
What basic action can a mariner take to reduce the hydrodynamic effects of squat?
--- ANSWER ---
Reduce the speed of the vessel.

--- QUESTION 1258 ---
What is the "bank effect" when a vessel navigates close to a gently shelving bank?
--- ANSWER ---
The vessel experiences forces pushing the bow away from the bank and pulling the stern towards the bank.

--- QUESTION 1259 ---
What causes the strong suction experienced around a ship's stern during bank interaction?
--- ANSWER ---
The restricted water flow between the hull and the bank increases water velocity, which causes a drop in pressure and generates suction.

--- QUESTION 1260 ---
What is "bank cushion"?
--- ANSWER ---
The buildup of a wedge of water between the bow and a nearby bank as a ship moves ahead, forcing the bow out sharply away from the bank.

--- QUESTION 1261 ---
What dangerous combined movement can bank cushion and bank suction cause a ship to take?
--- ANSWER ---
A sudden and decided sheer toward the opposite bank.

--- QUESTION 1262 ---
What immediate helm and engine action should be taken if a vessel's stern swings dangerously close to a bank?
--- ANSWER ---
Apply helm towards the bank, with a burst of power if necessary, ensuring the correcting rudder is removed once clear.

--- QUESTION 1263 ---
According to UK Marine Guidance Note MGN 199 (M), what three main dangers of interaction should mariners appreciate?
--- ANSWER ---
Sudden steering changes when passing vessels at close range, the need to reduce speed in narrow channels, the dangerous effects on tugs near large vessels, and the effects of squat.

--- QUESTION 1264 ---
What occurs when the elliptical hydrodynamic domains of two separate ships come into contact?
--- ANSWER ---
Hydrodynamic interaction between the vessels occurs.

--- QUESTION 1265 ---
What occurs during Stage 1 of a passing interaction between a large and a smaller vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
Both ships swing to port, potentially causing the smaller vessel to hit the river bank.

--- QUESTION 1266 ---
What movement risk does a smaller vessel face during Stage 2 of a passing interaction?
--- ANSWER ---
The smaller vessel may swing in towards the midships of the larger vessel.

--- QUESTION 1267 ---
What happens to the headings of two vessels during Stage 3 of a passing interaction?
--- ANSWER ---
Both vessels go to starboard, and the smaller vessel will be drawn across the stern of the larger vessel.

--- QUESTION 1268 ---
What physical movement may occur to both vessels during Stage 2 of an overtaking interaction?
--- ANSWER ---
Both vessels are drawn ahead and may incline and move bodily towards each other.

--- QUESTION 1269 ---
What is the risk to a smaller vessel during Stage 3 of an overtaking interaction?
--- ANSWER ---
Both vessels go to starboard, and the smaller vessel will be drawn across the bow of the larger vessel.

--- QUESTION 1270 ---
When a cape-size bulk carrier is moving at a speed above 4 knots with small under-keel clearance, what effect can its significant bow wave have on a tug working around its bow?
--- ANSWER ---
The tug may be pushed forward due to the significant bow wave generated by the bulk carrier.

--- QUESTION 1271 ---
Why might a tug experience difficulty breaking free from the side of an assisted vessel when working alongside it, and what action should be taken to help the tug?
--- ANSWER ---
The tug may experience difficulty breaking free due to a suction effect. It may be necessary to reduce the speed of the assisted ship to allow the tug to break free.

--- QUESTION 1272 ---
How do the azimuth drives of a tug typically point when proceeding at low speed, and what hazard can the wash from the propeller closest to the assisted vessel's hull create?
--- ANSWER ---
The thruster units are generally pointed slightly outward of the centerline. The wash from the propeller closest to the hull can increase water speed between the tug and the ship, dropping the pressure and causing the tug to be rapidly drawn onto the assisted ship's hull.

--- QUESTION 1273 ---
Why should an assisted ship's propeller be stopped, if possible, when making a tug fast at the stern?
--- ANSWER ---
It prevents rope entanglement in the propeller and allows the tug to work in a relatively undisturbed area of water.

--- QUESTION 1274 ---
What does the term "girting" refer to in tug operations?
--- ANSWER ---
Girting refers to a hazardous situation where a tug is towed broadside by a towline and is unable to maneuver out of this position, putting it at risk of capsizing.

--- QUESTION 1275 ---
What physical effect does a towline on the beam exert on a tug during a girting scenario, and what is the ultimate risk if it exceeds the tug's natural righting lever?
--- ANSWER ---
The towline exerts a heeling moment. If it is sufficiently powerful to overcome the tug's natural righting lever, it will cause the tug to capsize rapidly.

--- QUESTION 1276 ---
Which design characteristics make certain tugs particularly vulnerable to girting?
--- ANSWER ---
Tugs with propulsion aft and the towing point located amidships are particularly vulnerable.

--- QUESTION 1277 ---
Why can crew members not safely assume that a dangerous girting situation will be prevented automatically by equipment behavior?
--- ANSWER ---
It should not be assumed that the tow wire will part or that the winch brake will render prior to the tug capsizing.

--- QUESTION 1278 ---
Under what specific operational condition can a conventional tug positioned at the bow of an assisted vessel be overtaken and girted?
--- ANSWER ---
It can happen when the conventional tug at the bow is overtaken by its tow at speed.

--- QUESTION 1279 ---
How can a conventional tug positioned at the stern and moving astern end up girting?
--- ANSWER ---
It can girt if the centerline of the tug becomes misaligned with the direction of the tow.

--- QUESTION 1280 ---
What sudden action by an assisted vessel can prone a conventional bow tug to girting?
--- ANSWER ---
If the assisted vessel takes an abrupt shear.

--- QUESTION 1281 ---
What routine safety action regarding a tug's openings must be taken to mitigate the risk of downflooding?
--- ANSWER ---
Weathertight doors and hatches should be tightly closed.

--- QUESTION 1282 ---
With what type of emergency system should a tug be fitted to quickly release the towline in an emergency?
--- ANSWER ---
An emergency quick release system, such as a trip hook or a winch brake release.

--- QUESTION 1283 ---
What hull features and obstructions must a Master take note of prior to engaging tugs?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master should take note of bulbous bows, overboard discharges, and hull overhangs.

--- QUESTION 1284 ---
What action should be taken regarding a vessel's stabilizers prior to engaging tugs?
--- ANSWER ---
It is prudent to retract stabilizers prior to engaging tugs.

--- QUESTION 1285 ---
What are "push points" on a ship's hull, and why must a ship's crew ensure that tugs use them?
--- ANSWER ---
Push points are strengthened hull areas designed specifically for tugs to push against. Ensuring tugs use them avoids creating indents on the ship's hull.

--- QUESTION 1286 ---
How should a tug's lines be connected to a ship to prevent the line from jumping out of place?
--- ANSWER ---
They should be connected via a Panama lead and not through open fairleads.

--- QUESTION 1287 ---
What is the generally recommended maximum speed for connecting a tug to the center lead forward of a vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
A maximum of 6 knots.

--- QUESTION 1288 ---
What directive should be covered in toolbox talks regarding how crew members lower lines to a tug?
--- ANSWER ---
Lines should be lowered in a controlled manner, unless otherwise requested by the Master.

--- QUESTION 1289 ---
From what location on the ship should a heaving line be passed to a tug, and what type of heaving lines are forbidden?
--- ANSWER ---
The heaving line should be passed from the shoulder rather than right ahead, and weighted heaving lines must not be used.

--- QUESTION 1290 ---
What five key questions must be asked regarding ship and tug positioning to achieve the desired maneuver?
--- ANSWER ---

1. What is the intended movement and/or maneuver?
2. Will the ship be predominantly with headway, backing, or stopped?
3. Where will the ship's pivot point be?
4. Where will the tug be in relationship to the ship's pivot point?
5. What type of tug is being used?

--- QUESTION 1291 ---
What are the primary purposes of deploying an escort tug in a channel?
--- ANSWER ---
To assist a vessel in making a tight turn that it could not make alone, and to safeguard against the risk of pollution if the vessel loses power and faces grounding.

--- QUESTION 1292 ---
Where is an escort tug most effectively secured on a vessel, and what modes can it operate in at higher speeds?
--- ANSWER ---
It is most effective if secured to the aft end of the vessel, where it can stop the vessel or be used in indirect mode at higher speeds.

--- QUESTION 1293 ---
What types of tugs are ideal for channel escort towage operations?
--- ANSWER ---
Azimuth Stern Drive (ASD) and Tractor tugs.

--- QUESTION 1294 ---
How does an ASD escort tug operate its thrusters to initially slow down a vessel from a speed of approximately 3 knots?
--- ANSWER ---
The ASD tug operates both thrusters on the beam to create a wall of water to slow down the vessel.

--- QUESTION 1295 ---
Where is the towing point located on a conventional tug relative to its propulsion?
--- ANSWER ---
The towing point is located forward of the propulsion, at a distance of 0.45 times the Length Overall (LOA) from aft.

--- QUESTION 1296 ---
How does the astern power of a conventional tug compare to its ahead power?
--- ANSWER ---
Its astern power is less than its ahead power.

--- QUESTION 1297 ---
What operational difficulty do conventional tugs face when the tow line extends beyond 30 degrees from astern?
--- ANSWER ---
The tug experiences difficulty in returning the line of pull dead astern.

--- QUESTION 1298 ---
Why is the push mode inefficient for conventional tugs?
--- ANSWER ---
Because their propellers are positioned close to the assisted vessel's hull.

--- QUESTION 1299 ---
What are two economic and structural advantages of conventional tugs?
--- ANSWER ---
They are economical to build, simpler to run, and possess an excellent Bollard Pull (BP) to Brake Horsepower (BHP) ratio.

--- QUESTION 1300 ---
Where are the propulsion units and the towing/pushing points located on a Tractor Tug?
--- ANSWER ---
The propulsion is located forward, and the tow or push point is located aft.

--- QUESTION 1301 ---
What types of propulsion systems are typically found on Tractor Tugs?
--- ANSWER ---
Voith Schneider Propulsion (VSP) or Azimuth thrusters.

--- QUESTION 1302 ---
What is the purpose of the skeg on a tractor tug?
--- ANSWER ---
The skeg provides course stability.

--- QUESTION 1303 ---
How does the astern thrust of a tractor tug compare to its ahead thrust?
--- ANSWER ---
The astern thrust is nearly equal to the ahead thrust.

--- QUESTION 1304 ---
What are two major operational advantages of tractor tugs regarding girting and maneuverability?
--- ANSWER ---
Girting is almost eliminated, and they offer excellent maneuverability.

--- QUESTION 1305 ---
What are common alternative names or designations given to reverse tractor tugs?
--- ANSWER ---
Z-Peller, Z-Drive, or ASD (Azimuth Stern Drive) tugs.

--- QUESTION 1306 ---
Where is the primary towing point located on an ASD / reverse tractor tug, and what capabilities does this layout provide?
--- ANSWER ---
The towing point is located on the foredeck, which allows for excellent push capability and a large turning pull.

--- QUESTION 1307 ---
Under what circumstances does a risk of girting still exist on an ASD or reverse tractor tug?
--- ANSWER ---
The risk of girting is much reduced when using the bow towing point, but it still exists if the tug utilizes its additional towing gear located aft.

--- QUESTION 1308 ---
Describe the three primary methods of tug assistance.
--- ANSWER ---

1. On the line towing: The tug is connected to the assisted vessel by a line.
2. Push-Pull: The tug is connected by a short line (well-suited for ASD/Tractor tugs).
3. Indirect Towing: The tug is dragged by the assisted vessel while maintaining a sheered position; tow force is generated by the forces acting directly on the tug's hull.

--- QUESTION 1309 ---
What seven factors should be considered when planning tug deployment?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Port requirements, type, and availability of tugs.
2. Under-keel clearance (and the decrease in maneuverability due to shallow water effects).
3. Wind strength and direction.
4. Windage area of the ship.
5. Stopping power and handling of own ship.
6. State and height of tide.
7. Proximity of other ships, quays, or hazards.

--- QUESTION 1310 ---
Describe the four stages of forward tug interaction that can cause a loss of control at the bow of an assisted vessel.
--- ANSWER ---

1. Suction effect forces the tug alongside the ship.
2. As the tug moves, it passes a zone separating suction and positive forces, pushing the tug's bow away.
3. The tug enters a pressure zone where greater pressure is felt at the tug's stern and over her rudders, causing the stern to shear away.
4. If not countered, the tug may lose control and find itself lying dangerously across the bow of the assisted vessel.

--- QUESTION 1311 ---
During aft tug interaction, what occurs at Position 1 as the tug approaches the suction zone?
--- ANSWER ---
A suction effect may be felt at the tug's bow, inducing a dangerous shear toward the assisted vessel.

--- QUESTION 1312 ---
What happens to a tug at Position 2 in aft interaction due to the reduced water pressure around the ship?
--- ANSWER ---
The tug experiences less water pressure in the suction zone, resulting in an increase in speed as it enters the zone.

--- QUESTION 1313 ---
What risk does a tug face at Position 3 in aft interaction if it cannot retract itself from the suction zone?
--- ANSWER ---
The tug may be drawn bodily towards the hull and land against it, which can cause severe damage to the tug's accommodation, bridge, or mast—especially with ballasted ships or ships with large hull overhangs.

--- QUESTION 1314 ---
Where is a ship's pivot point located roughly when the ship is moving astern (making sternway)?
--- ANSWER ---
The pivot point will be approximately 1/4 of the ship's length from the stern.

--- QUESTION 1315 ---
Why do ship propellers generate relatively poor turning moments when the vessel has sternway?
--- ANSWER ---
Because the pivot point moves aft, any forces created by the propeller (ahead or astern) work on a very small lever, resulting in poor turning moments.

--- QUESTION 1316 ---
Where does a ship's pivot point move when the vessel is making headway, and what factors cause its position to vary?
--- ANSWER ---
The pivot point moves to a position roughly 1/8 to 1/3 of the ship's length from forward. It varies according to the ship's speed and whether the vessel is turning or steady.

--- QUESTION 1317 ---
In what two categories should the turning ability of a twin-screw vessel of modest power be viewed?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Low-speed maneuvering.
2. Maneuvering at higher speeds.

--- QUESTION 1318 ---
How can a twin-screw vessel execute a prolonged "kick ahead" at low speed to maximize turning ability while keeping headway minimized?
--- ANSWER ---
The appropriate engine is run ahead with full rudder to develop the turning force, while the opposite engine is run astern to keep the ship's speed down.

--- QUESTION 1319 ---
What phase of a turn is a twin-screw vessel kept in when going astern on the inside engine during a low-speed maneuver, and where is its pivot point?
--- ANSWER ---
The ship is kept permanently in the first stage of a standing turn, maintaining the pivot point at approximately 1/8 of the ship's length (L) from the bow while keeping lateral resistance low.

--- QUESTION 1320 ---
What does it mean when a modern vessel "splits" its rudders during slow-speed maneuvering?
--- ANSWER ---
It means the vessel operates its rudders independently of each other.

--- QUESTION 1321 ---
Describe the "flare in" or "split" rudder method used to maximize rudder force at slow speeds.
--- ANSWER ---
Both rudders are turned inwards and remain there. This ensures that the appropriate rudder is perfectly pre-positioned to maximize rudder force whenever either engine is operated ahead.

--- QUESTION 1322 ---
Why is the twin-screw effect greatly diminished when a vessel is moving at speeds over 6 knots?
--- ANSWER ---
At speeds over 6 knots, reversing one engine to utilize transverse thrust is unlikely to have a significant effect.

--- QUESTION 1323 ---
What are the five core factors that must be considered in combination during twin-screw ship maneuvering?
--- ANSWER ---

1. The rudder configuration.
2. The effect of torque.
3. The effect of transverse thrust.
4. The pivot point.
5. Turning ability.

--- QUESTION 1324 ---
When viewed from astern, how are outward-turning fixed-pitch propellers defined when rotating ahead?
--- ANSWER ---
The blades of the propellers turn outward in the upper half of their circle of rotation.

--- QUESTION 1325 ---
If a twin-screw ship with outward-turning fixed-pitch propellers puts its starboard propeller astern to assist a turn, how does that propeller behave and how does its transverse thrust affect the ship?
--- ANSWER ---
The starboard propeller behaves like a right-handed propeller on a single-screw ship, deflecting its helical discharge up onto the starboard quarter. The resulting transverse thrust cants the bow to starboard, assisting the turn.

--- QUESTION 1326 ---
How is the term "torque" defined in the context of twin-screw ship handling?
--- ANSWER ---
Torque describes the natural turning effect created by one engine pushing ahead and one engine pulling astern (or, to a lesser extent, just one engine operating), provided the propellers are not positioned too close together.

--- QUESTION 1327 ---
Why do inward-turning fixed-pitch propellers offer poor ship handling characteristics during a turn?
--- ANSWER ---
Because when an engine is put astern to assist a turn, their transverse thrust acts against the intended direction of the turn.

--- QUESTION 1328 ---
What configuration must two controllable pitch (CP) propellers have to achieve the same positive handling results as outward-turning fixed-pitch propellers?
--- ANSWER ---
The two CP propellers need to be inward turning when viewed from astern.

--- QUESTION 1329 ---
What component is the standing part of a preventer wire secured to when hanging off an anchor?
--- ANSWER ---
The standing part is secured to the bitts.

--- QUESTION 1330 ---
Where is the hauling part of a preventer wire led when performing an anchor hanging-off procedure?
--- ANSWER ---
The hauling part is led to the windlass warping drum.

--- QUESTION 1331 ---
Immediately after a collision occurs, what initial action should be taken regarding the vessel's propulsion and damage evaluation?
--- ANSWER ---
Stop the engine and assess the damage.

--- QUESTION 1332 ---
To reduce the ingress of water after a collision, what action should be taken if the other vessel remains embedded in your hull?
--- ANSWER ---
Make fast to the other vessel to help reduce the ingress of water.

--- QUESTION 1333 ---
Which five external organizations, authorities, or internal company representatives must be informed following a collision?
--- ANSWER ---
The local Coast Guard, MCA (Maritime and Coastguard Agency), the classification society, the Designated Person Ashore (DPA), and the other vessel (by exchanging information).

--- QUESTION 1334 ---
Under SOLAS Chapter II-1/3-8, what type of plan must ships carry on board regarding mooring equipment?
--- ANSWER ---
A management plan for the inspection and maintenance of mooring equipment.

--- QUESTION 1335 ---
According to SOLAS regulations for mooring equipment, what specific manufacturer document and what historical structural records must be available on board?
--- ANSWER ---
The manufacturers' criteria for the replacement of mooring lines, and records of the original design, equipment, arrangements, and specifications.

--- QUESTION 1336 ---
Define Ship Design Minimum Breaking Load (MBLSD).
--- ANSWER ---
MBLSD means the minimum breaking load of new mooring lines for which shipboard fittings and supporting hull structures are designed to meet mooring restraint requirements.

--- QUESTION 1337 ---
Define Line Design Break Force (LDBF).
--- ANSWER ---
LDBF is the minimum force at which a new, dry, spliced mooring line will break. It applies to all synthetic cordage materials.

--- QUESTION 1338 ---
What is the specific distinction between the applications of Safe Working Load (SWL) and Working Load Limit (WLL) in mooring operations?
--- ANSWER ---
Safe Working Load (SWL) applies to shipboard fittings used for mooring operations in harbors or similar sheltered waters, whereas Working Load Limit (WLL) applies directly to the mooring lines themselves.

--- QUESTION 1339 ---
How is the Working Load Limit (WLL) calculated for steel wire ropes and other synthetic ropes relative to MBLSD?
--- ANSWER ---
For steel wire ropes, the WLL is 55% of the ship design MBLSD. For other synthetic ropes, the WLL is 50% of the ship design MBLSD.

--- QUESTION 1340 ---
Under SOLAS requirements, what must be true about the format and content of an Emergency Towing Booklet (ETB)?
--- ANSWER ---
The ETB must be ship-specific and presented in a clear, concise, and ready-to-use format (such as a booklet, plan, or poster).

--- QUESTION 1341 ---
List at least five specific shipboard data items that must be included in the Emergency Towing Booklet (ETB).
--- ANSWER ---
Ship's name, call sign, IMO number, anchor details, cable and chain details, height of mooring deck(s) above base, draft range, and displacement range.

--- QUESTION 1342 ---
What is the minimum number of Emergency Towing Booklet (ETB) copies required on board, and where must they be located?
--- ANSWER ---
A minimum of three copies must be kept on board, located on the Bridge, Forecastle space, and either the Ship's office or Cargo Control room.

--- QUESTION 1343 ---
What action should the Master take if a tug arrives with a bollard pull that exceeds the Safe Towing Load (STL) of the ship's deck fittings?
--- ANSWER ---
Inform the tug master of the ship's STL limit and agree on a towing plan where the tug limits its pull to match or stay below the STL (e.g., by adjusting engine power or using a load monitoring system).

--- QUESTION 1344 ---
What is the core definition of "salvage" as set out by the International Convention on Salvage 1989?
--- ANSWER ---
Salvage is any act or operation undertaken to assist a vessel or any other property in danger in navigable waters or in any other waters whatsoever.

--- QUESTION 1345 ---
How do towage services and salvage operations differ in terms of contract law, remuneration terms, and voluntariness?
--- ANSWER ---
Towage services are governed by ordinary contract law for an agreed purpose and fixed sum (tariff rates), and the services are not voluntary. Salvage is an operation to assist a vessel in danger, often operating under the "no cure, no pay" principle, and entitles the salvor to a maritime lien and a reward based on success and environmental protection.

--- QUESTION 1346 ---
What are the two primary types of salvage agreements noted under maritime practice?
--- ANSWER ---
Pure salvage (where no specific prior agreement is made) and Contractual salvage (such as Lloyd's Open Form or MARSALVE).

--- QUESTION 1347 ---
Under a standard salvage contract, what are the two core environmental duties shared by both the Salvor and the Owner/Master?
--- ANSWER ---
Both parties must exercise due care to prevent or minimize damage to the environment during the salvage operations.

--- QUESTION 1348 ---
Name at least five factors considered under maritime law when fixing the financial reward for a successful salvage operation.
--- ANSWER ---

1. The salved value of the vessel and other property.
2. The skill and efforts of the salvors in preventing or minimizing damage to the environment.
3. The measure of success obtained.
4. The nature and degree of the danger.
5. The skill and efforts of the salvors in salving the property and life.
6. The time used, expenses, and losses incurred by the salvors.
7. The risk of liability run by the salvors.
8. The promptness of services.
9. Availability and use of specialized salvage equipment.

--- QUESTION 1349 ---
What fundamental principle forms the commercial basis of the Lloyd's Open Form (LOF) salvage agreement?
--- ANSWER ---
The principle of "No Cure, No Pay."

--- QUESTION 1350 ---
According to MSC.1/Circ.1175/Rev.1, what is the specific difference between the terms SWL and TOW on a ship's arrangement plan?
--- ANSWER ---
TOW (Safe Towing Load) is the safe load limit of shipboard fittings used for normal and other towing purposes, whereas SWL (Safe Working Load) is the safe load limit for fittings used for mooring operations in sheltered waters.

--- QUESTION 1351 ---
What two major limitations of Article 14 of the International Convention on Salvage led to the development and widespread adoption of the SCOPIC clause?
--- ANSWER ---
Article 14 was applicable only in coastal/territorial areas and required the salvor to prove an active threat to the environment. SCOPIC applies in all geographical locations and is applicable even if there is no environmental threat.

--- QUESTION 1352 ---
What happens to Article 14 of the International Convention on Salvage once the SCOPIC clause is formally invoked?
--- ANSWER ---
Once invoked, the SCOPIC clause completely substitutes Article 14.

--- QUESTION 1353 ---
What are the four essential criteria that must be satisfied for an event to be legally declared a valid General Average act?
--- ANSWER ---

1. The ship and cargo must face a common danger.
2. An extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure must be intentionally made to avert that danger.
3. The sacrifice must be real and intentional.
4. The endangered property must be successfully saved by the sacrifice.

--- QUESTION 1354 ---
What are the three permanent forces of magnetism that act on a ship?
--- ANSWER ---
The P force (fore and aft permanent magnetism), Q force (athwartships permanent magnetism), and R force (vertical force due to permanent magnetism).

--- QUESTION 1355 ---
In ship permanent magnetism, what color is assigned to positive attractions?
--- ANSWER ---
Blue

--- QUESTION 1356 ---
According to the ship's permanent magnetism color designations, what does a negative P (-P) force indicate?
--- ANSWER ---
Red Bow

--- QUESTION 1357 ---
What component of the Earth's magnetic field induces magnetism in horizontal soft iron?
--- ANSWER ---
The horizontal component (H)

--- QUESTION 1357 ---
What component of the Earth's magnetic field induces magnetism in vertical soft iron?
--- ANSWER ---
The vertical component (Z)

--- QUESTION 1358 ---
Which soft iron rod represents the continuous fore and aft structure of a vessel, such as girders?
--- ANSWER ---
The "a" rod

--- QUESTION 1359 ---
Why are the "d", "f", and "h" soft iron rods normally absent at a well-placed compass?
--- ANSWER ---
Because the vessel is symmetrical about the fore and aft vertical plane through the compass.

--- QUESTION 1360 ---
Which soft iron rod represents the continuous transverse structure of a ship, such as transverse beams?
--- ANSWER ---
The "e" rod

--- QUESTION 1361 ---
What structural feature of a ship typically creates a positive "k" rod effect because it is mostly below the compass?
--- ANSWER ---
Pillars

--- QUESTION 1362 ---
Define deviation in the context of a magnetic compass.
--- ANSWER ---
Deviation is the deflection of the compass needle to the left or right of the magnetic meridian.

--- QUESTION 1363 ---
What is the primary objective of reducing deviations on a ship?
--- ANSWER ---
To eliminate sectors of sluggishness and unsteadiness around the magnetic compass.

--- QUESTION 1364 ---
When a vessel is upright, how is the total deviation of the compass calculated from the component divisions?
--- ANSWER ---
It is the algebraic sum of the components identified as coefficients (A, B, C, D, and E).

--- QUESTION 1365 ---
What formula is used to determine the total deviation of a compass using coefficients and the compass course?
--- ANSWER ---
Total Deviation = A + B sin Co + C cos Co + D sin 2Co + E cos 2Co

--- QUESTION 1366 ---
What type of deviation is represented by Coefficient A?
--- ANSWER ---
A constant deviation.

--- QUESTION 1367 ---
Name two structural or alignment defects that can cause Coefficient A.
--- ANSWER ---
The lubber line not being parallel to the ship's fore and aft line, and the magnetic axis of the needle system not being parallel to the 0-180 direction of the compass card.

--- QUESTION 1368 ---
How does Coefficient B vary in relation to the ship's compass course?
--- ANSWER ---
It varies as the sine of the compass course.

--- QUESTION 1369 ---
On which headings does Coefficient B cause the maximum deflection of the compass?
--- ANSWER ---
East and West

--- QUESTION 1370 ---
What corrector is used to counteract the induced component of Coefficient B caused by vertical soft iron?
--- ANSWER ---
The Flinders bar

--- QUESTION 1371 ---
How does Coefficient C vary in relation to the ship's compass course?
--- ANSWER ---
It varies as the cosine of the compass course.

--- QUESTION 1372 ---
Which permanent force is responsible for permanent magnetism Coefficient C, and how is it corrected?
--- ANSWER ---
The Q force (athwartships permanent magnetism), corrected by moving the permanent athwartships magnets.

--- QUESTION 1373 ---
How does Coefficient D vary in relation to the ship's compass course?
--- ANSWER ---
It varies as the sine of twice the compass course.

--- QUESTION 1374 ---
What soft iron components are used to correct Coefficient D, and where are they placed?
--- ANSWER ---
Two soft iron quadrantal spheres placed on either side of the compass in the athwartship plane.

--- QUESTION 1375 ---
How does Coefficient E vary in relation to the ship's compass course?
--- ANSWER ---
It varies as the cosine of twice the compass course.

--- QUESTION 1376 ---
How is the correction for Coefficient E carried out on a compass binnacle?
--- ANSWER ---
By slewing the quadrantal spheres.

--- QUESTION 1377 ---
What causes heeling error (Coefficient J) when a vessel rolls or lists?
--- ANSWER ---
When the ship heels, the vertical magnetic field acts at an oblique angle, and its horizontal component causes a deflection because the compass remains horizontal in its gimbals.

--- QUESTION 1378 ---
Where are the correctors for heeling error located, and how is the amount of correction adjusted?
--- ANSWER ---
They are permanent magnets set vertically in a heeling error bucket, adjusted by raising or lowering the bucket within its central vertical tube.

--- QUESTION 1379 ---
What is the correct chronological order for placing correctors during a compass adjustment?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Flinders bar, 2. Spheres, 3. Heeling Error, 4. Hard Iron (Fore-and-Aft & Port-and-Starboard magnets).

--- QUESTION 1380 ---
Name three situations or events after which a full compass correction or re-adjustment must be carried out.
--- ANSWER ---
Any three of the following: when first installed, 3 to 6 months after first correction, after large structural alterations/repairs, after a major fire, after a collision or stranding, after being struck by lightning, after long lay-up periods, or after carrying/discharging magnetic cargo with electromagnets.

--- QUESTION 1381 ---
What is the specific purpose of the tentative method of compass adjustment?
--- ANSWER ---
To determine the deviations of the compass for a ship already in commission that has had her compasses previously adjusted.

--- QUESTION 1382 ---
What precaution must be taken regarding the ship's cargo gear before performing a compass swing?
--- ANSWER ---
All derricks and cranes must be stowed in their sea-going positions.

--- QUESTION 1383 ---
How far away from a distant object must a vessel remain during a compass swing to minimize parallax errors?
--- ANSWER ---
Not less than 10 miles.

--- QUESTION 1384 ---
What component of the Earth's magnetic field provides the directive force for a magnetic compass?
--- ANSWER ---
The horizontal component (H).

--- QUESTION 1385 ---
Where on Earth is the compass directive force at its maximum, and where does it have little to no effect?
--- ANSWER ---
Maximum at the equator; little to no effect at high latitudes (magnetic poles).

--- QUESTION 1386 ---
What is retentive error, and when does it typically occur?
--- ANSWER ---
It is an error caused by sub-permanent magnetism acquired when a ship has been laid up or steering the same course for a prolonged period.

--- QUESTION 1387 ---
If a vessel alters course after steering a steady heading for days, how long should the compass error be checked frequently to monitor retentive error?
--- ANSWER ---
Over the next 6 hours.

--- QUESTION 1388 ---
What error is caused when a ship turns too fast, causing different parts of the structure to cut the Earth's magnetic field at varying rates and generating electromagnetic forces?
--- ANSWER ---
Gausing error

--- QUESTION 1389 ---
According to SOLAS Chapter V performance standards, what is the minimum frequency for recording a compass error under normal watchkeeping conditions?
--- ANSWER ---
At least once a watch.

--- QUESTION 1390 ---
Apart from once a watch, when else must a compass error be taken during a passage?
--- ANSWER ---
After every large alteration of course.

--- QUESTION 1391 ---
What company provides the remote magnetic compass adjustment service described in the text?
--- ANSWER ---
NAV-CONSULT (nav-consult.dk / nav-consult.eu)

--- QUESTION 1392 ---
What is the name and IMO number of the vessel addressed in the correspondence dated 2019-03-01?
--- ANSWER ---
mt: STI SYMPHONY (IMO NO: 9719692)

--- QUESTION 1393 ---
Which specific components inside the binnacle are designated as the B-correctors?
--- ANSWER ---
The for-and-aft magnets located in the starboard and port compartments or sides of the binnacle.

--- QUESTION 1394 ---
Which specific components inside the binnacle are designated as the C-correctors?
--- ANSWER ---
The port-starboard (athwartships) magnets located in the after part of the binnacle.

--- QUESTION 1395 ---
What color identifies the north end of the corrector magnets inside the binnacle?
--- ANSWER ---
Red

--- QUESTION 1396 ---
Where is the Flinder's Bar normally located on a magnetic compass binnacle?
--- ANSWER ---
On the forepart of the binnacle.

--- QUESTION 1397 ---
What physical components make up the D-correctors, and where are they located relative to the binnacle?
--- ANSWER ---
Balls, spheres, or plates in boxes placed on brackets abeam of the binnacle.

--- QUESTION 1398 ---
To what component is the Heeling-Magnet attached in the middle of the binnacle?
--- ANSWER ---
A chain

--- QUESTION 1399 ---
Which specific model does the compass adjuster use to obtain information regarding magnetic variation, inclination, and horizontal intensity?
--- ANSWER ---
World Magnetic Model 2016/2017

--- QUESTION 1400 ---
How can the bridge team verify if the magnetic compass binnacle index line is parallel to the fore-and-aft line of the vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
By placing a bearing dioptre on the magnetic compass and taking a bearing to the foremast to see if it matches 000 degrees or the compass heading lubber line.

--- QUESTION 1401 ---
If a small binnacle index error needs to be physically corrected, what mechanical steps must be taken?
--- ANSWER ---
Loosen the nuts in the base/bottom of the binnacle, turn the binnacle to correct the error, tighten the nuts, and seal it with silicone to prevent water entry.

--- QUESTION 1402 ---
When performing a full swing for deviation control, what is the standard interval between course notes?
--- ANSWER ---
20 degrees apart

--- QUESTION 1403 ---
If time is limited during a swing, what are the 8 primary compass headings on which deviation control should be performed?
--- ANSWER ---
North (N), Northeast (NE), East (E), Southeast (SE), South (S), Southwest (SW), West (W), and Northwest (NW).

--- QUESTION 1404 ---
Why must all magnetic compass readings be taken directly from the compass rather than a remote digital repeater on the bridge?
--- ANSWER ---
Because there is often a difference between the physical compass readings and the digital repeater, and electronic devices like handheld VHFs must be kept at a distance due to internal magnets.

--- QUESTION 1405 ---
Which mathematical coefficients are calculated by the adjuster using the vessel's observations?
--- ANSWER ---
Deviation Coefficients A, B, C, D, and E

--- QUESTION 1406 ---
To eliminate the semicircular deviation caused by "C" x cos k, which correctors must be adjusted up or down?
--- ANSWER ---
The port-starboard magnets (C-correctors).

--- QUESTION 1407 ---
To eliminate the semicircular deviation caused by "B" x sin k, which correctors must be adjusted up or down?
--- ANSWER ---
The for-and-aft magnets (B-correctors).

--- QUESTION 1408 ---
To eliminate the quadrant-circle deviation caused by "D" x sin 2k, how must the D-correctors be manipulated?
--- ANSWER ---
The balls/spheres/boxes must be moved closer to or away from the binnacle.

--- QUESTION 1409 ---
What specific data from the Earth's magnetic properties and the vessel's history are required to calculate changes to the Flinder's Bar?
--- ANSWER ---
Knowledge of the Earth's Magnetic Horizontal Force, the Magnetic Dip, and two historic vessel positions (latitude and longitude) with a large difference in magnetic latitude.

--- QUESTION 1410 ---
When must any necessary changes to the Flinder's Bar be completed during the adjustment process?
--- ANSWER ---
Before adjusting the fore-and-aft magnets at True Magnetic 090 degrees.

--- QUESTION 1411 ---
What formula represents the remnant deviation if the adjustments have been accurately performed?
--- ANSWER ---
"A" + "E" x cos 2k

--- QUESTION 1412 ---
In what digital file format does the adjuster email the final signed and stamped Deviation Card to the vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
PDF file

--- QUESTION 1413 ---
Why does the adjuster warn against using a GPS to determine the actual Magnetic Variation (MagVar)?
--- ANSWER ---
The software in the GPS equipment may not have been updated since delivery, which can result in an error of up to 4 degrees.

--- QUESTION 1414 ---
What source is recommended as more accurate for Magnetic Variation than an un-updated GPS?
--- ANSWER ---
The vessel's Navigation Charts (Nav-Charts) or the provided British Geological Survey (BGS) web calculator link.

--- QUESTION 1415 ---
According to the Merchant Shipping and Fishing Vessels (Health and Safety at Work) Regulations 1997, whose legal duty is it to protect the health and safety of seafarers and others on board so far as is reasonably practicable?
--- ANSWER ---
The shipowners and employers.

--- QUESTION 1416 ---
What specific UK publication has a legal requirement to be carried and readily accessible on board UK flagged merchant ships?
--- ANSWER ---
The Code of Safe Working Practices for Merchant Seafarers (COSWP).

--- QUESTION 1417 ---
The Master must ensure that the shipowner's health and safety policy and procedures are implemented on board. What additional action must the Master take regarding these policies for those working on board?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master must ensure that they are clearly communicated to all those working on board.

--- QUESTION 1418 ---
What are the seven core principles specified for ensuring health and safety on board a ship?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Avoidance of risks (replacing dangerous practices with less dangerous ones).
2. Evaluation of unavoidable risks and taking action to reduce them.
3. Adopting work patterns and procedures to reduce adverse effects on health and safety.
4. Adapting procedures to take account of new technology.
5. Adopting a coherent approach to vessel management at every level.
6. Giving collective protective measures priority over individual protective measures.
7. Providing appropriate and relevant information and instruction for workers.

--- QUESTION 1419 ---
What operational elements are shown in the Health and Safety Organisation diagram under the three main pillars of "Implementation", "Risk Assessment", and "Safety Awareness"?
--- ANSWER ---
Safety Officer, Safety Procedures, Tool Box Talks, Investigations, Committee, Captain, Permit to Work, Safety Representatives, Record Keeping, Familiarisation, and Training.

--- QUESTION 1420 ---
What is the definition of a risk assessment based on the provided text?
--- ANSWER ---
A careful examination of what could cause harm, so that decisions can be made as to whether enough precautions have been taken or whether more should be done to prevent that harm.

--- QUESTION 1421 ---
In the context of risk assessment, how is a hazard defined versus a risk?
--- ANSWER ---
A hazard is something with the potential to cause harm. A risk consists of two elements: the likelihood that a hazard may occur and the consequences of that hazardous event.

--- QUESTION 1422 ---
What are the four active steps involved in conducting a Risk Assessment?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Identification of Hazards.
2. Assessment of the risks involved (severity and likelihood).
3. Implementing control measures to reduce the risk.
4. Monitoring of the effectiveness of the controls.

--- QUESTION 1423 ---
What does the acronym ALARP stand for in shipboard safety management?
--- ANSWER ---
As Low As Reasonably Practical.

--- QUESTION 1424 ---
Is there an explicit legal requirement for a risk assessment to be written down on board?
--- ANSWER ---
No, there is no explicit requirement for it to be written down, but a written record assists the review process and provides evidence of compliance.

--- QUESTION 1425 ---
When must a shipboard risk assessment be reviewed?
--- ANSWER ---
Regularly and after any significant change in practices or circumstances.

--- QUESTION 1426 ---
What is the primary purpose of a Permit to Work system?
--- ANSWER ---
It is a standard operational procedure used to issue documented permission to perform tasks that are considered hazardous or non-routine.

--- QUESTION 1427 ---
Which shipboard system determines exactly which activities will require a permit to work?
--- ANSWER ---
The Safety Management System (SMS).

--- QUESTION 1428 ---
Name at least five categories of hazardous work that would normally require a Permit to Work system.
--- ANSWER ---
Any five from: Entry into enclosed space, Hot work, Working at height/over the side, Underwater work, Cold work, Working on deck during adverse weather, or Lifts, lift trunks and machinery.

--- QUESTION 1429 ---
What is the maximum period of validity that can be specified on a Permit to Work?
--- ANSWER ---
It should not exceed 24 hours.

--- QUESTION 1430 ---
What must the authorised officer ensure before signing off on a Permit to Work?
--- ANSWER ---
That all safety measures specified as necessary have in fact been taken, or that the correct procedures are actively in place.

--- QUESTION 1431 ---
Can the competent person responsible for performing the work also act as the authorised officer who issues the Permit to Work?
--- ANSWER ---
No, the competent person carrying out the specified work should not be the same person as the authorised officer.

--- QUESTION 1432 ---
Under the Work at Height Regulations 2010, what defines work carried out "at height"?
--- ANSWER ---
Any work carried out where a person could fall a distance liable to result in an injury to them.

--- QUESTION 1433 ---
Why does the "2 metre rule" no longer apply to maritime workers under the Work at Height Regulations 2010?
--- ANSWER ---
Because the regulations apply to all work where a person could fall and suffer an injury, irrespective of whether the work is being carried out at, above, or below 2 metres.

--- QUESTION 1434 ---
If a crew member discovers a Bosun working up a mast without a safety harness, what immediate intervention strategy should be applied?
--- ANSWER ---
Use Stop Work Authority to ask the worker to come down in a safe manner.

--- QUESTION 1435 ---
Under Regulation 16 of the health and safety regulations (Duties of other persons), what is the explicit duty of a worker regarding provided equipment?
--- ANSWER ---
To make full and proper use of any work equipment provided by the employer for their use.

--- QUESTION 1436 ---
Under Section 17, what is the maximum penalty for a general person convicted on indictment for failing to comply with certain regulations?
--- ANSWER ---
Imprisonment for a term not exceeding two years, a fine, or both.

--- QUESTION 1437 ---
What is the minimum number of seafarers that must be employed on a seagoing ship to legally require the Company to appoint a Safety Officer?
--- ANSWER ---
Five or more seafarers.

--- QUESTION 1438 ---
Where must the Master officially record the appointment of a shipboard Safety Officer?
--- ANSWER ---
In the official logbook.

--- QUESTION 1439 ---
Name three operational duties carried out by a designated shipboard Safety Officer.
--- ANSWER ---
Any three from: Ensuring safe work practices are followed; ensuring joining crew members receive adequate familiarization; developing safety consciousness; ensuring safety inspections are carried out; advising on risk assessments; investigating incidents; and maintaining accident records.

--- QUESTION 1440 ---
Where must the Master record the election of a Safety Representative on board?
--- ANSWER ---
Either in the Official Log Book or in the minutes of the Safety Committee meeting.

--- QUESTION 1441 ---
What are the minimum age and sea service criteria required for a crew member to be elected as a Safety Representative?
--- ANSWER ---
They must have at least 2 years of sea service since reaching the age of 18 years.

--- QUESTION 1442 ---
What additional service requirement applies to a crew member serving as a Safety Representative on board a tanker?
--- ANSWER ---
They must have at least six months of service on board a tanker within their two years of sea service.

--- QUESTION 1443 ---
Who must chair the mandatory shipboard Safety Committee?
--- ANSWER ---
The Master.

--- QUESTION 1444 ---
Apart from the chairperson, who constitutes the absolute minimum required membership of a shipboard Safety Committee?
--- ANSWER ---
The Safety Officer and the elected safety representatives.

--- QUESTION 1445 ---
Apart from regular scheduled intervals, when is it mandatory to hold an emergency Safety Committee meeting?
--- ANSWER ---
After any serious incident or accident on the ship.

--- QUESTION 1446 ---
What does the acronym MAIB stand for, and what is its official role?
--- ANSWER ---
Marine Accident Investigation Branch. It is an agency authorized to investigate all maritime accidents in UK waters and accidents involving UK registered ships worldwide.

--- QUESTION 1447 ---
Which specific Marine Guidance Note (MGN) details the reporting requirements for accidents and incidents to the MAIB?
--- ANSWER ---
MGN 564.

--- QUESTION 1448 ---
What distinguishes a "marine casualty" from a "marine incident" according to the MAIB criteria?
--- ANSWER ---
A marine casualty results in death, serious injury, loss of a person, loss/abandonment of a ship, material damage, stranding, disabling, or pollution. A marine incident does not meet the casualty criteria but still endangers or could endanger the safety of the ship, occupants, or environment.

--- QUESTION 1449 ---
Give three examples of events classified specifically as a "marine incident" under MAIB guidance.
--- ANSWER ---
Any three from: Close-quarters situations requiring urgent collision-avoidance action; near-miss events with serious injury potential; fires without material damage; unintended temporary groundings on soft mud; a person overboard recovered without serious injury; or snagging of fishing gear causing a dangerous heel.

--- QUESTION 1450 ---
What is the purpose of an Improvement Notice issued under the Merchant Shipping Act 1995?
--- ANSWER ---
To ensure statutory health, safety, and welfare standards are complied with by forcing a remedy to a contravention within a specified time limit, aiming to prevent accidents rather than punish through prosecution.

--- QUESTION 1451 ---
What is the minimum statutory time period allowed for compliance when an inspector issues an Improvement Notice?
--- ANSWER ---
Not earlier than 21 days from the date of service of the notice.

--- QUESTION 1452 ---
Under what exact circumstances can an inspector serve a Prohibition Notice on a vessel?
--- ANSWER ---
When the inspector is of the opinion that an activity as carried out, or likely to be carried out, will involve the risk of serious personal injury or a serious pollution incident.

--- QUESTION 1453 ---
What four pillars define the practical application of effective "Knowledge Management" in shipboard operations?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Getting the right information.
2. Making it easy to understand.
3. Getting it to the people who need it, when they need it.
4. Encouraging them to use it.

--- QUESTION 1454 ---
What is the core philosophy behind a shipboard "Just Culture"?
--- ANSWER ---
It recognizes behaviors that exceed Company expectations as well as those that fall below expectation, while acknowledging that sub-standard behaviors are not always entirely the fault of the individual seafarer.

--- QUESTION 1455 ---
According to the Just Culture decision tree, what three severe consequences can result from deliberate sabotage, malicious acts, or reckless violations?
--- ANSWER ---
Dismissal, a human factor analysis, and an antecedents, behaviors, and consequence analysis.

--- QUESTION 1456 ---
List five of the ten core leadership qualities highlighted in COSWP Chapter 1.
--- ANSWER ---
Any five from: Instil respect and command authority; Lead the team by example; Draw on knowledge and experience; Remain calm in a crisis; Practise "tough empathy"; Be sensitive to different cultures; Recognise seafarers' limitations; Motivate a sense of community; Place the safety of crew and passengers above everything; Communicate clearly.

--- QUESTION 1457 ---
According to the COSWP 2025 "How to use this document" guide, what does the book symbol represent?
--- ANSWER ---
The book symbol indicates further reading, which could be the legislation on which the guidance is based or another publication on the same subject.

--- QUESTION 1458 ---
According to the COSWP 2025 guide, what information is specifically provided in the sidebars containing a flowchart symbol?
--- ANSWER ---
The flowchart symbol identifies steps in procedures.

--- QUESTION 1459 ---
What is the purpose of the magnifying glass symbol used in the COSWP 2025 text?
--- ANSWER ---
It means the word or phrase is explained in the glossary.

--- QUESTION 1460 ---
In the COSWP 2025 text layout, what type of information is highlighted inside black boxes?
--- ANSWER ---
Critical safety information.

--- QUESTION 1461 ---
What content immediately follows the key points summary at the beginning of each COSWP chapter?
--- ANSWER ---
A list of the organisation's responsibilities in a given subject.

--- QUESTION 1462 ---
According to COSWP 2025 section 5.2, what specific safety rule is given regarding electrical circuits and their rated capacity?
--- ANSWER ---
Never exceed the rated capacity of an electrical circuit.

--- QUESTION 1463 ---
Under COSWP 2025 section 5.2, if it is impracticable to provide permanent connections for electrical equipment, what safety step must be taken after closing and locking the circuit breaker in the off position?
--- ANSWER ---
Make the trip features inoperative for the period of the work, and apply a warning notice at the point of work.

--- QUESTION 1464 ---
What core process should underpin all safety measures put in place by a shipowner?
--- ANSWER ---
Risk assessment.

--- QUESTION 1465 ---
What policy must a shipowner develop and implement onboard a ship?
--- ANSWER ---
A Health and Safety policy.

--- QUESTION 1466 ---
What support must shipowners ensure masters have regarding health and safety?
--- ANSWER ---
Adequate support to carry out their responsibility for health and safety management effectively while on board.

--- QUESTION 1467 ---
What training must all seafarers receive to ensure they can work safely on board?
--- ANSWER ---
Familiarization with on-board equipment and procedures, including emergency procedures.

--- QUESTION 1468 ---
At what cost to workers should health and safety measures be provided by the shipowner?
--- ANSWER ---
Health and safety measures must be provided free of charge to workers.

--- QUESTION 1469 ---
When are employers required to provide health surveillance to workers?
--- ANSWER ---
Where a risk to their health is identified as a result of their work activities.

--- QUESTION 1470 ---
According to the Just Culture Decision Tree for behavior that meets or exceeds expectations, what three administrative actions are recommended at the bottom of the tree?
--- ANSWER ---
Encourage, Recognise, and Award.

--- QUESTION 1471 ---
In the context of the Just Culture Decision Tree, what three components of behavior fall under "Exemplary behaviour"?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Excellent planning and risk management.
2. Effective Sharing of Lessons Learned.
3. Creating a safer work environment through intervention.

--- QUESTION 1472 ---
According to the Just Culture Decision Tree, what test is used to evaluate an unsafe act by asking: "Would someone else of equal experience, knowledge and competence do the task the same?"
--- ANSWER ---
The substitution test.

--- QUESTION 1473 ---
What two analytical assessments must be conducted when behavior falls below expectations according to the Just Culture framework?
--- ANSWER ---
A human factor analysis, and an antecedents, behaviours and consequence analysis.

--- QUESTION 1474 ---
Name the five categories of behavioral outcomes identified on the Just Culture Decision Tree when behavior falls below expectations.
--- ANSWER ---
Sabotage/malicious act, Reckless violation, System induced violation, Negligent error, and System produced error.

--- QUESTION 1475 ---
What type of error or violation is identified if actions and results were unintended, but training, selection, or experience were found to be defective?
--- ANSWER ---
A system produced error (requiring training/re-training).

--- QUESTION 1476 ---
What are the four management disciplinary responses listed at the bottom of the Just Culture Decision Tree for behavior falling below expectations?
--- ANSWER ---
Coaching, Verbal Warning, Written warning (initial, subsequent, or final), and Dismissal.

--- QUESTION 1477 ---
According to MGN 560, what organizations are permitted to authorize a service provider to conduct the 5-yearly servicing and maintenance of lifeboats?
--- ANSWER ---
Only organizations authorized by either the Original Equipment Manufacturer (OEM) or a UK authorized Recognised Organisation (RO) in accordance with MGN 555 (M).

--- QUESTION 1478 ---
Prior to a 5-year load test on a lifeboat launch/recovery system, what three main areas must the service provider's risk assessment cover?
--- ANSWER ---

1. Dangers to personnel within the vicinity of the lifeboat while the test is carried out.
2. Possible free surface movement of water in the water-weight bags.
3. Possible failure of the equipment during the test and its effect on the equipment and personnel carrying out the test.

--- QUESTION 1479 ---
Who is the designated Officer in Charge (OIC) responsible for the safety of the 5-year dynamic testing operation?
--- ANSWER ---
The Chief Officer (C/O).

--- QUESTION 1480 ---
What pre-testing check must be performed regarding the ship's stability before loading a lifeboat to its proof load?
--- ANSWER ---
Ensure there are no free surface effects which may cause a rise in the vessel's center of gravity when loading the boat.

--- QUESTION 1481 ---
Why must special attention be given to the ship's risk assessment if a lifeboat is equipped with gravity pivoting davits?
--- ANSWER ---
To consider the loading of the lifeboat to the proof load and the possibility of pivoting.

--- QUESTION 1482 ---
To what specific total weight must a lifeboat be loaded during a 5-year static or dynamic load test?
--- ANSWER ---
1.1 times the total mass (weight) of the boat when fully loaded with its full complement of persons, equipment, and stores.

--- QUESTION 1483 ---
At what positions can the lifeboat be located when weights are distributed around it for the system test?
--- ANSWER ---
At the embarkation position, which is either the stowed position or alongside the ship and bowsed in.

--- QUESTION 1484 ---
How is the weight of the water bags monitored during the loading process, and what happens automatically when they reach their design weight?
--- ANSWER ---
A load cell monitors the weight, and a relief valve opens automatically once the water bags achieve their design weight.

--- QUESTION 1485 ---
What role does the attending surveyor play once the lifeboat is loaded and the test procedure is agreed upon?
--- ANSWER ---
The surveyor will only observe from that point forward.

--- QUESTION 1486 ---
During a winch brake test, how is the brake applied to test its performance?
--- ANSWER ---
The brake is abruptly (suddenly) applied when the proof load has reached its maximum lowering speed, holding the boat 1 meter above the water.

--- QUESTION 1487 ---
According to LSA Code regulations for launching appliances, a winch brake must withstand a static test with a proof load of not less than how many times the maximum working load?
--- ANSWER ---
Not less than 1.5 times the maximum working load.

--- QUESTION 1488 ---
According to LSA Code regulations, a winch brake must withstand a dynamic test with a proof load of not less than how many times the maximum working load?
--- ANSWER ---
Not less than 1.1 times the maximum working load.

--- QUESTION 1489 ---
What is the safety requirement regarding winch hand gear handles or wheels when a survival craft is being lowered or hoisted by power?
--- ANSWER ---
Hand gear handles or wheels shall not be rotated by moving parts of the winch.

--- QUESTION 1490 ---
What safety devices must be fitted if davit arms are recovered by power?
--- ANSWER ---
Safety devices that automatically cut off the power before the davit arms reach the stops to prevent overstressing the falls or davits (unless the motor is inherently designed to prevent such overstressing).

--- QUESTION 1491 ---
What is the LSA Code formula used to calculate the minimum lowering speed (S) in meters per second for a fully loaded survival craft?
--- ANSWER ---
S = 0.4 + 0.02H (where H is the height in meters from the davit head to the waterline with the ship at its lightest sea-going condition).

--- QUESTION 1492 ---
What method of load simulation can be used to perform an On-Load Release test without putting actual weight on the falls?
--- ANSWER ---
Mechanical or hydraulic load simulation provided by the Original Equipment Manufacturer (OEM).

--- QUESTION 1493 ---
If mechanical/hydraulic simulation is unavailable, how can an On-Load Release test be alternatively conducted by a minimum operating crew?
--- ANSWER ---
The crew enters the boat from another tender to override the release mechanism while wearing full PPE (safety helmets and life jackets) and remaining seated when the boat is released.

--- QUESTION 1494 ---
What conditions must be met to safely conduct an Off-Load Release test?
--- ANSWER ---
The boat must be fully waterborne before operating the off-load release gear.

--- QUESTION 1495 ---
During the recovery phase of a lifeboat test, what specific check must a competent person perform after the boat is raised just clear of the water?
--- ANSWER ---
Double check the hook and interlock mechanism.

--- QUESTION 1496 ---
Can a lifeboat be raised to its stowed and secured position with personnel inside after a load test?
--- ANSWER ---
No, the boat must be raised with no personnel inside.

--- QUESTION 1497 ---
Who must sign the final test certificate to validate the results of the lifeboat and winch testing?
--- ANSWER ---
The witnessing MCA (Maritime and Coastguard Agency) surveyor.

--- QUESTION 1498 ---
Under what condition is a lifeboat hook release tested during a 5-year static load test?
--- ANSWER ---
Under a static load using a hydraulic ram.

--- QUESTION 1499 ---
How is the Safe Working Load (SWL) of an item of lifting equipment calculated?
--- ANSWER ---
The Safe Working Load is calculated by dividing the equipment's minimum breaking strength/load (MBL) by a safety factor (SF).

--- QUESTION 1500 ---
What is the general safety factor used when calculating the Safe Working Load for lifting cargo or stores?
--- ANSWER ---
A safety factor of 6 is generally used for cargo and stores.

--- QUESTION 1501 ---
What safety factor is utilized when calculating the Safe Working Load for equipment used to lift persons?
--- ANSWER ---
A safety factor of 10 is used for lifting persons.

--- QUESTION 1502 ---
What safety factor is utilized when calculating the Safe Working Load for lifeboats or fast rescue boats (FRB)?
--- ANSWER ---
A safety factor of 2.2 is used for lifeboats and fast rescue boats.

--- QUESTION 1503 ---
What immediate action must be taken if a lifting appliance or piece of equipment is suspected of being subjected to an excessive load exceeding its SWL?
--- ANSWER ---
It should be immediately taken out of service until it can be subjected to a thorough examination by a competent person.

--- QUESTION 1504 ---
How is a "competent person" defined within the context of lifting regulations?
--- ANSWER ---
A competent person is defined as a person possessing the knowledge or experience necessary to enable them to satisfactorily perform the required duties.

--- QUESTION 1505 ---
What kind of indicator must be fitted to lifting equipment carried on a ship if its safe working load varies with its operating radius?
--- ANSWER ---
It must be fitted with an accurate indicator that is clearly visible to the operator, showing the radius of the load lifting attachment at any time and the corresponding safe working load for that radius.

--- QUESTION 1506 ---
What information must be kept in the records and service history of lifting equipment?
--- ANSWER ---
The records must include the dates when and where the equipment was brought into use, its safe working load, and details of any repairs, modifications, tests, and examinations carried out.

--- QUESTION 1507 ---
What is the difference between "loose gear" and "lifting equipment" under maritime regulations?
--- ANSWER ---
"Loose gear" means any gear by means of which a load can be attached to lifting equipment but does not form an integral part of either the lifting equipment or the load. "Lifting equipment" means work equipment used for lifting or lowering loads and includes its attachments used for anchoring, fixing, or supporting it.

--- QUESTION 1508 ---
Under what conditions can standard lifting equipment be used to lift persons if it was not specifically designed for that purpose?
--- ANSWER ---
It can only be used in exceptional circumstances provided that the employer ensures the control position of the lifting equipment is manned at all times and the persons being lifted have a reliable means of communication with the operator.

--- QUESTION 1509 ---
According to the MCA, are appliances such as Fast Rescue Craft and ship's lifeboats considered lifting equipment for lifting persons under LOLER regulations?
--- ANSWER ---
No, the MCA views appliances like Fast Rescue Craft and ship's lifeboats, which are raised and lowered with personnel on board, as not being lifting equipment for lifting persons for the purposes of LOLER.

--- QUESTION 1510 ---
Which specific circular and SOLAS regulation govern the testing and maintenance of gangways and accommodation ladders?
--- ANSWER ---
MSC.1/Circ.1331 and SOLAS regulation III/20.7.2.

--- QUESTION 1511 ---
What is the minimum ship length threshold that mandates the carriage of a gangway versus an accommodation ladder?
--- ANSWER ---
A gangway should be carried on ships of 30 meters in length or over, whereas an accommodation ladder must be carried on ships of 120 meters in length or over.

--- QUESTION 1512 ---
What are the maximum allowable angles from the horizontal for the safe use of a gangway and an accommodation ladder?
--- ANSWER ---
A gangway should not be used at an angle of more than 30 degrees from the horizontal, while an accommodation ladder should not be used at an angle of more than 55 degrees from the horizontal.

--- QUESTION 1513 ---
What are the structural and structural orientation differences between a rigged gangway and an accommodation ladder?
--- ANSWER ---
A gangway is a portable structure rigged at right angles to the fore and aft line of the ship. An accommodation ladder is a fixed structure rigged in the fore and aft direction of the ship, must always lead aft, and features a horizontal bottom platform and self-levelling intermediate platforms.

--- QUESTION 1514 ---
How often must an accommodation ladder undergo operational testing with its specified maximum operational load?
--- ANSWER ---
Operational load testing must be carried out at every five-yearly survey.

--- QUESTION 1515 ---
At what angle from the horizontal should a portable ladder be positioned for correct use?
--- ANSWER ---
It should be positioned at an angle of 75 degrees from the horizontal (maintaining a 4:1 height-to-horizontal distance ratio).

--- QUESTION 1516 ---
To ensure safety, how far must a portable ladder extend above the upper landing place if alternate suitable handholds are unavailable?
--- ANSWER ---
The ladder must extend at least 1 meter above the upper landing place.

--- QUESTION 1517 ---
What is the minimum required clearance space behind the rungs of a correctly positioned portable ladder?
--- ANSWER ---
There must be a clearance of at least 150 mm behind the rungs.

--- QUESTION 1518 ---
When rigging or inspecting boarding equipment, what specific structural components must be checked looking for signs of distortion, cracks, or corrosion?
--- ANSWER ---
The welding connections must be checked in particular.

--- QUESTION 1519 ---
